The one thing shaping every game on this card

Twenty fixtures, one round in each of the five biggest European leagues, and the structural fact running through the whole card is a change of head coach at more than half the clubs on it. Twenty-three of the forty clubs below have appointed a new head coach since 1 June 2026, and the appointments form a single chain rather than twenty-three separate decisions. Milan dismissed Massimiliano Allegri, who is now at Napoli; Milan replaced him with Rúben Amorim, who had left Manchester United, where Michael Carrick was made permanent. Liverpool dismissed Arne Slot and took Andoni Iraola from Bournemouth, who appointed Marco Rose. Real Madrid dismissed Álvaro Arbeloa on 9 June and appointed José Mourinho on 11 June; Arbeloa was at Fulham by 7 July. Bruno Génésio left Lille for Marseille and Lille appointed Davide Ancelotti. Every one of those clubs plays today. Fiorentina have had two head coaches in this four-match season alone, sacking Fabio Grosso on 6 September and reappointing Paolo Vanoli the next day, and Nice are on their third in twelve months after surviving a relegation play-off.

The transfer market followed the same closed circuit. More than a dozen moves on this card run between two clubs that both play today: Højlund from Manchester United to Napoli, Gonçalo Ramos from PSG to Milan, Kang-in Lee from PSG to Atlético, Miguel Gutiérrez from Napoli to Leverkusen, Grimaldo from Leverkusen to Atlético, James Trafford from Manchester City to Leeds, Harry Wilson from Fulham to Leeds, and four separate Marseille sales — Rulli to Manchester City, Medina to Leverkusen, Hamed Traoré to Bournemouth, Aubameyang to Deportivo. Ibrahima Konaté and Bernardo Silva were released by Liverpool and Manchester City, and both signed for Real Madrid. Three fixtures contain a player who moved between the two clubs in it — Berrocal from Getafe to Málaga, Etta Eyong from Villarreal to Levante, Scaglione from Genoa to Parma — and one fixture, Paderborn against Hoffenheim, contains three players on loan from the away club to the home one.

That matters for one reason, and it is about evidence rather than quality. Nearly every 2025–26 rate on this board was produced by someone who is no longer picking the team, at a club whose spine has since been sold. Against that, the current sample is four league matches in the Premier League, Serie A and Ligue 1, three in the Bundesliga and five to seven in LaLiga — and the divisional baselines have moved further than most clubs have. Four of the five leagues are scoring above their own completed rate (Bundesliga 3.97 against 3.24, Serie A 2.96 against 2.43, LaLiga 3.05 against 2.69, Premier League 2.83 against 2.75) while Ligue 1 sits exactly level, and three of the five are booking materially less than last season. Those new baselines rest on 33 to 64 matches each.

Structural consequence. On this card the two numbers a family is normally read from — the club’s own rate and the divisional baseline — have both moved, and on most fixtures neither has moved for a settled reason. That is why 1X2 is rated Dangerous on all twenty cards without a single exception, and the Asian handicap on eighteen of twenty: a result needs a settled team, and more than half the teams here are three or four matches into a new one. Where a card rates a family well-suited, it is because a counted rate, a mechanism in the shot, pressing or possession data, and a transfer ledger all point the same way. That test is passed most often by Totals, rated well-suited on twelve of the twenty and Dangerous on none — because a goal rate that survives a change of coach is evidence of something the result record cannot supply.

Provenance — evidence, not images. No odds of any kind entered this pipeline or left it: the source sheet’s columns were read for league, teams and kickoff only. Every rate, result, corner, card, foul, shot, venue and referee figure on this board was counted, not searched — from 2,915 ESPN match summaries (1,751 completed 2025–26 league matches, 230 of 2026–27, 604 cup and European matches, plus 1,164 second-tier matches for the promoted clubs), with Understat as the independent xG source. The goal-timing parser was reconciled against the official final score on every one of those matches, 1,751 of 1,751 and 230 of 230; see baselines_0920.md for the method and the validation. Unpublished across the slate: no 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion series exists for any promoted club, because Understat covers no second tier — so Málaga, Frosinone, Deportivo, Schalke, Elversberg and Paderborn have no modelled history at all, and on f13 both clubs are in that position; nothing is estimated in its place. No source reached characterises in words how any appointed official reads contact in the box, though every penalty rate quoted is counted — and on f5 no referee record of any kind exists, because Adam Herczeg is making his Premier League debut. Six clubs’ transfer ledgers could not be obtained at all — Real Betis, Marseille’s incoming window, Brest, Nice, and every RB Leipzig, Levante, Atalanta and Genoa fee — and no injury or suspension information whatsoever could be found for Schalke, Elversberg, Auxerre, Brest, Nice, Lille, Juventus, Atalanta, Paderborn, Hoffenheim or Real Sociedad. Paderborn’s head coach and Crystal Palace’s could not be identified and are not named. Last season standing in for a thin sample: the Premier League, Serie A, Bundesliga and Ligue 1 fixtures here have three or four league matches each, below the five-match line this board uses, so every 26/27 figure in those facts strips is printed with its 25/26 counterpart beside it and the match count in brackets. Four contradictions were logged on the first build and all four are now resolved, each against one of this board’s own cards. Enes Ünal did not leave Getafe for Beşiktaş: he joined Getafe from Bournemouth on a free on 13 August 2026, and card f2’s transfer bullet has been rewritten around it. Piero Hincapié did not join Leeds: Arsenal made his Leverkusen loan permanent on 1 July for a reported £34.5m, and he is removed from card f5. Timothy Weah did not leave Marseille for Juventus: he moved the other way and is a permanent Marseille player, so card f18’s departure total falls from €126.9m to €112.5m and he becomes its one confirmed arrival. Ayyoub Bouaddi’s fee is £86m — £81.3m fixed plus £4.3m — not the €130m the French press reported. One contradiction remains genuinely open: Parma’s Adrián Bernabé is listed as both out and expected to start, and both readings are printed on card f8. A systematic audit of every board-wide superlative was also run against the counted data, and roughly thirty claims were corrected — most were true when written against a part-built board and became false as the remaining cards were added. Each is now stated at its true rank or softened to a range. One archive error corrected: this board’s card of 5 September named Ole Werner as Leverkusen’s departing coach; the Bundesliga’s own announcement confirms Carles Martínez Novell replaced Kasper Hjulmand, and Werner was Leipzig’s. Two strings in this page’s furniture — the masthead line and the slate heading — are inert leftovers from the frozen shell and carry no data.
Completion. All 20 of 20 fixtures are carded, and all 20 at full RESEARCH SPEC depth with sections A–E. No placeholder and no reduced-depth card appears on this board. That is the result of two instructions from you rather than of triage: the five fixtures scored T2 — f4, f13, f16, f18 and f19 — were researched at full T1 depth, and the seven scored T3 — f3, f5, f8, f11, f14, f17 and f20 — were raised to T1* and researched in full afterwards. The scored tier stays visible beside each override in progress_0920.md, and the triage reasons still stand: f3, f11 and f17 were separated out because a side had only four competitive matches, f5, f14 and f20 because a club had been carded in full within the last three slates, and f8 because it scored zero for a structural separator. Those reasons describe how much evidence each card could draw on, which is now a property of the card rather than of the selection — and where the evidence is thin, each card says so in its own words. A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Fiorentina finished 15th in 2025–26 on 42 points — 9 wins, 15 draws, 14 defeats, 41 scored, 50 conceded, a season spent close enough to the relegation places that the club changed coach twice inside it. Napoli finished 2nd on 76 points — 23 wins, 7 draws, 8 defeats, 58 scored, 36 conceded, the best defensive record in the division and the second-fewest cards of any side in it. The structural headline is that both clubs responded to those two very different seasons by replacing the head coach, and that one of them then spent more money in a single window than it ever has while the other spent its summer converting loans into permanent deals and selling a left-back for €26m. Fiorentina committed roughly €150m in fees and loan fees against about €5m of published income, and sit 16th on 3 points with the worst goal difference in Serie A. Napoli committed roughly €87m against roughly €50m and sit 10th on 6. The 2025–26 records on this card belong, on both sides, to coaches who are no longer in the dugout — and on Fiorentina’s side, to two of them.

  • Fiorentina — turnover very high in money and across every position the spec asks about; two head coaches this season, the second appointed 7 September 2026. Fabio Grosso was appointed in June 2026 and dismissed on 6 September after three straight defeats left the club bottom with one goal scored and nine conceded; Paolo Vanoli took over on 7 September, his second spell — he had replaced Stefano Pioli in November 2025 after a winless ten-match start and kept the club up. Centre-forward: in — Mateo Pellegrino from Parma, €25m plus €5m in add-ons, and Beto from Everton, €18m; out — M’Bala Nzola free to Cagliari. Centre-back: in — Viery from Grêmio, €15m plus €2m, Víctor Valdepeñas from Real Madrid, €8m, and Radu Drăgușin on loan from Tottenham, €1.5m loan fee. Defensive midfield: in — Christ Inao Oulaï from Trabzonspor, €26m plus €4m, and Marco Brescianini from Atalanta, €10m (loan made permanent); out — Rolando Mandragora to Torino, €5m. Midfield and attack, the rest: Arthur Atta from Udinese, €25m plus €5m; Giovanni Fabbian from Bologna, €13m; and five wide loans — Franco Mastantuono from Real Madrid (€3.5m fee), Pedro Gonçalves from Sporting (€2.5m), Wilfried Gnonto from Leeds (€1m), João Mário from Juventus (€1.8m), Alieu Njie from Torino and Álex Jiménez from Bournemouth free. No goalkeeper was signed. Eight permanent arrivals, seven loans, three departures: the squad was rebuilt in one window and has since been handed to a different coach from the one it was bought for.
  • Napoli — turnover high and concentrated on the spine, all of it with published fees; manager changed 3 July 2026. Antonio Conte confirmed his departure at the end of May 2026 and Massimiliano Allegri was appointed on 3 July on a three-year contract to June 2029, having been dismissed by AC Milan — the same chain of moves that put Rúben Amorim at Milan, who play later on this slate. Goalkeeper: in — Vanja Milinković-Savić from Torino, €21m (loan converted). Centre-forward: in — Rasmus Højlund from Manchester United, €44m (loan converted), the largest fee in this fixture; out — Romelu Lukaku to Fenerbahçe, €6m, Giovanni Simeone to Torino, €6m, and Walid Cheddira to Avellino, €2.5m. Centre-back and defence: in — Benoît Badiashile on loan from Chelsea, €4m with an option, and Costantino Favasuli on loan from Catanzaro, €1m with an obligation; out — Miguel Gutiérrez to Bayer Leverkusen, €26m (to a club also on this slate), Alessandro Zanoli to Udinese €5m, and Juan Jesus free at contract end. Other: in — Alisson Santos from Sporting €16.5m, Mattia Esposito from Sorrento €700,000; out — Luis Hasa €2.5m and Alessio Zerbin €2m, both to Frosinone, who also play on this slate. No defensive midfielder was bought: the Lobotka–Anguissa–McTominay unit was kept and is now the part of the squad the injury list has taken apart.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Napoli are missing seven, and the list runs through the exact positions the window did not reinforce: Alex Meret (goalkeeper), Alessandro Buongiorno (centre-back), Luca Marianucci (centre-back), Scott McTominay (central midfield), Leonardo Spinazzola (left-back, mild right-hamstring strain confirmed this week), Alisson Santos (a €16.5m summer signing) and Giovane. André-Frank Zambo Anguissa has recovered from the thigh problem that kept him out of the Arsenal match and is in the squad. That McTominay and Buongiorno are both out matters more than the count: Napoli bought neither a defensive midfielder nor a centre-back this summer, so both absences are covered from inside the squad. Fiorentina are missing Fabiano Parisi (left-back, injured) and no one else is reported out.
  • Sample. Fiorentina: six competitive matches — Serie A 0–4 at Roma (24 August), 0–3 v Frosinone (29 August), 1–2 v Torino (5 September), 4–2 at Venezia (11 September); Coppa Italia 4–1 v Benevento (14 August) and 3–0 v Pisa (15 September), both at the Franchi. Sixteenth on 3 points, 5 scored and 11 conceded, and every one of the four league matches has gone over 2.5. The four-match league record splits cleanly in two: three defeats under Grosso with one goal scored and nine conceded, then four scored in Vanoli’s first match. Napoli: five competitive matches — Serie A 2–0 at Genoa (22 August), 1–2 v Como (30 August), 2–3 at Inter (5 September), 1–0 v Bologna (13 September); Champions League 0–1 v Arsenal at the Maradona (9 September). Tenth on 6 points, 6 scored and 5 conceded. Neither club has five league matches, so every 26/27 rate in the strip above is a four-match rate and the 25/26 figure is printed beside it.
  • Referee. Daniele Doveri of the Roma 1 section, confirmed in the AIA designations published on 16 September, with Gariglio on VAR. He is the opposite of an unknown: 19 Serie A matches in 2025–26, 3.37 cards per game (63 yellows, 1 red), 23.7 fouls per match, 7.0 fouls per card and 0.16 penalties per match, all counted from the ESPN match record. Against the division he is a lenient official on both axes — 3.37 cards against a 2025–26 league rate of 3.77, 7.0 fouls per card against 6.72, and 0.16 penalties against 0.28, roughly half the divisional penalty rate. This season he has taken one match, Atalanta 1–0 Bologna on 31 August, which produced a single card in 23 fouls; one match is not a record and is reported only so the 2025–26 figures are not mistaken for current ones. History with both clubs, and a lot of it: he took two Fiorentina matches last season (1–1 v Juventus in November, six cards; 1–2 at Bologna in January, three) and three Napoli matches (2–2 at Inter in January, two cards and a penalty; 1–0 v Milan in April; 4–0 v Cremonese in April, no cards at all and a penalty). Across those five he averaged 2.2 cards a match, below even his own rate.
  • Not sourced. The fee for Antonín Barák’s move to APOEL is undisclosed and is not estimated. Positions are not published for two of Napoli’s smaller outgoings. No source reached describes in words how Doveri reads contact in the box — his 0.16 penalties per match is counted, but the characterisation the spec asks for is not published and is not invented. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division: Serie A carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data per match, the official for every fixture, and complete transfer fees.

B · Style & early-season reality

Fiorentina have gone from a possession side to a transition side, and the numbers say it happened before the coach changed rather than because of it. Possession has fallen from 51.0% to 43.8% and deep completions have risen from 5.03 to 9.25 per match — they have the ball less and get it into the final third more often when they do, which is the shape of a team playing faster and straighter. Shots are up from 12.87 to 16.75 per match and shots on target from 3.45 to 6.00, and the xG line agrees: 1.65 for against 1.37 last season. The problem is entirely at the other end. xG conceded has gone from 1.51 to 1.88 and actual goals conceded from 1.32 to 2.75 per match — the worst defensive record in the division on both the counted and the modelled figure, from a back line rebuilt around Viery, Valdepeñas and a loaned Drăgușin in front of an unchanged goalkeeping department. PPDA is essentially static at 12.80 against 12.49, so the press has not changed; the defence has. Vanoli’s one match produced four goals scored and two conceded, which is the same pattern at higher volume and is a single match.

Napoli are the more conservative side of the two on every measure and have become more so. Possession has dropped sharply, from 58.9% to 47.8%, while PPDA has risen from 11.87 to 13.45 — less ball and a looser press, the clearest system signature on this card. Allegri’s Napoli are nonetheless creating more than Conte’s did in raw terms: deep completions have almost doubled, 6.92 to 12.00, and xG is up from 1.52 to 1.69, with shots up from 13.21 to 14.25. What has gone is the defensive floor that defined last season: 1.10 xG conceded and 0.95 goals conceded per match in 2025–26 have become 1.39 and 1.25, and the clean-sheet rate has held at 50% over four matches only because two of those four were 2–0 and 1–0. The corner column is the single most changed line on their card and it runs the wrong way: 5.47 won against 2.76 conceded last season, against 5.50 and 5.50 now — the same attacking volume, double the concession. The evidence that Allegri has changed the shape is eleven points of possession, 1.6 points of PPDA and four league matches; the evidence about what it produces is four league matches and nothing else, with the two players who anchored the old midfield and the old back line unavailable for this one.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
The two full-season records that would settle a result — 15th on 42 against 2nd on 76 — belong to three coaches who are all gone. Fiorentina have had two head coaches in this four-match season and the current one has taken exactly one match; Napoli have a coach appointed on 3 July and a squad missing its goalkeeper, a centre-back and a central midfielder in the positions the window did not reinforce. Serie A’s home-win rate this season is 40.0% against 38.9% last, so the venue supplies no separation either. Four league matches each, under coaches with one and four matches respectively, is not a base a result can be read off.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
A handicap needs a readable margin and Fiorentina’s four are −4, −3, −1, +2 — two routs, a narrow loss and a win by two, with no cluster anywhere. Napoli’s are +2, −1, −1, +1, which is tighter but sits on a 1.25 goals-conceded rate that is a third worse than last season’s. The coach change on the home side falls between the routs and the win, so the sample is not one distribution but two, of three matches and one. Serie A’s draw rate has collapsed to 17.8% from 26.8%, which widens margins generally and does nothing to make these two readable.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — the best-evidenced family here, with one sample caveat
This is the one line where the counted rate, the modelled rate and the divisional baseline all point the same way. All four of Fiorentina’s league matches have gone over 2.5, at 4.00 goals per match in games they have played, and their xG line of 1.65 for and 1.88 against agrees with it rather than contradicting it. The division is running at 2.96 goals per game against 2.43 last season and 55.6% over 2.5 against 45.8%, so the baseline moves the same direction. Against that: Napoli’s own four matches are 50% over and their 25/26 record was built on the division’s meanest defence, and four matches is below the five-match line this board treats as a sample. Well evidenced on one side, thin on the other, and the two do not conflict.
BTTSOK — asymmetric
Both sides read 50.0% over four matches, which is uninformative on its own; what sits underneath is not. Fiorentina have not kept a clean sheet in the league this season (0.0% against 26.3% last) and have failed to score in half their matches (50.0%) — two opposite-facing facts that both suppress the rate. Napoli are the mirror: 50% clean sheets, 0% failed to score. The divisional rate is 60.0% against 45.3% last season, the largest BTTS move of the five leagues on this slate. The structural reason to treat this as readable is that the one thing both sides agree on is that Fiorentina concede — 2.75 per match, 1.88 xG against, the worst in the division on both.
Team goalsSplit — Fiorentina-side readable, Napoli-side not
Fiorentina’s attacking side has a real evidence base for once: 16.75 shots and 6.00 on target per match against 12.87 and 3.45 last season, 9.25 deep completions against 5.03, and 1.65 xG against 1.37 — four matches, but four matches where three separate counted series move together. Napoli’s side is the problem. Højlund at €44m is the centre-forward and has two goals; behind him McTominay, Anguissa’s fitness and Spinazzola’s absence have broken the supply line that produced 1.53 goals per match last season, and the 26/27 figure is 1.50 on four matches under a coach whose possession share has dropped eleven points. One side’s volume is evidenced, the other’s is a €44m signing and a treatment room.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Dangerous
Both sides are well below the divisional first-half rate and for reasons that do not obviously persist. Fiorentina 0.50 scored and 1.00 conceded before the break; Napoli 0.25 and 0.50 — a combined 2.25 first-half goals per match between them against a divisional 1.16 per match, and Napoli’s 0.25 is the lowest attacking first-half figure on this card against 0.82 last season. A 12:30 kickoff, a home side on its second coach in a fortnight and an away side whose first-half scoring has fallen by two-thirds gives four data points per team and no mechanism. The first-half result market compounds the 1X2 problem on a shorter sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous — every input points low, which is itself the risk
Every number here is below its baseline and they are not independent. Doveri books 3.37 per match against a 25/26 divisional 3.77, and lets 7.0 fouls run per card against 6.72. Napoli were the cleanest side in Serie A last season at 1.29 cards per team per match and are at 1.00 this season on 9.75 fouls; Fiorentina are at 1.50 on 11.0. The division itself has fallen to 3.22 cards per match from 3.77 on 11.21 fouls per team from 12.67. In Doveri’s five previous matches involving these two clubs he averaged 2.2 cards. Four separate series all sitting under their reference points, on four-match team samples and a one-match current referee sample, is a stack of low readings with very little underneath any of them — and Doveri’s 0.16 penalties per match against a divisional 0.28 removes the one event that reliably produces cards.
CornersOK — the clearest single structural change on the card
Napoli’s corner column has moved further than any other series in this fixture and the mechanism is stated elsewhere on the card rather than assumed: 5.47 won against 2.76 conceded in 2025–26 became 5.50 and 5.50 in 2026–27 — identical attacking volume, exactly double the concession, alongside an eleven-point drop in possession and a looser press. Fiorentina read 4.50 for and 3.50 against, both under a divisional 4.78, from a side taking 16.75 shots per match. The caveat is the same four matches as everywhere else, but the corner series is the one where the change is large relative to its own variance and where a named structural cause — less possession, deeper defending, more deep completions at both ends — is already established in section B.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Fiorentina’s 2025–26 record describes a squad that no longer exists. Eight permanent signings and seven loans arrived for roughly €150m against about €5m of income; the centre-forward, both centre-back slots and the defensive midfield were all replaced. Reading 1.08 goals scored and 1.32 conceded per match into this fixture reads a different team.
  • The four-match league sample is two samples. Three matches under Grosso produced one goal scored and nine conceded; one match under Vanoli produced four and two. Any rate quoted over the four averages two regimes, and the current one has a sample of one. This is why the strip carries the 25/26 figure beside every 26/27 one.
  • Napoli’s absences fall exactly where the window did not spend. No defensive midfielder and no permanent centre-back was bought; McTominay and Buongiorno are both out, Badiashile is a loan with an option, and Meret and Spinazzola are out too. The 25/26 defensive record — 36 conceded, the best in the division — was produced by a back line and a midfield that are not available here.
  • The divisional baselines have moved more than the clubs have. Serie A is at 2.96 goals per game against 2.43, 55.6% over 2.5 against 45.8%, 60.0% BTTS against 45.3% and 3.22 cards against 3.77 — on 45 matches. Where a club rate and the divisional rate disagree here, both are short samples and the divisional one is not a fixed point.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and the halves markets — all three need a settled team, and the home side has had a coach for thirteen days while the away side is missing seven players. Least sensitive: Corners and Totals — both rest on counted series that moved together across several independent columns (possession, deep completions, shots, xG) rather than on a result.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than 1X2 here because the goal evidence survives the coach change and the result evidence does not. Fiorentina’s 100% over-2.5 rate spans both Grosso’s three defeats and Vanoli’s win; their 1.88 xG conceded is the worst in the division under either coach. The result record splits into 0–3 and 1–0 by regime.
  • Corners is more robust than Team goals because the corner change has a named cause and the goal change has a name. Napoli’s concession doubling from 2.76 to 5.50 sits alongside possession falling 58.9%→47.8% and PPDA rising 11.87→13.45 — three counted series moving together. Napoli’s attacking output rests on Højlund at €44m and a supply line missing McTominay and Spinazzola.
  • BTTS is more robust than the halves markets because it depends on one fact that four matches establish firmly. Fiorentina have not kept a league clean sheet this season and concede 2.75 per match on 1.88 xG against. The first-half split depends on when goals arrive, and Napoli’s first-half scoring has fallen from 0.82 to 0.25 with no stated mechanism.
  • Discipline is the least robust family on this card despite having the most data behind it. Doveri has 19 matches of record, which is more than any team rate here — but his rate, both clubs’ rates, his record with these two clubs and the divisional rate all sit below their reference points simultaneously, and Napoli were the division’s cleanest side. A stack of independent low readings converging is not the same as a low reading with a reason.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best here is whether Buongiorno and McTominay are available. Napoli bought neither a centre-back nor a defensive midfielder this summer, so both slots are filled from inside a squad that conceded 36 goals last season with those two in them. A confirmed lineup naming both would move Totals and BTTS materially; the club has published an injury list but not a starting eleven, and the lineup is normally released an hour before kickoff.

Spain · LaLiga · Jornada 7

Getafe – Málaga

14:00 CEST · Coliseum Alfonso Pérez, Getafe · the lowest-scoring side in LaLiga over two seasons hosts a promoted club that spent nothing, in front of a referee who awarded penalties at 0.55 per match last season against a divisional 0.35

Played this season
Getafe 8 competitive (6 LaLiga, 2 Conference League play-off) · Málaga 6 competitive (6 LaLiga) · six league matches each clears the five-match line, so the 26/27 figures below are the primary ones and 25/26 is carried for comparison — but Málaga’s 25/26 is a LaLiga 2 record and is read against the LaLiga 2 line, never the top-flight one

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Málaga are promoted and are back in the top flight after an absence long enough that no part of the squad’s record is a LaLiga record. They finished 4th in LaLiga 2 on 73 points from 42 matches and came up through the play-off, not automatically — Racing Santander (82) and Deportivo (77) took the direct places, both of which are also in this division now and both of which Málaga have already faced. Across the full 46-match second-tier campaign including the play-off they were 23-12-11 with 79 scored and 54 conceded. Getafe finished 7th in LaLiga on 51 points — 15 wins, 6 draws, 17 defeats, 32 scored, 38 conceded, a record whose shape matters more than its position: 32 goals in 38 matches and a 26.3% over-2.5 rate against a divisional 50.0%, the extreme end of the division on that measure. The structural headline is that neither club changed anything. José Bordalás remains at Getafe and Juan Francisco “Juanfran” Funes remains at Málaga, the coach who won the promotion — and Málaga, the promoted side, spent nothing at all while Getafe spent about €19m gross on fourteen arrivals. On a slate where nearly every other fixture turns on a new coach or a rebuilt spine, this one turns on two unchanged dugouts rather than two unchanged squads — Getafe moved fourteen players each way — and on the fact that both clubs have started badly: Getafe 17th on 5 points, Málaga bottom on 3.

  • Getafe — turnover high in bodies and net-negative in money; no managerial change, Bordalás renewed for two more seasons. Centre-forward, and the correction that matters: in — Enes Ünal, returning from Bournemouth on a free transfer on 13 August 2026, permanently, to 2029. No fee was paid because Bournemouth still owed Getafe money from the 2023 deal that took Ünal to England for €16.5m; Bournemouth retain a 20% sell-on and up to €1.5m in bonuses. Also in: Martín Satriano from Lyon, about €6.5m, so the club signed two centre-forwards. Defence: in — Zaid Romero from Club Brugge, €5m, and Johan Mojica; out — Juan Berrocal free to Málaga, a defender who left for today’s opponent and may face his old club. Midfield: in — Mario Martín from Real Madrid, €3.5m, Ramón Terrats from Villarreal, €2.5m (a club on this slate) and Sebastián Boselli from River Plate, €1.5m; out — Luis Milla to Como for €6m, also a club on this slate, and Mauro Arambarri. Fourteen in and fourteen out, for a net outlay of roughly €5m — a busier and more expensive window than the two-signing summer first reported here, and one that re-signed the striker rather than selling him. Bordalás renewed for two further seasons.
  • Málaga — turnover medium in bodies, almost nil in money; no managerial change, Funes continuing. Juanfran Funes, who won the play-off, had his contract renewed along with sporting director Loren Juarros, so the promotion staff is intact. Every arrival was a free transfer or a loan. Defence, where the recruitment was concentrated: Fernando Calero free from Espanyol, José Salinas on loan from Espanyol with an option, Adam Aznou on loan from Everton with no option, and Juan Berrocal free from Getafe. Midfield: Carlos Dotor from Celta, fee undisclosed, Jens Cajuste on loan from Napoli with an option, and Pablo Martínez free from Levante. Wide: Juan Cruz on loan from Leganés, loan fee under €500,000. Out: Darko Brašanac and Josué Dorrio at contract end, Juanpe Jiménez to Gamba Osaka for an undisclosed fee, Dani Sánchez by mutual rescission. No centre-forward and no goalkeeper was signed. A side promoted on 79 goals has come up and added nobody to score them, with the forward who did it, Chupe, the subject of reported interest at around €8m that did not become a transfer.
  • Suspensions / absences. Getafe are missing five and have one suspended: Christantus Uche, Kiko, Abdelkabir Abqar, Juanmi and Ramon Terrats are injured, and Mario Martín is suspended. Málaga are missing five, three of them long-term and two of them summer arrivals: Diego Murillo (ACL), Julen Lobete (ACL) and Aaron Ochoa (meniscus) are long-term; Jens Cajuste (muscular) and Fernando Calero (broken rib) are both out — a loan signing and a free signing, two of the four defensive additions. Adam Aznou, a third defensive arrival, is doubtful with a stomach virus. Málaga therefore arrive with three of the four defenders they recruited this summer unavailable or doubtful, in front of a back line that has conceded 11 in six.
  • Sample. Getafe: eight competitive matches — LaLiga 0–3 at Alavés (15 August), 1–0 v Racing Santander (23 August), 0–1 at Osasuna (31 August), 1–1 v Celta Vigo (7 September), 1–1 v Deportivo (13 September), 0–1 at Real Betis (17 September); Conference League play-off 3–1 v Partizan Belgrade (20 August) and 1–2 at Partizan (27 August), through 4–3 on aggregate and into the league phase. Seventeenth on 5 points, 3 scored and 7 conceded, one win in six and four straight league matches without one. The home/away split is total: 1-2-0 at the Coliseum with 3 scored and 2 conceded, 0-0-3 away with nothing scored and 5 conceded, and they have lost all four away matches in all competitions. Málaga: six competitive matches, all league — 0–2 at Atlético Madrid (19 August), 1–1 v Deportivo (24 August), 0–4 at Real Madrid (30 August), 0–0 v Levante (6 September), 1–1 at Celta Vigo (13 September), 1–3 v Villarreal (17 September). Bottom of the table on 3 points, 3 scored and 11 conceded, no win, three draws, and blanked in three of six. They have not scored more than once in a match all season.
  • Referee. Javier Alberola Rojas of the Castilla-La Mancha committee, confirmed by both clubs’ official match pages, with Juan Luis Pulido Santana on VAR. He has a full record and one figure on it stands out sharply. Across 20 LaLiga matches in 2025–26: 4.30 cards per match (80 yellows, 6 reds), 26.4 fouls per match, 6.1 fouls per card, and 0.55 penalties per match. Cards and fouls-per-card are close to divisional — 4.30 against 4.52, 6.1 against LaLiga’s 5.57 — so on booking he is very slightly lenient. The penalty rate is not close to divisional: 0.55 against 0.35, more than half again the LaLiga average, and 11 penalties in 20 matches. This season he has taken three matches at 5.00 cards and 0.33 penalties per match, a rate above his own. History with both clubs and recent: he refereed Málaga 1–1 Deportivo on 24 August this season, a match with five cards and a penalty, and Osasuna 2–1 Getafe in October 2025, in which Getafe took four of the five cards.
  • Not sourced. No 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion figure exists for Málaga at all, because Understat does not cover LaLiga 2 and no other public source reached publishes a second-tier xG series; nothing is estimated in its place, and half of this card’s xG comparison is therefore missing rather than filled. The fee for Carlos Dotor’s move from Celta is undisclosed, as is Juanpe Jiménez’s to Gamba Osaka. No source reached characterises in words how Alberola Rojas reads contact in the box — the 0.55 penalties per match is counted from 20 matches, but the description the spec asks for is not published and is not invented. Everything else the spec asks for is published: LaLiga carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data per match, the official for every fixture, and complete fees.

B · Style & early-season reality

Getafe are the most stable object on this slate and also the most extreme. Possession 39.5% this season against 40.1% last — a difference of half a point — and the most fouls per team of any side in the division in both seasons, 15.21 last and 14.50 now against a divisional 12.58 and 12.41. Bordalás is in his job, the squad is broadly the squad, and the method has not moved: fewest shots (9.26 last season, 10.33 now, against a divisional 12.1), fewest deep completions (2.87 and 3.17), lowest xG (0.89 and 0.85). The one thing that has changed is the defensive side, and it has changed a lot: xG conceded has risen from 1.27 to 1.69 per match while the press has loosened from 10.59 PPDA to 12.04. A side built to make matches small is still making them small at one end — 0.50 goals scored per match, 16.7% of matches over 2.5 — while conceding at a rate its structure was designed to prevent. Its over-2.5 rate has now been far below the divisional line in two consecutive seasons under the same coach, 26.3% against 50.0% and 16.7% against 56.2%, which is the single most repeatable fact on this card.

Málaga’s numbers are a promoted side’s numbers and the step up is visible in every column that exists. In LaLiga 2 they scored 1.72 per match and conceded 1.17, had both teams score in 60.9% of matches and went over 2.5 in 56.5%, all above a second-tier line of 2.63 goals per game. In LaLiga they have 0.50 scored and 1.83 conceded, and an xG line of 0.83 for and 2.13 against — the worst defensive xG figure of any club carded on this slate. The detail that makes the attacking figure worse than it looks is the penalty share: non-penalty xG of 0.58 against a total of 0.83 means roughly 30% of everything they create comes from the spot, and the open-play figure is under 0.6 per match. PPDA has gone from a pressing second-tier side to 14.09, the loosest press of any club on this card, and deep completions to 1.83 per match, the lowest. Possession is 49.9%, which is the one number that looks normal; they are having the ball without doing anything with it. The evidence that this is the division rather than a slump is that it is uniform — every attacking series is at or near the bottom of the league simultaneously — but it is six matches, three of them against Atlético, Real Madrid and Villarreal, and no 2025–26 xG exists to compare it against.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Two winless-shaped sides in the bottom four, one of which has won once in six and lost all four away matches, the other of which has not won at all and is bottom. Getafe’s home record is the only asymmetry — 1-2-0 at the Coliseum against 0-0-3 away, unbeaten in six home matches counting last season — and it rests on three home matches this season producing three goals. LaLiga’s home-win rate is 43.8% against 48.9% last season. A meeting of the 17th and 20th placed sides, both scoring 0.50 per match, is a fixture where the result is the least determined thing on the card.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Margins need a distribution and these two do not have one. Getafe’s six league results are −3, +1, −1, 0, 0, −1 — four matches decided by one goal or none, one rout, and a total of three goals scored all season. Málaga’s are −2, 0, −4, 0, 0, −2: three draws and three defeats of two or more, with nothing in between. Between them they have produced a single margin of exactly two in twelve matches. The handicap needs to price a gap between two sides whose entire combined output is six goals in twelve matches.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — the best-evidenced family on this card by a distance
This is the rare case where a team rate has survived a change of division, a change of season and a divisional baseline moving the other way. Getafe went over 2.5 in 26.3% of 38 matches last season against a divisional 50.0%, and in 16.7% of six this season against a divisional 56.2% — under the same coach, the same squad, the same 39–40% possession and the same bottom-of-the-division shot count. Their xG is 0.85 and Málaga’s non-penalty xG is 0.58; Málaga have not scored twice in a match all season and have been blanked in three of six; Getafe have scored three goals in six league matches. The counted rate, the modelled rate, the shot volume and the deep-completion count all point the same way on both sides, across two seasons on one side. The one genuine counterweight is stated rather than hidden: LaLiga as a division is at 3.05 goals per game against 2.69, so the baseline is moving against these two clubs, and Málaga’s 1.83 conceded is a real concession rate.
BTTSOK — strong on Getafe, unreadable on Málaga’s attack
Getafe’s side is as firm as the totals line: 28.9% BTTS across 38 matches last season against a divisional 56.6%, 33.3% across six this season against 56.2%, and a failed-to-score rate of 44.7% and 50.0% — this is a club that regularly removes one half of the market by itself. The obstacle is that the same fact cuts both ways here: Getafe fail to score half the time, and Málaga have failed to score in three of six, so the market resolves on whether either side manages one. Málaga’s 0.58 non-penalty xG makes their scoring genuinely hard to read, and 30% of their expected output arriving from penalties puts it partly in the hands of an official who gives 0.55 per match. Well evidenced on the pattern, dependent on a single low-probability event on one side.
Team goalsSplit — Getafe-side readable, Málaga-side penalty-dependent
Getafe’s attacking volume is the most consistent series on this card and it is consistently low: 9.26 shots per match last season and 10.33 now against a divisional 12.1, 2.76 and 2.00 on target, 2.87 and 3.17 deep completions, 0.89 and 0.85 xG — five columns, two seasons, one direction, and they have since re-signed the centre-forward they sold in 2023. Málaga are readable in total and not in composition: 0.83 xG of which 0.25 is penalties, the loosest press on the card at 14.09 PPDA, 1.83 deep completions, no centre-forward signed after promotion, and the forward who scored the promotion goals still at the club but reportedly valued elsewhere. A team-goals line on Málaga is partly a line on whether they get a penalty.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — on first-half goals only, not on the first-half result
The first-half goal figures are extreme in the same direction as everything else and are not an artefact of one match. Getafe: 0.17 scored and 0.33 conceded before the break this season, 0.37 and 0.39 last season — under 0.8 first-half goals per match in both, against a divisional 1.42 this season and 1.15 last. Málaga score 0.33 and concede 1.17. Getafe’s figure has now held across 44 matches and two seasons under one coach, which is more evidence than most full-match rates on this slate carry. The first-half result is a different matter and is not readable: Getafe have been level at the break in most of their matches and the 1X2 objections above apply with a shorter sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited — the strongest structural case on this card after totals
Every input points the same way and each is independently sourced. Getafe commit more fouls than any other side carded on this slate: 15.21 per match last season and 14.50 this, against a divisional 12.58 and 12.41, and they take 3.17 cards per match this season against a divisional 2.22 per team. Last season they took 103 yellows and 8 reds in 38 matches. The method producing that is intact: same coach, same 39.5% possession, same low-block-and-foul structure. The official reinforces rather than dilutes it — Alberola Rojas is running at 5.00 cards per match across three matches this season, above his own 4.30 and above the divisional 4.44, and in the one previous Getafe match of his in the record Getafe took four of the five cards. Málaga are the quieter side at 2.67 and 12.3, but their second-tier card rate of 2.37 was set in a division that books at 5.31 per match, so it understates what they will meet here. The one caveat worth stating: LaLiga is the one division on this slate whose card rate has not fallen this season, 4.44 against 4.52.
CornersDangerous
This is the flattest series on the card and the one with no structural story attached. Getafe read 3.83 for and 4.67 against, Málaga 5.00 and 5.33, against a divisional 4.82 — all four numbers within a corner of the baseline and of each other. Málaga’s second-tier figures (4.33 for, 4.63 against against a LaLiga 2 line of 4.74) say the same thing from the division below. A side taking 10.33 shots a match and completing 3.17 passes deep is not a corner-generating structure, and nothing in either window changed the shape that produces them. With six matches per side and no series more than a corner from its baseline, there is nothing here to read.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Málaga’s entire 2025–26 record is a second-tier record and half of it does not exist. 1.72 goals per match and 60.9% BTTS were produced in a division scoring 2.63 per game and booking at 5.31 — and Understat does not cover LaLiga 2, so there is no xG, PPDA or deep-completion baseline to set against their current 0.83/2.13. Their six-match top-flight sample is the only modelled evidence that exists for this club.
  • Three of Málaga’s four defensive signings are unavailable or doubtful. Calero (broken rib) and Cajuste (muscular) are out, Aznou is doubtful; Salinas and Berrocal are the two still standing. A promoted side that recruited almost exclusively in defence is fielding a defence largely without the recruitment, having already conceded 11 in six.
  • Getafe’s low-scoring record is the most repeatable fact on this card and also the oldest. It was set by a squad that has since re-signed Enes Ünal and added Martín Satriano at about €6.5m. The rate has if anything intensified — 26.3% to 16.7% over 2.5 — but the reading rests on continuity of method rather than continuity of personnel, and the defensive half of that method has visibly weakened (1.27 to 1.69 xG conceded).
  • The divisional baseline is moving against both clubs. LaLiga is at 3.05 goals per game against 2.69 last season and 56.2% over 2.5 against 50.0%, on 64 matches. Every low reading on this card is low against a line that has itself risen, which strengthens the club-level finding and weakens any attempt to lean on the divisional number.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and the first-half result — two bottom-four sides with six goals between them in twelve matches give a result no series on this card speaks to. Least sensitive: Totals and Discipline — both rest on Getafe rates that have held across two seasons, 44 matches and an unchanged coach, and both are reinforced rather than contradicted by the second side and by the official.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than 1X2 here because Getafe’s scoring rate has survived everything that would normally break a rate. 26.3% over 2.5 in 38 matches became 16.7% in six, across a summer, a striker sale and a divisional baseline that rose from 50.0% to 56.2% — with possession moving 40.1% to 39.5% and shots 9.26 to 10.33. The result record over the same period is one win in six.
  • Discipline is more robust than Corners because the foul count has a named, unchanged cause and the corner count has none. Getafe’s 15.21 and 14.50 fouls per match come from Bordalás’s low block at 39.5% possession, still in place; their corner figures (3.83 for, 4.67 against) sit within a corner of a 4.82 divisional line and have no structural driver in either window.
  • First-half goals is more robust than the first-half result because one has 44 matches behind it and the other has none. Getafe’s 0.37+0.39 last season and 0.17+0.33 this season are two independent sub-one-goal halves under the same coach. Who is ahead at the break is the 1X2 question asked of a smaller sample.
  • BTTS is less robust than Totals despite resting on the same facts, because it depends on a single event rather than a rate. Getafe’s 28.9% and 33.3% and Málaga’s three blanks in six are the same evidence — but 30% of Málaga’s expected output is penalties, and the referee gives 0.55 per match against a divisional 0.35, so one decision by Alberola Rojas resolves a large share of the market.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Málaga field a recognised centre-forward, and specifically whether Chupe starts. They signed no striker after promoting on 79 goals, their non-penalty xG is 0.58, and three of their four defensive signings are out — so the shape of their team sheet decides whether the Totals and BTTS readings rest on two low-scoring sides or on one low-scoring side and one that cannot field an attack. The club named a 23-man squad on Saturday but not a starting eleven; the lineup is normally published an hour before kickoff.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Auxerre finished 15th in 2025–26 on 34 points — 8 wins, 10 draws, 16 defeats, 34 scored, 44 conceded — and failed to score in 47.1% of their matches, among the highest blank rates of any club carded on this slate. Brest finished 12th on 39 points — 10 wins, 9 draws, 15 defeats, 43 scored, 55 conceded — with a 7-5-5 home record against 3-4-10 away. The structural headline is that both clubs changed head coach in June, a fortnight apart, and for opposite reasons. Auxerre appointed Will Still on 12 June 2026 on a contract to 2028, after Christophe Pélissier’s departure was confirmed on 4 June; Still, 33, arrived from spells at Reims and Lens with a stated plan for an intense, vertical, attacking side. Brest’s change was a bereavement: Éric Roy, the head coach, died at 58 of pancreatic cancer, and on 27 June 2026 the club promoted Julien Lachuer, one of Roy’s assistants since 2023, to succeed him. Auxerre then spent about €17.6m, a club record; Brest’s window could not be established in anything reached. Both clubs sit in the bottom half on three and five points.

  • Auxerre — turnover high and, by this club’s standards, expensive; manager changed 12 June 2026. Roughly €17.6m invested, described in the local press as a record outlay for AJA, in a late and dense recruitment. Centre-forward: in — Cameron Archer, announced on 1 September, and Rémi Labeau-Lascary on loan from RC Lens with an option to buy. Centre-back: in — Axel Tuanzebe, also on 1 September, and Christ Makosso from Luton Town, a 22-year-old Congolese centre-back. Out: Lassine Sinayoko, flagged for departure across several previous windows, and Romain Del Castillo to Osasuna for €1m on 1 September, recorded on this board’s card of 19 September (fx_0919_f2) from the buying club’s side. Two of the four principal arrivals were completed on deadline day, which means the squad Still is fielding has been complete for under three weeks. The carried note from this board’s card of 29 August (fx_9_f18) records the same appointment and a first window that brought a new first-choice goalkeeper, two centre-backs and a centre-forward.
  • Brest — turnover not sourced; head coach changed 27 June 2026 by internal promotion after the death of Éric Roy. Julien Lachuer, an assistant at the club since 2023, was promoted rather than recruited — a continuity appointment in the most difficult circumstances, and the reason Brest’s tactical identity would be expected to persist rather than reset. No summer 2026 transfer ledger for Brest was obtainable from any source reached, so the named-signings requirement of the RESEARCH SPEC is not satisfied for this club and nothing is estimated. That matters more than usual here, because section B shows the largest stylistic change in this fixture belongs to the club whose squad changes cannot be checked.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club was obtainable from any source reached, and none is invented. The Ligue 1 appointments page confirms the officials and the kickoff but carries no team news, and neither club’s published preview material reached here names an absentee. This is a real gap on a card that otherwise has complete counted data, and it is one of two RESEARCH SPEC fields this fixture leaves empty.
  • Sample. Auxerre: four competitive matches, all league — 2–5 at Lens (22 August), 1–3 v Angers (29 August), 1–3 at Lyon (4 September), 1–0 v Nice (12 September) — Nice play later on this same slate. Sixteenth on 3 points from one win, 5 scored and 11 conceded, and all three defeats came by two goals or more, all of them before the Nice win. The home and away split is total: 1-0-1 at the Abbé-Deschamps with 2 scored and 3 conceded, 0-0-2 away with 3 scored and 8 conceded. Brest: four competitive matches, all league — 2–2 at Le Mans (22 August), 2–2 v Toulouse (29 August), 2–1 at Le Havre (5 September), 0–1 v Paris Saint-Germain (13 September), who also play later today. Ninth on 5 points, 6 scored and 6 conceded, and they have not kept a clean sheet in any of the four. They out-shot their opponents 26–8 and 26–6 in the two opening draws and took one point from each.
  • Referee. Guillaume Paradis, confirmed by the Ligue 1 appointments, with Nicolas Durand and Nicolas Rodrigues on the lines, Faouzi Benchabane as fourth official and Hamid Guenaoui and Ludovic Zmyslony on video. Across 14 Ligue 1 matches in 2025–26: 3.93 cards per match — 55 yellows and, notably, no red card at all — 24.1 fouls per match, 6.1 fouls per card, and 0.07 penalties per match, a single award in fourteen matches. Against the division that is average on cards (3.93 against 3.91), marginally strict on conversion (6.1 fouls per card against 6.18) and one of the two lowest penalty rates of any official appointed on this slate — 0.07 against a divisional 0.36, roughly a fifth of it. This season he has taken two matches at 2.50 cards and 10.4 fouls per card, a much longer leash on a two-match sample. History: two Brest matches last season (0–2 at home to Toulouse with five cards, 1–1 at Nantes with four) and no Auxerre match in the record.
  • Not sourced. Brest’s entire summer 2026 transfer ledger was not obtainable, and no injury or suspension information exists for either club in anything reached — two RESEARCH SPEC fields left empty rather than filled. Fees are undisclosed for Cameron Archer, Axel Tuanzebe and Christ Makosso into Auxerre, and for the Labeau-Lascary loan; the €17.6m is the aggregate the local press reports and is not broken out. No source characterises in words how Paradis reads contact in the box; his 0.07 penalties per match is counted from 14 matches. Ligue 1’s 2025–26 record in the ESPN feed holds 305 of 306 matches, as noted in baselines_0920.md.

B · Style & early-season reality

Auxerre under Still look almost exactly like Auxerre under Pélissier at one end and considerably worse at the other, which is the opposite of what a record window and a stated change of philosophy would suggest. Possession 43.5% against 43.9%, PPDA 13.77 against 13.30, deep completions 4.50 against 4.29 — three of the most style-sensitive series in the dataset, all unmoved. Shots are up modestly (12.25 against 10.71) and shots on target sharply (5.25 against 3.50), and the xG has risen from 1.19 to 1.52. The defence has collapsed: xG conceded from 1.47 to 2.59, goals conceded from 1.29 to 2.75 per match, and corners conceded from 4.76 to 7.75 — the third-highest concession rate on this board. The one clear improvement is at the sharp end and it is real: a side that failed to score in 47.1% of 34 matches last season has scored in all four this season. That the two deadline-day signings arrived on 1 September, after three of the four matches, is the obvious caveat on all of it.

Brest are the more interesting side of this fixture by a distance, because their underlying numbers have moved further than almost any club on the board and their points have not followed. Shots have doubled, from 11.24 to 21.75 per match; xG has nearly doubled, from 1.36 to 2.63; shots on target from 3.53 to 7.00; deep completions from 4.65 to 6.00; and PPDA has fallen from 11.25 to 8.96, a 20% increase in pressing intensity. The defensive model has improved with it, from 1.65 xG conceded to 1.26. The corner column says the same thing from the other direction: 6.25 won against 2.50 conceded, having won 3.85 and conceded 5.85 last season — a differential that has swung by nearly six corners a match. And the return is five points from four, with 1.50 goals scored against an expected 2.63. The cost is disciplinary: 15.50 fouls per match against 12.76 last season and a divisional 12.10, the second-highest figure on this board — which is what a press at 8.96 PPDA produces. A continuity appointment has produced the largest stylistic change in this fixture, and it belongs to the club whose window could not be sourced.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs changed head coach in June and both 2025–26 records — 15th on 34 and 12th on 39 — belong to men who are gone. Auxerre completed two of their four principal signings on 1 September, after three of their four matches. Brest’s squad changes could not be sourced at all. Three points against five after four matches, in a division whose home-win rate has fallen from 46.2% to 38.1% with a 31.0% draw rate. The two clubs’ expected-goal figures point one way (Brest 2.63 against Auxerre 1.52) and their points separate them by two. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Auxerre’s four margins are −3, −2, −2, +1 — three defeats by two or more, then a 1–0 win; Brest’s are 0, 0, +1, −1, every one inside a goal. One club has produced nothing but wide margins and the other nothing but narrow ones, on four matches each. Last season’s two meetings were 2–0 to Brest and 3–0 to Auxerre, both by two or more and in opposite directions. A handicap has to price a gap between a side whose model says it should be winning comfortably and a side whose scoreboard says it has been losing comfortably.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — counted, modelled and divisional evidence all agree
This is the clearest reading on the card and every series points the same way. Both clubs have gone over 2.5 in 75.0% of four matches against a divisional 57.1%; between them their eight matches have produced 23 goals; and the combined xG is 3.89 per match against a divisional 3.30. The mechanism is documented on both sides: Brest take 21.75 shots and press at 8.96 PPDA, the third-highest shot volume and fifth-highest pressing intensity on this board; Auxerre concede 2.59 xG and 7.75 corners per match, the fourth-worst and third-highest figures here. Both clubs were below the divisional over-2.5 line last season (44.1% and 50.0% against 52.8%), so this is a change rather than a continuation — and four matches each is the limit.
BTTSWell-suited — both concession records are complete and one blank record has reversed
Both sides read 75.0% against a divisional 57.1%, and the components behind it are unusually firm. Brest have not kept a clean sheet in any of their four league matches (0.0% against 26.5% last season). Auxerre concede 2.75 goals per match on 2.59 xG against. On the scoring side the change is the more striking: Auxerre failed to score in 47.1% of 34 matches last season — among the highest blank rates on this board — and have scored in all four this season, with shots on target up from 3.50 to 5.25. Brest have failed to score once in four. Four separate counted facts, all pointing one way, against a divisional rate that has itself risen from 50.8% to 57.1%.
Team goalsSplit — Brest’s volume is the best evidenced here and is not converting
Brest’s attacking series is emphatic and unanimous: 21.75 shots per match against 11.24 last season, 7.00 on target against 3.53, 6.00 deep completions against 4.65, 2.63 xG against 1.36 and 6.25 corners against 3.85 — five columns, all roughly doubled, under a coach promoted from within. The return is 1.50 goals per match against 2.63 expected, the second-widest negative conversion gap on this board. Auxerre’s side is thinner: 1.25 goals from 1.52 xG on 12.25 shots, with two of the four principal signings three weeks old. One line has exceptional volume and no conversion; the other has a squad that was not complete when three of its four matches were played.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — on first-half goals, carried by Auxerre’s concession
Auxerre concede 1.00 first-half goal per match against 0.59 last season; Brest score 0.75 and concede 0.75 against 0.65 and 0.79. The combined 3.00 first-half goals per match sits well above a divisional 1.19, and Auxerre’s share of it is the part that has moved. They trailed at half-time in each of their three heavy defeats and were level at the break in the win over Nice. Brest are close to unchanged on both figures, which is what a continuity appointment would predict. The first-half result is not readable, inheriting every 1X2 objection on a shorter sample, and Brest’s two opening draws were both 1–1 at the interval.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — a large, mechanically explained foul rise meeting a referee who does not book it
The team side has the clearest structural cause on this card and the official runs against it. Brest commit 15.50 fouls per match — the third-highest figure on this board, against 12.76 last season and a divisional 12.10 — and the mechanism is on the page: PPDA 11.25→8.96. Their cards have fallen anyway, 1.85 to 1.25. Auxerre are at 13.50 fouls and 2.00 cards, both close to last season. Paradis shows no sign of converting it: 3.93 cards per match over 14 matches with no red card at all, and 2.50 cards at 10.4 fouls per card across two matches this season, against a divisional 6.18. The division has fallen from 3.91 cards to 3.21. A rising foul count with a named cause, meeting a long leash.
CornersOK — a wide gap, fully explained on one side and inverted on both
Brest win 6.25 corners and concede 2.50; Auxerre win 5.50 and concede 7.75 — against a divisional 4.79, and 5.25 corners between the two sides. Both have inverted from last season: Brest from 3.85 won and 5.85 conceded, Auxerre from a neutral 4.79 and 4.76. Brest’s inversion has the fuller mechanism — shots 11.24→21.75, xG 1.36→2.63, PPDA 11.25→8.96, deep completions 4.65→6.00 — four series moving with it. Auxerre’s concession rise sits with an xG conceded that has risen by more than a goal, which is at least consistent. Four matches per side, and both clubs moved in the same window their coach did.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Four league matches each is the entire 2026–27 record, and neither club plays outside the league. No cup tie, no European fixture, no Coupe de France match in the record. Every 26/27 figure on this card rests on four matches with nothing else to corroborate it, which is exactly why this fixture was triaged out on sample depth.
  • Auxerre’s squad was not complete when three of its four matches were played. Cameron Archer and Axel Tuanzebe were both announced on 1 September, after the 2–5, 1–3 and 1–3. Reading the 2.75 goals-conceded rate into today reads a defence that has since added a centre-back.
  • Brest’s transfer window could not be sourced at all. Their numbers have changed more than any other club’s in this fixture — shots doubled, xG nearly doubled, corners inverted — under a coach promoted from the existing staff, and there is no ledger reached here to say whether the squad producing that is the squad that finished 12th.
  • Brest are scoring at roughly half their expected rate and Auxerre at roughly their own. 1.50 goals from 2.63 xG against 1.25 from 1.52. When one side’s conversion is that far from its model on four matches, every goals-based reading here is one or two finishes from a different number.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and any Brest team-goals line — a side out-shooting its opponents by a distance with five points from four, and a home side whose recruitment finished three weeks ago. Least sensitive: Totals and BTTS — both rest on four separate counted series per side and a divisional baseline that has moved the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than 1X2 here because the goal evidence is corroborated four ways and the result evidence is contradicted by its own model. 75.0% over 2.5 each, 23 goals in eight matches, a combined 3.89 xG against a divisional 3.30, and Auxerre conceding 2.59 xG. The result has to reconcile Brest’s 2.63 xG with their five points.
  • BTTS is more robust than Team goals because it needs only one goal from each side and both records now supply it. Auxerre’s blank rate has gone from 47.1% across 34 matches to 0.0% across four with shots on target up by half; Brest have not kept a clean sheet all season. A Brest team-goals line, by contrast, asks the 2.63 xG to become goals, which it has not in four attempts.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and the referee is the whole reason. Brest’s 12.76→15.50 fouls has PPDA 11.25→8.96 behind it. Paradis showed no red card in fourteen matches last season and is running at 10.4 fouls per card this season against a divisional 6.18 — the same offences produce fewer cards in front of him.
  • Any penalty-dependent line is the least robust thing on this card. Paradis awarded one penalty in fourteen Ligue 1 matches — 0.07 per match against a divisional 0.36 — and gave none in two matches this season.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Brest’s 21.75 shots per match is a level or four matches of noise. It is double last season’s figure, it underpins the totals, corners and team-goals readings at once, and it has produced 1.50 goals. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth match of shot and xG data, or a published Brest squad list showing whether the personnel behind it changed — and that ledger is exactly what could not be sourced.
