A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Bournemouth finished 6th in 2025–26 on 57 points — 13 wins, 18 draws, 7 defeats, 58 scored, 54 conceded, and qualified for the Europa League; the 18 draws are the number to hold on to, because they are the most of any side in the division and the club has since drawn three of its first four this season. Liverpool finished 5th on 60 points — 17 wins, 9 draws, 12 defeats, 63 scored, 53 conceded, which was enough for the Champions League and not enough for the manager. The structural headline is that this fixture is the transaction itself: Liverpool dismissed Arne Slot and appointed Andoni Iraola, Bournemouth’s head coach, who returns to the Vitality Stadium today in the away dugout — and both clubs then rebuilt at opposite scales. Liverpool spent roughly £247m against £700,000 of income, releasing Mohamed Salah and Ibrahima Konaté and letting Andrew Robertson leave free; Bournemouth spent £54m against nothing, replacing their centre-back, right-back, goalkeeper and centre-forward, and appointed Marco Rose. Every meaningful number on this card belongs to a coach who is no longer at the club that produced it — and on Liverpool’s side, to a forward line that has gone.

  • Bournemouth — turnover high and precisely targeted; manager changed 1 June 2026. Andoni Iraola left for Liverpool and Marco Rose was appointed, formally starting work on 1 June 2026 on a three-year deal, bringing Marco Kurth and Frank Geideck from his Mönchengladbach and Leipzig staff. Centre-forward: in — Álvaro Rodríguez from Elche, £21.5m. Centre-back: in — António Silva from Benfica, £21.4m; out — Marcos Senesi free to Tottenham Hotspur. Goalkeeper: in — Michele Di Gregorio on loan from Juventus; out — Alex Paulsen on loan to Motherwell and Will Dennis to MK Dons, fee undisclosed. Right-back: in — Juanlu Sánchez from Sevilla, £11.1m; out — Álex Jiménez on loan to Fiorentina, who also play on this slate. Roughly £54m spent against £0 of published income — the spine replaced in one window, for a coach who did not pick any of the players it replaced. Contradiction, now resolved: an earlier version of this board carried two incompatible accounts of Enes Ünal — this club’s window had him leaving free to Getafe, Getafe’s had him leaving Getafe for Beşiktaş at €5m. The Bournemouth record is the correct one: Ünal joined Getafe permanently on a free transfer on 13 August 2026, on a deal to 2029, with Bournemouth retaining a 20% sell-on and up to €1.5m in bonuses. No fee changed hands because Bournemouth still owed Getafe from the 2023 €16.5m purchase. Card f2 has been corrected.
  • Liverpool — turnover very high in money and at every position the spec asks about; manager changed in the summer. Arne Slot was dismissed after finishing 5th and Andoni Iraola was appointed from Bournemouth. Attack, the headline: Mohamed Salah released after nine seasons, and replaced with Bradley Barcola from Paris Saint-Germain, £123m — the largest fee anywhere on this slate — alongside Víctor Muñoz from Osasuna, £34.5m and Djylian N’Guessan from Saint-Étienne, £4.3m. Centre-back: in — Jérémy Jacquet from Rennes, £55m, and Ronald Araújo on loan from Barcelona; out — Ibrahima Konaté released and Rhys Williams released. Goalkeeper: in — Lucca Brughmans from Genk, £30m. Defensive midfield: out — Stefan Bajcetic to Celta Vigo, fee undisclosed; nobody was bought. Left-back: out — Andrew Robertson free to Tottenham. Roughly £246.9m spent against £700,000 received — a net outlay of about £246m, and the two players released, Salah and Konaté, left for nothing.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Bournemouth are missing four, two in each unit: Julián Araújo (defender, thigh), Veljko Milosavljević (defender, knock), Junior Kroupi (forward, foot) and Amine Adli (forward, calf). Liverpool are missing four, and three of them are attacking or defensive first-choices: Hugo Ekitike (forward, achilles), Giovanni Leoni (defender, knee), Conor Bradley (defender, knee) and Federico Chiesa (forward, back). Both clubs are missing a right-back — Bournemouth’s £11.1m signing is available but Araújo is not, and Liverpool’s Bradley is out with Frimpong expected to start. Neither absence list touches the part of either squad that the cards below actually turn on: Bournemouth’s new centre-back and Liverpool’s new front line are both available.
  • Sample. Bournemouth: six competitive matches — Premier League 1–2 at Manchester City (23 August), 1–1 v Everton (29 August), 2–2 at Newcastle (5 September), 2–2 v Brentford (12 September); EFL Cup 4–0 v Lincoln City (8 September); Europa League 2–1 at Real Sociedad (17 September), their first European away win. Sixteenth on 3 points from three draws and a defeat, 6 scored and 7 conceded — and both teams have scored in every one of the four league matches. Liverpool: six competitive matches — Premier League 2–2 at Newcastle (23 August), 2–2 v Nottingham Forest (29 August), 2–0 at Ipswich (4 September), 0–0 v Fulham (12 September); EFL Cup 3–1 v Tottenham (15 September); Champions League 2–1 v Atlético Madrid (9 September). Tenth on 6 points from one win and three draws, unbeaten, 6 scored and 4 conceded. Between them the two clubs have drawn six of their eight league matches this season and 21 of their last 42 — Bournemouth 18 draws in 38 last season plus three in four, Liverpool 9 in 38 plus three in four.
  • Referee. Michael Oliver, confirmed, with Craig Pawson on VAR. He is the most experienced official on this slate and the most lenient. Across 29 Premier League matches in 2025–26: 3.10 cards per match (88 yellows, 2 reds), 22.9 fouls per match, 7.4 fouls per card, and 0.07 penalties per match — two penalties in twenty-nine matches. Against the division that is low on every axis: 3.10 cards against 3.86, 7.4 fouls per card against 5.60, and 0.07 penalties against 0.24 — under a third of the Premier League rate, the lowest penalty rate of any official appointed on this slate. This season he has taken four matches at 3.00 cards, 22.8 fouls, 7.6 fouls per card and 0.25 penalties, so the card and foul figures are reproducing almost exactly while the penalty figure is on one award in four matches. History, and a lot of it: four Bournemouth matches last season (Leeds 2–2, a goalless draw at home to Chelsea, 2–0 at Wolves, 2–1 at Arsenal) and two Liverpool matches (1–0 at Burnley with a penalty, 1–2 at home to Manchester United). Across those six he averaged 2.8 cards a match, below even his own rate.
  • Not sourced. The fees for Stefan Bajcetic to Celta Vigo, Will Dennis to MK Dons and Archie Harris to Wigan are undisclosed and are not estimated. The Enes Ünal contradiction above is now resolved in this card’s favour — he joined Getafe on a free, and card f2 has been corrected — two club-level windows record incompatible destinations for the same player in the same summer, and the board reports both rather than choosing. No source reached characterises in words how Oliver reads contact in the box; his 0.07 penalties per match is counted from 29 matches, which is itself the strongest statement available, but the description the spec asks for is not published and is not invented. Everything else is published: the Premier League carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data, the official and VAR for every fixture, and complete fees.

B · Style & early-season reality

Bournemouth under Rose look, so far, almost exactly like Bournemouth under Iraola, which is the opposite of what a £54m spine replacement and a new German coach would suggest. PPDA 9.55 against 9.68 last season, possession 47.9% against 50.1%, xG 1.79 against 1.76, xG conceded 1.43 against 1.49 — four of the most style-sensitive series in the dataset, all within noise of the previous regime. Shots are up slightly (14.75 against 13.79) and shots on target sharply (6.00 against 4.66), deep completions are marginally down (6.50 against 7.24). What has changed is the discipline column and it has changed a lot: 3.25 cards per match against 2.37 last season, on almost identical fouls, 12.25 against 12.03 — more bookings from the same number of offences. The result profile is the club’s signature amplified: no clean sheet and no blank in four matches, both teams scoring in 100% of them, and three draws. A side that drew 18 of 38 has started by drawing three of four.

Liverpool are the side that has actually changed shape, and the change is Iraola’s trademark rather than an inference. PPDA has fallen from 10.03 to 7.06 — by a distance the most aggressive press of any club carded on this slate, and a 30% increase in pressing intensity on the same possession share (60.3% against 59.3%). Two things move with it. Fouls have risen from 10.08 to 13.00 per match and cards from 1.53 to 2.50 — Liverpool were the cleanest side in the Premier League last season and are now booking at over one and a half times their own rate, which is what a press that wins the ball higher and more often concedes at the other end of the same action. And deep completions have collapsed from 9.82 to 4.75 per match: they are getting into the final third far less often, while their xG holds at 1.74 against 1.77. That is a side creating the same volume from fewer, faster entries rather than from sustained territory. The attacking personnel behind it is entirely new — Salah released, Barcola at £123m, Muñoz at £34.5m, Isak and Wirtz in the expected eleven — so the xG continuity is being produced by different players in a different structure, which is the reason it should not be read as continuity. The one clear result-level consequence so far: three draws in four, and 1.00 conceded per match against 1.39.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs changed head coach and one of the changes is this fixture — Iraola left Bournemouth for Liverpool, so neither club’s 2025–26 record describes the team that will be picked. Liverpool spent £247m and released their record scorer; Bournemouth replaced four of their spine positions. What has emerged is a pile of draws: six from eight league matches between them this season, 21 from the last 42. The division’s home-win rate has fallen to 37.0% from 42.6% with draws at 26.1%. Two unbeaten-or-nearly sides that cannot stop drawing, with new coaches at both ends, is a result nothing on this card speaks to.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — and the draw density is the specific reason
A draw-no-bet or handicap line is worst placed exactly where draws dominate, and these two are the densest draw pairing on the slate. Bournemouth have drawn 21 of their last 42 league matches, Liverpool 12 of theirs; six of the eight league matches they have played this season finished level. Bournemouth’s four margins are −1, 0, 0, 0; Liverpool’s are 0, 0, +2, 0. Seven of eight are within one goal. That distribution makes the handicap pricing problem hardest at exactly the line it would be set at, and the DNB variant removes the single most likely outcome from the market.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — both sides’ goal evidence survives the coaching change
This is the rare family where the counted rate and the modelled rate agree across the regime change on both sides. Bournemouth: 55.3% over 2.5 last season and 75.0% this, with xG 1.76 then 1.79 and xG conceded 1.49 then 1.43 — four series essentially unmoved by a new coach and a £54m spine. Liverpool: 60.5% then 50.0%, xG 1.77 then 1.74. Both clubs are above the divisional xG line of 1.63 per team, and Bournemouth’s four matches have produced 13 goals. The division is at 2.83 goals per game against 2.75 — the smallest baseline movement of the five leagues on this slate, so the reference point here is unusually stable. The caveat is Liverpool’s 0–0 with Fulham and a 1.00 goals-conceded rate under a coach pressing 30% harder; that is four matches against Bournemouth’s 42 of consistency.
BTTSWell-suited — the single best-evidenced line on this card
These were the two highest BTTS rates in the Premier League last season and the pattern has not broken. Bournemouth 65.8% and Liverpool 68.4% across 38 matches each, against a divisional 56.1%; Bournemouth 100% across four this season and Liverpool 50%. Underneath it: Bournemouth have not kept a league clean sheet this season and have not failed to score either — 0% and 0% across four matches — and last season both figures were extreme in the same direction (28.9% clean sheets, 18.4% blanks; Liverpool 10.5% blanks, the lowest in the division). Both clubs concede at or above 1.4 xG and create at or above 1.74. The evidence spans two seasons, two coaches per club and 84 matches, which is more than any other family on this card can claim.
Team goalsOK — Bournemouth-side firm, Liverpool-side rebuilt
Bournemouth’s attacking series is the most stable thing in this fixture: 13.79 shots then 14.75, 4.66 on target then 6.00, 1.76 xG then 1.79, 7.24 deep completions then 6.50 — across a change of coach and a new centre-forward at £21.5m. Liverpool’s total is equally stable (1.77 then 1.74 xG, 15.50 then 16.00 shots) but its composition is not: Salah released, Barcola at £123m, Muñoz at £34.5m, Ekitike and Chiesa injured, and deep completions halved from 9.82 to 4.75. The same xG arriving from half the final-third entries with an entirely new front line is a different production process reaching the same number over four matches. The clean-sheet and failed-to-score sub-lines point in opposite directions and both are short: Liverpool have kept clean sheets in 50% of four matches against 26.3% last season, Bournemouth have failed to score in 0% of four against 18.4%, and one of those has to give.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — on first-half goals, driven by one side
Bournemouth’s first-half attacking rate has nearly doubled and it is not one match: 1.25 scored before the break this season against 0.68 last, having led at half-time in three of their four league matches (1–0 at City, 1–0 v Everton, 2–1 at Newcastle, 1–1 v Brentford). That is a counted change with a plausible structural companion in Rose’s higher shots-on-target rate. Against it: every one of those leads was surrendered or reduced, which is the draw pattern showing up inside the match as well as at the end of it. Liverpool are flat at 0.50 and 0.50. The combined 2.25 first-half goals per match sits well above the divisional 1.26. The first-half result inherits the 1X2 problems on a shorter sample and is not readable.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — a genuine structural signal pointing into a lenient official
The team-level evidence is the clearest card story on this slate and it has a named mechanism. Liverpool’s PPDA has gone from 10.03 to 7.06, fouls from 10.08 to 13.00 and cards from 1.53 to 2.50 per match — the division’s cleanest side last season, now booking at 63% above its own rate, with the pressing figure to explain it. Bournemouth have gone from 2.37 to 3.25 cards on unchanged fouls. Both point up. The official points hard the other way: Oliver books 3.10 per match against a divisional 3.86, lets 7.4 fouls run per card against 5.60, and across his six previous matches involving these two clubs averaged 2.8 cards. A rising team signal meeting the most lenient whistle in the division is a genuine split, not a wash — the foul count is the part that is structurally established, the conversion of fouls into cards is the part the appointment argues against.
CornersDangerous
Both corner series have fallen towards the baseline and neither fall has a stated cause. Bournemouth 5.58 won last season to 4.50 now; Liverpool 6.11 to 4.25 — Liverpool’s drop of nearly two per match is the larger, and it sits alongside deep completions halving from 9.82 to 4.75, which is at least consistent, but four matches cannot separate a structural change from a run. Both clubs now read within half a corner of a divisional 4.68 at both ends. There is nothing to distinguish either side from the league average on this measure, and the one series that moved did so in a direction that the totals and BTTS readings do not require.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Liverpool’s 2025–26 numbers were produced by a squad missing its three most recognisable components. Salah and Konaté were released and Robertson left free; £247m arrived, including a £123m winger and a £55m centre-back. The xG line looks continuous (1.77 to 1.74) and the personnel producing it is not.
  • The press change is real and measurable, and its consequences are four matches old. PPDA 10.03→7.06, fouls 10.08→13.00, cards 1.53→2.50, deep completions 9.82→4.75. Four separate series move together, which makes the change credible — but nothing here establishes what it does to results, and the result so far is three draws.
  • Bournemouth’s numbers look unchanged because the coach changed in the direction the style was already going. Rose’s PPDA (9.55) and possession (47.9%) are within noise of Iraola’s. That is a finding about two similar coaches, not evidence that a £54m rebuilt spine plays like the old one; the defensive personnel is almost entirely new and has four matches behind it.
  • The draw density is the dominant fact on this card and it is not a projection. Bournemouth 18 draws in 38 plus three in four; Liverpool 9 in 38 plus three in four; six of their eight matches this season level. It is reported as a counted rate, and it is the reason the result-based families are rated as they are.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and the clean-sheet sub-family — all three need a settled result distribution, and this pairing’s distribution is overwhelmingly concentrated on the draw. Least sensitive: BTTS and Totals — both rest on rates that held across 84 matches, two coaching changes and two complete squad rebuilds.

E · Short market-type guide

  • BTTS is more robust than 1X2 here because it is the one rate that survived both coaching changes at both clubs. 65.8% and 68.4% were the division’s two highest last season against a 56.1% line; Bournemouth are at 100% over four and have neither kept a clean sheet nor failed to score. The result records over the same period produced 21 draws in 42.
  • Totals is more robust than Corners because the goal series held and the corner series did not. Bournemouth’s xG moved 1.76→1.79 and Liverpool’s 1.77→1.74 across a change of coach; Liverpool’s corners fell 6.11→4.25 and Bournemouth’s 5.58→4.50, both to within half a corner of the divisional 4.68, with only four matches to tell a change from a run.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and the reason is the appointment. Liverpool’s 10.08→13.00 fouls has a measured cause in PPDA 10.03→7.06. Turning those fouls into bookings runs through Michael Oliver, who books 3.10 against a divisional 3.86, allows 7.4 fouls per card against 5.60, and gave 2.8 per match across six previous matches involving these clubs.
  • Any penalty-dependent line is the least robust thing on this card. Oliver awarded two penalties in twenty-nine Premier League matches last season, 0.07 per match against a divisional 0.24, and Bournemouth have had none at all this season. Whatever the box action, the official appointed to judge it has the lowest award rate on this slate.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Liverpool’s press holds up over ninety minutes against a side that has scored first in three of four. The 7.06 PPDA, the 13.00 fouls and the 4.75 deep completions are all four matches old and all point at a high-risk, high-reward structure; Bournemouth’s 1.25 first-half goals per match is the direct test of it. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth league match of pressing data, or a confirmed eleven showing whether Araújo or Jacquet partners van Dijk behind it — the lineup is normally published an hour before kickoff.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted, and neither is new to this division — Leeds came up in 2025–26 and this is their second consecutive Premier League season. Leeds finished 14th in 2025–26 on 47 points — 11 wins, 14 draws, 13 defeats, 49 scored, 56 conceded — with one of the widest venue splits on this board: 9-5-5 at Elland Road against 2-9-8 away. Crystal Palace finished 15th on 45 points — 11 wins, 12 draws, 15 defeats, 41 scored, 51 conceded — and won the Conference League. The structural headline is that one club kept its coach and rebuilt around him while the other lost both its manager and its most valuable player in the same summer. Leeds did not change head coach. Crystal Palace did: Oliver Glasner left for Nottingham Forest in July 2026 and a replacement was appointed in June, and the club then sold Eberechi Eze to Arsenal for £60m rising to £67m — a record fee received by Crystal Palace. Leeds spent heavily in the other direction. Two of the players involved moved between clubs that play elsewhere on this slate: Harry Wilson arrived free from Fulham and James Trafford from Manchester City for £40m.

  • Leeds United — turnover high and concentrated on the goalkeeper and the back line; no managerial change. Goalkeeper: in — James Trafford from Manchester City, £40m, recorded from the selling club’s side on card f6, and Illan Meslier free; out — Karl Hein to Werder Bremen. Centre-back: in — Nico Elvedi and Tarik Muharemović; out — Jakub Kiwior to Porto. Correction: an earlier version of this card listed Piero Hincapié among Leeds’s arrivals at £25m, from a Premier League transfer round-up. He did not join Leeds: Arsenal made his loan from Bayer Leverkusen permanent on 1 July 2026 for a reported £34.5m plus add-ons on a five-year deal, which is what card f9 records from Leverkusen’s side. He is removed here. Wide: in — Christos Tzolis from Club Brugge, £34m, and Harry Wilson free from Fulham, today’s slate-mate at f12; out — Leandro Trossard to Beşiktaş, £15.3m. A goalkeeper, two centre-backs and two wide forwards in one window, with the coach unchanged — the opposite configuration to most of this slate, where the dugout changed and the spine stayed. Contradiction, now resolved: an earlier version of this card had Hincapié joining Leeds for £25m, while the Leverkusen window on card f9 had him joining Arsenal for €40m. The Leverkusen record is the correct one: Arsenal converted a season-long loan into a permanent transfer on 1 July 2026 for a reported £34.5m plus add-ons, on a five-year deal to 2031. He never joined Leeds and has been removed from the arrivals above.
  • Crystal Palace — turnover high, dominated by one sale; manager changed in June 2026. Oliver Glasner, who won the Conference League, left for Nottingham Forest in July 2026, and Palace appointed a replacement in June — the sequence recorded on this board’s card of 17 September (fx_0917_f5) as a coach appointed in June with a rebuilt defence. Out, the headline: Eberechi Eze to Arsenal, £60m rising to £67m — the largest fee Crystal Palace have ever received, and Maxence Lacroix, reported at £52m. In: Yéremy Pino, a Spain international, and Jaydee Canvot, a France under-21 defender. The club that won a European trophy has sold its best attacking player and a centre-back for a combined nine figures, replaced them with two younger players, and changed coach. The carried note from fx_0917_f5 puts it directly: Palace’s European record belongs to Glasner and to a defence that has since been sold.
  • Suspensions / absences. Crystal Palace have a suspension and it matters: Axel Disasi begins a three-match ban after his red card against Ipswich. They are also missing three: Dean Henderson (ankle), Jean-Philippe Mateta (hamstring) and Chadi Riad (knee) — a goalkeeper, the centre-forward and a centre-back, which together with Disasi’s ban removes four of the spine positions the spec weights. Leeds are missing three: Harry Wilson (quad — one of the summer’s two free signings), Joe Rodon (hamstring) and Mateo Joseph (knee). No Leeds suspension is reported. Palace arrive having played Lech Poznań in the Europa League on Thursday, three days ago, winning 4–0.
  • Sample. Leeds: six competitive matches — Premier League 1–0 at Nottingham Forest (22 August), 1–1 v Brentford (30 August), 1–1 at Brighton (5 September), 4–1 v Newcastle (14 September); EFL Cup 2–0 at Nottingham Forest (25 August) and 3–6 at Chelsea (9 September). Sixth on 8 points, unbeaten in the league from two wins and two draws, 7 scored and 3 conceded — and only one of their four league matches has gone over 2.5, in a 4–1. Three of their four league results were 1–0, 1–1 and 1–1. Crystal Palace: six competitive matches — Premier League 0–2 at Everton (22 August), 1–4 v Manchester City (28 August), 3–2 at Fulham (5 September), 2–3 v Ipswich (12 September); EFL Cup 3–0 v Middlesbrough (8 September); Europa League 4–0 v Lech Poznań (17 September). Seventeenth on 3 points from one win, 6 scored and 11 conceded, and they have not kept a clean sheet in any league match. Both home league matches were lost, 1–4 and 2–3.
  • Referee. Adam Herczeg, confirmed — and this is his Premier League debut. He has no top-flight record of any kind: 34 Championship matches to date, having begun refereeing at 15 and worked up through the National League, and he is 33. That is the single most important thing on this card’s discipline row, and it is an absence of evidence rather than a finding. There is no cards-per-game figure, no fouls-per-card ratio and no penalty rate at this level to quote, because none exists; his Championship record is not in the ESPN top-flight dataset this board counts from and is not imported across divisions. An official with no record at the level is itself a source of variance, as the RESEARCH SPEC notes, and it is carried into the Discipline row below for that reason alone. Timothy Wood and Simon Long run the lines and Farai Hallam is fourth official; no VAR appointment was published in anything reached. He has no history with either club at this level.
  • Not sourced. No Premier League record exists for the referee, so the entire referee block of the RESEARCH SPEC — cards per game, fouls per card, penalties per game, history with either club — is empty here and nothing is estimated from his Championship work. Fees were not obtainable for Yéremy Pino or Jaydee Canvot into Palace, nor for Nico Elvedi, Tarik Muharemović or Jakub Kiwior; the Lacroix figure is reported at £52m without a confirmed structure. The identity and exact appointment date of The Hincapié destination is now settled: he joined Arsenal, not Leeds, and has been removed from the arrivals above. Palace’s head coach could not be established beyond “appointed in June”, carried from this board’s own earlier card; his name is not stated here rather than guessed. No VAR appointment was published.

B · Style & early-season reality

Leeds have pressed harder and made their matches smaller, which is an unusual combination and is visible in four columns. PPDA has fallen from 12.06 to 9.60 — a 20% increase in pressing intensity — while possession has dropped from 45.8% to 43.8%, shots are flat at 12.00 against 12.58, and deep completions are flat at 5.00 against 5.29. They press more, hold less, and create the same amount. The output is where the change sits: 1.75 goals per match against 1.29 from an xG of 1.54 against 1.57 — the same model, better finishing — and 0.75 conceded against 1.47 from an xG conceded of 1.32 against 1.48. Both ends are running ahead of the model, which is what an unbeaten start off four matches usually means. The counted shape is emphatically low-scoring: three of the four league matches finished 1–0, 1–1 and 1–1, and only the 4–1 against Newcastle cleared 2.5. Against that sits a 3–6 at Chelsea in the cup, which is outside every league rate above.

Palace have been taken apart in exactly the places the summer emptied. xG conceded has risen from 1.58 to 2.23, goals conceded from 1.34 to 2.75, and first-half goals conceded from 0.66 to 1.50 — they have shipped six first-half goals in four matches. The pressing figure explains part of it: PPDA has gone from 13.44 to 16.36, the second-loosest press on this board, on possession down from 45.9% to 41.9%. At the other end the model has barely moved (1.64 to 1.61 xG) but the delivery mechanism has collapsed: corners won have halved, from 4.21 to 2.25, the second-lowest figure on this board, and shots are down from 11.63 to 10.25. That is what selling a £60m creator and a £52m centre-back, changing coach, and then losing Mateta, Henderson, Riad and Disasi looks like in the data. The 4–0 against Lech Poznań on Thursday is the counter-example and it is three days old and in a different competition.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Palace changed head coach and sold Eze for £60m and Lacroix for a reported £52m; four of their spine positions are unavailable today through injury or suspension. Leeds kept their coach but replaced a goalkeeper and three centre-backs. Leeds are unbeaten and sixth, Palace seventeenth on three points — but Leeds’s figures are running ahead of their model at both ends on four matches, and Palace beat Lech Poznań 4–0 three days ago. The division’s home-win rate has fallen to 37.0% from 42.6%. Last season these two drew 0–0 and Leeds won 4–1, both under different circumstances.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Leeds’s four league margins are +1, 0, 0, +3 and Palace’s are −2, −3, +1, −1 — one side clustered at zero or one, the other spread from −3 to +1. Leeds have drawn two of four after drawing fourteen of thirty-eight last season, the most in the division; Palace drew twelve. Both clubs’ expected-goal figures for are within 0.07 of each other (1.54 against 1.61), so the model sees no attacking gap at all; the entire separation is Palace’s 2.23 xG conceded. A handicap has to price that on four matches with four Palace absentees.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the two clubs sit at opposite extremes of the same divisional line
This is the sharpest disagreement between two sides of one fixture on this board. Leeds have gone over 2.5 in 25.0% of four matches, matched by six other clubs here and bettered only by Getafe; Palace in 75.0%, and their four matches have produced 17 goals against Leeds’s 10. Last season they were identical at 55.3% each, against a divisional 55.0%. The models split the difference and lean high: a combined 3.15 xG per match against a divisional 3.26, with Palace’s 2.23 conceded the third-worst figure on this board. The division is at 2.83 against 2.75, the most stable baseline on the slate. One club is making its matches small and the other cannot stop conceding; four matches each cannot say which governs.
BTTSWell-suited — both sides read 75% and both components are independently firm
Both clubs sit at 75.0% against a divisional 50.0%, which is already the widest agreement on this card, and the components hold it up from opposite directions. Leeds have failed to score in 0.0% of four matches, against 31.6% last season — they have scored in every league game. Palace have kept no clean sheet at all (0.0% against 31.6% last season) and concede 2.23 xG per match. The two facts that would break the market are Leeds keeping a clean sheet, which they have managed once in four against a Palace side that has scored in three of four, and Palace failing to score, which they have done once. Both clubs were above or at the divisional line last season too (57.9% and 52.6%), so this is continuation rather than a four-match swing.
Team goalsSplit — neither volume has moved, both conversions have
The unusual feature here is that both clubs’ creation is static and both clubs’ output is not. Leeds: 12.00 shots against 12.58, 5.00 deep completions against 5.29, 1.54 xG against 1.57 — three flat series — producing 1.75 goals against 1.29. Palace: 10.25 shots against 11.63, 1.61 xG against 1.64 — also flat — producing 1.50 against 1.08. Both are finishing better than last season on unchanged chance volume, which is the least durable kind of improvement. The one real volume change on the card is Palace’s corners halving from 4.21 to 2.25, and their centre-forward, Mateta, is injured.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited on first-half goals — the widest asymmetry on this card
Palace have conceded six first-half goals in four league matches — 1.50 per match, against 0.66 last season — and trailed at half-time in three of the four. Leeds have conceded one, 0.25 per match against 0.42, and scored 1.00 before the break against 0.63, leading 3–0 at the interval against Newcastle. That is two six-match-old series moving hard in opposite directions from their own prior seasons, with a combined 3.25 first-half goals per match against a divisional 1.26. The mechanism is on the page: Palace’s PPDA has loosened from 13.44 to 16.36 and their defence is missing Riad, Disasi and Henderson. The first-half result is the weaker half of the family and inherits the 1X2 objections.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous — and the referee is the reason, not the teams
No Premier League record exists for Adam Herczeg. This is his debut at the level: there is no cards-per-game figure, no fouls-per-card ratio, no penalty rate and no history with either club to read. Every other card on this slate rates discipline against a referee record of nine to twenty-nine matches; here there is none, and his 34 Championship matches are in a division this board does not count from and are not imported. On the team side both clubs are unusually quiet and getting quieter: Leeds 1.25 cards on 10.75 fouls against 1.66 and 10.47; Palace 1.00 on 9.00 against 2.00 and 10.47 — the second-lowest foul count on this board — against a divisional 1.96 per team and 11.40 fouls. Two low, falling team rates and a total absence of officiating evidence is the least readable discipline row on the board. Palace’s Disasi red against Ipswich is the one contrary data point.
CornersSplit — one large, recent movement against two seasons of flatness
Leeds are static and close to the line: 4.50 won and 5.25 conceded against 4.42 and 5.08 last season, with a divisional 4.68 — four numbers within a quarter-corner across two seasons. Palace are the movement: 2.25 won against 4.21 last season, the second-lowest corners-won figure on this board, with 5.00 conceded against 5.24. The supporting evidence is thin but consistent: shots down from 11.63 to 10.25, possession down from 45.9% to 41.9%, and the £60m creator sold. Against it, four matches, and a 4–0 European win three days ago that is not in the count. A real asymmetry on one side, no signal at all on the other.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • There is no referee evidence on this card at all. Adam Herczeg is making his Premier League debut, so the discipline row rests on two team rates and nothing else. Every other fixture on this slate reads its cards row against nine to twenty-nine matches of officiating record.
  • Palace are missing four of the positions the spec weights. Mateta (centre-forward), Henderson (goalkeeper), Riad (centre-back) and Disasi (centre-back, suspended three matches). Their 2.23 xG conceded was accumulated partly without them; today’s defence is not the one that produced last season’s 51 goals against either, after Lacroix was sold.
  • Both clubs are finishing above their models at both ends on four matches. Leeds score 1.75 from 1.54 xG and concede 0.75 from 1.32; Palace score 1.50 from 1.61. Three of those four gaps favour Leeds, and none has more than four matches behind it.
  • Leeds’s low-scoring profile contradicts their own prior season. 25.0% over 2.5 this season against 55.3% last, on unchanged shots, xG and deep completions. Nothing in the style data explains it, which usually means finishing and opposition rather than method.
  • Most sensitive: Discipline, on a debut referee with no record, and 1X2, where Leeds’s position and Leeds’s model disagree. Least sensitive: BTTS — 75% on both sides, a 0% blank rate for Leeds and a 0% clean-sheet rate for Palace, both continuing rather than contradicting last season.

E · Short market-type guide

  • BTTS is more robust than Totals here because the two clubs agree about it and disagree about everything else. Both at 75.0% against a divisional 50.0%, with Leeds scoring in every league match and Palace conceding in every one. On totals the same two clubs read 25.0% and 75.0% after being identical at 55.3% last season.
  • First-half goals is more robust than the first-half result because the concession side has a mechanism. Palace’s 0.66→1.50 first-half goals conceded sits with PPDA 13.44→16.36 and a back line missing Riad, Disasi and Henderson. Who leads at the break asks the 1X2 question of a shorter sample.
  • Palace’s side of Corners is more robust than Leeds’s because one series moved and the other did not. 4.21→2.25 won, with shots 11.63→10.25, possession 45.9%→41.9% and Eze sold for £60m. Leeds’s four corner figures sit within a quarter of each other across two seasons and within half a corner of the divisional line.
  • Discipline is the least robust family on this card and the reason is unusual: it is not that the evidence conflicts, but that half of it does not exist. A Premier League debutant has no cards rate, no fouls-per-card ratio and no penalty rate. Both clubs are booking below their own 2025–26 figures on falling foul counts, which removes the other half.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Leeds keep making matches small once they meet a defence conceding 2.23 xG. Their 25.0% over-2.5 rate was set against Nottingham Forest, Brentford and Brighton, all of whom defend; their one high-scoring league match was 4–1 and their one high-scoring match overall was a 3–6 cup tie at Chelsea. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth league match against open opposition — today is the first such test.

England · Premier League · Matchweek 5

Manchester City – Sunderland

15:00 CEST (14:00 BST) · Etihad Stadium, Manchester · a first-season coach with a £456m window, four league wins from four and his best player starting a three-match ban, against a European-qualified Sunderland who cleared twenty players out and whose underlying numbers have jumped further than anyone’s on this slate

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted this season, but one of them was last season and the fact still governs its numbers. Manchester City finished 2nd in 2025–26 on 78 points — 23 wins, 9 draws, 6 defeats, 77 scored, 35 conceded. Sunderland finished 7th on 54 points in their first season back — 14 wins, 12 draws, 12 defeats, 42 scored, 48 conceded — and qualified for the Europa League, their first European campaign in 53 years. The structural headline is a change of manager at one club and a change of squad at the other, and both are unusually large. Pep Guardiola was succeeded by Enzo Maresca, appointed on 29 June 2026 on a three-year contract with roughly £17m in compensation paid to Chelsea, and City then spent about £456m against £307m of income — nine signings, twenty-nine departures, and the club transfer record broken twice in one window. Sunderland did the opposite kind of violence to their squad: about £58m spent against £27m received, five in and twenty out, after a promotion summer in which they had already spent a record £161m on fifteen players. Régis Le Bris stays, which makes Sunderland one of the few clubs on this slate whose last-season record belongs to the coach picking the team — though not to the squad.

  • Manchester City — turnover extreme in both directions, concentrated almost entirely in central midfield; manager changed 29 June 2026. Enzo Maresca succeeded Pep Guardiola, and the recruitment was aimed at one unit. Central midfield, where the record went twice: Enzo Fernández from Chelsea, £125m — the club record — Elliot Anderson from Nottingham Forest, £116m, Ayyoub Bouaddi from Lille, £86m — £81.3m fixed plus £4.3m in add-ons, a five-year deal to 2031 and a Premier League record for a teenager and Allan Elias from Palmeiras, £34.4m: £361m on four central midfielders in one window. Out of the same unit: Rodri to Barcelona, £65.4m, Bernardo Silva free to Real Madrid, and Tijjani Reijnders to Al-Qadsiah. Wide: in — Iliman Ndiaye from Everton, £60m, Mathys Detourbet from Troyes £21.4m, Jeremy Monga from Leicester £10m; out — Savinho to Tottenham, £75m. Goalkeeper: in — Gerónimo Rulli from Marseille, fee undisclosed, and Pierce Charles from Sheffield Wednesday £3m; out — James Trafford to Leeds, £40m. Centre-back: out — John Stones free, Manuel Akanji to Inter, £13m, Nathan Aké; none bought. Roughly £455.8m spent against £307.2m received, a net £148.6m — and the one position not addressed is the one that lost three senior players.
  • Sunderland — turnover very high in bodies, moderate in money; no managerial change, Le Bris continuing. The club that spent £161m on fifteen players to survive has spent a fifth of that to play in Europe. In: Malick Fofana from Lyon, £25.7m (left wing), Dayann Methalie from Toulouse, £24m (left-back), Jules Ahoka from Royal Antwerp, £8.6m (central midfield), Thomas Meunier free from Lille (right-back) and Juan Riquelme Angulo Caicedo from Independiente del Valle, fee undisclosed (striker). Out, twenty players: Eliezer Mayenda to Rennes, £18.9m, Anthony Patterson to Wrexham, £8m, Lutsharel Geertruida, Dan Neil, Bertrand Traoré, Dennis Cirkin and Arthur Masuaku all free, Leo Hjelde to Mechelen, Timothée Pembelé to Le Havre, Simon Adingra on loan to Ajax, and ten more. Roughly £58.3m spent against £26.9m received. No centre-back and no goalkeeper was signed, while a centre-back, two left-backs, a right-back and a goalkeeper all left — the defensive unit is thinner in bodies than it was, in a season with a European fixture list attached.
  • Suspensions / absences. Both clubs are missing a player to suspension, and City’s is the more consequential: Phil Foden begins a three-match ban after a red card at Old Trafford last weekend. Sunderland are without Reinildo, suspended, and Habib Diarra and Romaine Mundle, both injured. Diarra and Mundle were both part of the £161m promotion window rather than this one. City’s wider injury position is reported as four out and one doubt in the club and press updates, though the list is not itemised in the sources reached and is not filled in here. Two of the three named absences — Foden and Reinildo — are suspensions, which is the one category of absence that is certain rather than doubtful.
  • Sample. Manchester City: seven competitive matches — Premier League 2–1 v Bournemouth (23 August), 4–1 at Crystal Palace (28 August), 1–0 v Coventry (5 September), 1–0 at Manchester United (13 September); Champions League 2–0 at Porto (8 September); EFL Cup 5–0 v Norwich (17 September); Community Shield 0–3 v Arsenal (16 August). Top of the league on 12 points from four wins, 8 scored and 2 conceded — and the shape of those wins has narrowed: 2–1 and 4–1, then 1–0 and 1–0. Sunderland: six competitive matches — Premier League 1–2 at Ipswich (22 August), 1–0 v Fulham (30 August), 1–1 at Brentford (5 September), 0–2 v Arsenal (12 September); EFL Cup 1–0 v Hull (8 September); Europa League 1–0 v AZ Alkmaar (16 September). Fourteenth on 4 points, 3 scored and 5 conceded, and every one of their six matches has finished with one goal or fewer for them. Sunderland have scored exactly one goal in five of six and none in the other.
  • Referee. Robert Jones, confirmed. Across 18 Premier League matches in 2025–26: 4.17 cards per match (75 yellows, no reds), 21.1 fouls per match, 5.1 fouls per card, and 0.28 penalties per match. Against the division that is a strict reading on the booking axis and an average one on penalties: 4.17 cards against a divisional 3.86, and 5.1 fouls per card against 5.60 — the shortest leash of any official appointed on this slate, booking roughly one foul in five — with 0.28 penalties against a divisional 0.24. Notably he issued no red card at all in 18 matches, so the card volume is entirely yellow. This season he has taken two matches at 4.00 cards and 32.5 fouls per match, 8.1 fouls per card and no penalty — the card rate reproducing, the fouls-per-card figure not, on two matches. History with both clubs and a lot of it, weighted to Sunderland: four Sunderland matches last season (3–0 v West Ham, 2–0 v Wolves, 2–1 v Crystal Palace, 1–0 v Tottenham — all four at the Stadium of Light, all four won) and three Manchester City matches (5–1 v Burnley, 1–2 at Nottingham Forest, 2–2 at Tottenham). No penalty was awarded in any of those seven.
  • Not sourced. Manchester City’s full injury list is not itemised in any source reached — the club and press updates report four ruled out and one doubt beyond Foden’s suspension, but do not name them consistently, and no names are invented here. The fees for Gerónimo Rulli, Angulo Caicedo and several of Sunderland’s twenty departures are undisclosed and are not estimated. No source reached characterises in words how Robert Jones reads contact in the box; the 0.28 penalties per match is counted from 18 matches, but the description the spec asks for is not published. Everything else is published: the Premier League carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data per match, the official for every fixture, and complete fees.

B · Style & early-season reality

City under Maresca are pressing harder and finishing with fewer goals in the same matches, and both halves of that are measurable. PPDA has fallen from 10.72 to 8.17 — a 24% increase in pressing intensity — on possession that has risen from 60.5% to 65.3%, the highest share of any club carded on this slate. The xG line is almost identical to Guardiola’s last season, 2.09 for in both, with xG conceded improved from 1.22 to 1.02. What has moved is everything about when and how the goals arrive. Shots are down from 15.63 to 13.00 per match and deep completions from 10.76 to 8.25, while first-half scoring has more than halved, from 1.08 to 0.50 per match. The four wins tell the same story in sequence: 2–1 and 4–1 to open, then 1–0 and 1–0. A side with the same expected output, a higher possession share, fewer shots and half the first-half goals is converting its dominance later and more narrowly than the record that produced 77 goals. The unit doing it is new — £361m of central midfield in, Rodri and Bernardo Silva out — and behind it a defence that lost Stones, Akanji and Aké and signed no replacement.

Sunderland have produced one of the larger jumps in underlying numbers on this slate, and the goals have not followed. xG has risen from 1.14 to 1.74 per match, PPDA from 11.95 to 9.73, deep completions from 5.58 to 7.25 and shots from 10.42 to 12.25 — four independent series, all moving hard in the same direction, under an unchanged coach with a £25.7m winger and a £24m left-back added. The output is 0.75 goals per match, down from 1.11. Two caveats are visible in the same data and both belong on the card: non-penalty xG is 1.36 against a total of 1.74, so about a fifth of the increase is penalties, and the sample is four matches against Ipswich, Fulham, Brentford and Arsenal. The defensive side has barely moved (1.59 to 1.55 xG conceded, 1.26 to 1.25 goals). Fouls have risen from 10.71 to 12.75 per match and cards from 2.24 to 2.50, which is what the PPDA change predicts — a side pressing two points higher up commits more of them. On corners they are the quietest team in this fixture at 3.75 won, among the lowest carded on this slate, and were 3.68 last season.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
A four-from-four home side against a side on four points looks like the least ambiguous fixture on the slate and is not. City have a coach four league matches into his first season, £361m of new central midfield, no centre-back signed after three left, and their best attacking player starting a three-match ban. Sunderland’s record says 4 points and their xG says 1.74 per match, the second-highest on this card. The division’s home-win rate has fallen to 37.0% from 42.6%. The 2025–26 records that would separate these clubs — 78 points against 54 — were produced by a different manager and a squad that lost twenty-nine and twenty players respectively.
Asian handicap / DNBSplit — the margin trend is readable, the level is not
Unusually for this slate there is a directional signal in the margins, and it belongs to the home side. City’s four league margins run +1, +3, +1, +1, and the narrowing is corroborated rather than isolated: shots down 15.63→13.00, deep completions 10.76→8.25, first-half goals 1.08→0.50, on identical xG. Three of four wins by exactly one goal, from a side that won 23 matches last season. Sunderland’s margins are −1, +1, 0, −2 — three of four inside a goal. What is not readable is where to set the line: City’s xG advantage over Sunderland is 2.09 to 1.74, the narrowest gap those two clubs’ underlying numbers have shown, and Foden’s absence is a removal from the side doing the scoring.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — the counted rates and the modelled rates disagree, which is itself informative
The two series point in opposite directions and both are sourced. Counted: these are two of the lower-scoring fixtures profiles in the division — Sunderland 25.0% over 2.5 this season and 44.7% last, City 50.0% and 63.2%, and the two clubs have produced 11 goals in eight league matches between them. Modelled: City 2.09 xG and Sunderland 1.74, a combined 3.83 per match against a divisional 3.26. The gap between those two readings is the finding. It is explained on City’s side by conversion narrowing (four wins, three by one goal, on unchanged xG) and on Sunderland’s by a 0.75 goals-per-match return on 1.74 xG. Four matches each, and the division is at 2.83 against 2.75 — the most stable baseline of the five.
BTTSOK — both sides sit at exactly the divisional rate, for different reasons
Both clubs read 50.0% over four matches against a divisional 50.0%, which on its own says nothing; the components do. City have kept clean sheets in half their matches and have not failed to score in any (50.0%/0.0%), continuing 42.1%/10.5% last season — they are reliably on the scoresheet and half the time their opponent is not. Sunderland are the opposite shape: 25.0% clean sheets and 25.0% blanks this season, and a 34.2% failed-to-score rate last season — among the highest carded on this slate. A side that has scored exactly one goal in five of six competitive matches, with 22% of its expected output coming from penalties, is the variable. Readable as a structure, dependent on one thin attacking record.
Team goalsSplit — City’s volume is documented and falling, Sunderland’s is documented and unconverted
City’s attacking series is the best-documented on the card and it is moving: xG flat at 2.09, shots 15.63→13.00, deep completions 10.76→8.25, first-half goals 1.08→0.50, and Foden suspended for three matches — a stable expected figure arriving through a narrower channel with its best creator removed. Sunderland’s is the most striking mismatch on the slate: 1.74 xG against 0.75 goals, with 1.36 of that xG non-penalty, from a side that signed a £25.7m winger and sold its centre-forward for £18.9m. Both team-goal lines are readable in direction; neither is settled in level, and both rest on four matches.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Dangerous — though the first-half collapse is the single most interesting counted fact here
City scored 1.08 first-half goals per match last season and 0.50 this; they conceded 0.26 and now 0.25. Sunderland are at 0.25 and 0.25 against 0.34 and 0.61. The combined 1.25 first-half goals per match is the second-lowest of any fixture on this slate, behind only f14, against a divisional 1.26, and on City’s side it sits alongside a matching fall in shots and deep completions, which makes it more than noise. It is still four matches, it coincides exactly with a change of manager and a rebuilt midfield, and the first-half result inherits every 1X2 objection on a shorter sample. A real signal at the edge of what four matches can carry.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — a strict official meeting the two cleanest sets of numbers in the fixture
The official and the teams point in opposite directions and both are well evidenced. Robert Jones has the shortest leash of any referee on this slate: 5.1 fouls per card against a divisional 5.60 and 4.17 cards per match against 3.86, across 18 matches, with the card rate reproducing at 4.00 over two this season. Against that, City commit fewer fouls than any club carded on this slate — 9.50 per match, against a divisional 11.40 — and take 1.75 cards, and they were at 9.68 and 1.76 last season under a different manager, so the figure is method rather than personnel. Sunderland are the live side: fouls up from 10.71 to 12.75 and cards from 2.24 to 2.50, with PPDA down from 11.95 to 9.73 to explain it, and Reinildo already suspended. In the seven previous matches Jones took involving these two clubs, no penalty was awarded in any of them.
CornersOK — asymmetric, and the asymmetry has held for two seasons
This is the one series on the card where both clubs have been stable across the manager change and the two are far apart. City win 6.25 corners per match and concede 4.00, against 6.50 and 3.82 last season — four figures within a quarter-corner of each other across two seasons and two managers. Sunderland win 3.75 and concede 3.50, against 3.68 and 5.16 — among the lowest corners-won figures on this slate, in both seasons. The divisional line is 4.68. A 6.25-against-3.75 split with 42 matches of consistency behind each side is as close to a settled structural reading as this card offers, and the mechanism is on the page: 65.3% possession and 8.25 deep completions against 49.4% and 7.25.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • City’s 2025–26 record belongs to Guardiola and to a midfield that has been sold. Rodri, Bernardo Silva and Reijnders left; £361m of central midfielders arrived; the club record was broken twice. The xG figure (2.09 in both seasons) looks like continuity and is being produced by four new players under a coach in his first season.
  • Three senior centre-backs left the Etihad and none was bought. Stones free, Akanji for £13m, Aké out; the goalkeeper was replaced (Trafford £40m out, Rulli in). The 0.50 goals-conceded rate over four matches is the only evidence for how that unit holds up, and two of the four were against Coventry and Crystal Palace.
  • Sunderland’s underlying jump is the biggest on the slate and its output is the smallest. xG 1.14→1.74, PPDA 11.95→9.73, shots 10.42→12.25, deep 5.58→7.25 — and 0.75 goals per match, one goal or fewer in all six competitive matches. About 22% of the xG is penalties. Four matches cannot separate a genuine step up from a fast start against a favourable set of opponents.
  • Two certain absences, both by suspension, and one of them is City’s best attacker. Foden begins a three-match ban; Reinildo is out for Sunderland. Unlike injuries these are not doubts, so they are the one part of the team news that can be taken as settled — and City’s wider injury list is not itemised in any source reached.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, the first-half result and any line resting on City’s attacking level — the scoring has halved before the break and narrowed to 1–0s while the xG has not moved, and the player most likely to resolve that ambiguity is banned. Least sensitive: Corners — a 6.25-against-3.75 split that both clubs have reproduced across two seasons and a change of manager, with possession and deep-completion figures to explain it.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because it is the only series that survived both clubs’ summers unchanged. City 6.50→6.25 won and 3.82→4.00 conceded through a change of manager and £456m of trading; Sunderland 3.68→3.75 won through twenty departures. The result records over the same period describe a squad that sold Rodri and one that sold Mayenda.
  • The Asian handicap reads better than the outright result because the margin has a documented direction and the winner does not. City’s +1, +3, +1, +1 sits with shots 15.63→13.00 and first-half goals 1.08→0.50 — three series narrowing together. Who wins is a question about a first-season coach, a new midfield and a suspended Foden.
  • Totals is readable only if the disagreement between counted and modelled goals is treated as the finding rather than resolved. Eleven goals in eight league matches says one thing; a combined 3.83 xG, well above the divisional 3.26, says another. Both are counted from the same 46-match divisional record and neither is a projection.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and here the split runs the opposite way to most cards on this slate. Sunderland’s 10.71→12.75 fouls has PPDA 11.95→9.73 behind it, and Robert Jones converts fouls to cards faster than anyone appointed today (5.1 per card). City’s 9.50 fouls is the lowest figure on the slate and has held across two managers, so the two sides of this fixture give the same official opposite material.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Sunderland’s 1.74 xG is a level or a run. It is the largest underlying jump on the slate, it has produced 0.75 goals per match, and a fifth of it is penalties — in front of an official who awarded none in seven previous matches involving these clubs. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth league match of Understat data, or a published breakdown of Sunderland’s open-play chance quality; neither exists yet at four matches.

Italy · Serie A · Giornata 5

Frosinone – Como

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Frosinone are promoted and are back in Serie A after two seasons away — they were relegated from the top flight in 2023–24 and went up as runners-up in Serie B on 81 points from 38 matches, one behind Venezia, who are also in this division now, with Monza third. Across that campaign they were 23-12-3 with 76 scored and 34 conceded — only three defeats in 38. Como finished 4th in Serie A on 71 points — 20 wins, 11 draws, 7 defeats, 65 scored and 29 conceded, the meanest defence in the division — and are in the Champions League for the first time in their 119-year history. The structural headline is that both clubs kept the coach and then went in opposite financial directions. Massimiliano Alvini stays at Frosinone, the coach who won the promotion, and recruited breadth rather than money — fourteen or more arrivals, none of them at a headline fee. Cesc Fàbregas stays at Como, and the club spent at a scale no promoted side could match: €60m on Nico Paz permanently from Real Madrid, €30m plus bonuses on Trevoh Chalobah, and a €10m loan for Moise Kean carrying a guaranteed €25m obligation and €5m in bonuses. Both clubs’ last-season records therefore belong to the men picking the team — the rarest thing on this slate — but one of those records was made in a different division and the other by a squad that has since been rebuilt upwards.

  • Frosinone — turnover very high in bodies, low in money; no managerial change, Alvini continuing. Goalkeeper: in — Sebastiano Desplanches from Palermo. Centre-back, where the recruitment was heaviest: in — Kevin Akpoguma free, with Bundesliga experience, Giorgio Cittadini from Atalanta, Nicolò Calvani from Genoa and Fayed from Fenerbahçe. Defensive midfield: in — Florian Grillitsch from Braga and Romano Schmid from Werder Bremen, the two most experienced arrivals. Centre-forward and attack: in — Antonio Raimondo from Bologna, Alessio Zerbin from Napoli, €2m, and Fini from Genoa. Midfield: in — Luis Hasa from Napoli, €2.5m, El Azzouzi from Düsseldorf, Masini from Genoa. Out: Marchizza to Vicenza, Ndow to Spezia, Dixon to Toronto, Masciangelo, Vergani, and loan returns for Fiori (Mantova), Zilli (Cosenza) and Gori (Grosseto). Two of the arrivals, Hasa and Zerbin, came from Napoli, who also play on this slate. The window kept the promotion core and added a goalkeeper, four centre-backs and two defensive midfielders — precisely the positions the spec weights, and all of them at small or undisclosed fees.
  • Como — turnover high and expensive, aimed at the forward line and the centre of defence; no managerial change, Fàbregas continuing. Centre-forward: in — Moise Kean, a €10m loan with a guaranteed obligation to buy at €25m plus €5m in bonuses, on a contract to June 2031, joining Douvikas. Centre-back: in — Trevoh Chalobah permanently from Chelsea, €30m plus bonuses. Attacking midfield: Nico Paz made permanent from Real Madrid for €60m — the largest single fee on this card — and Mattia Liberali for €6m. Loans with options: Yan Couto, Kaiki, Samuele Ricci and Willy Kambwala, all on paid loans with purchase rights. Roughly €100m of permanent fees plus a €30m committed obligation, and a reported ten players moved out as surplus. A side that conceded 29 goals last season has bought a €30m centre-back and a €35m striker on top of it, for a Champions League season it has never played before.
  • Suspensions / absences. Neither club has a suspension. Frosinone are missing two, and one of them is the most experienced signing of their window: Florian Grillitsch and Aleksa Terzić are both out with physical problems. Grillitsch is a defensive midfielder brought in specifically to hold a promoted midfield in Serie A, and the probable eleven replaces him with Cichella. Como are missing one, Addai. This is the lightest absence list on the slate so far, and it means both cards below rest on close to full-strength sides — the rare fixture here where team news is not the dominant uncertainty.
  • Sample. Frosinone: six competitive matches — Serie A 0–1 v Juventus (23 August), 3–0 at Fiorentina (29 August), 3–2 v Venezia (6 September), 1–1 at Genoa (12 September); Coppa Italia 4–1 v Juve Stabia (16 August) and 1–1 at Sassuolo (2 September). Seventh on 7 points, 7 scored and 4 conceded — the best start by any promoted club in the division. Como: five competitive matches — Serie A 1–1 at Udinese (22 August), 2–1 at Napoli (30 August), 4–1 at Genoa (4 September), 2–1 v Parma (14 September); Champions League 4–1 v RB Leipzig (10 September), whose opponents today — Leverkusen — also play on this slate. Fifth on 10 points, unbeaten, 9 scored and 4 conceded, and only one of their four league matches has been at home. Como have played three of four on the road and won two of them.
  • Referee. Daniele Chiffi of the Padova section, confirmed in the AIA designations published on 16 September. Across 15 Serie A matches in 2025–26: 2.60 cards per match (36 yellows, 3 reds), 22.7 fouls per match, 8.7 fouls per card, and 0.27 penalties per match. Against the division he is the most permissive official appointed anywhere on this slate: 2.60 cards against a 2025–26 Serie A rate of 3.77 and a 2026–27 rate of 3.22, and 8.7 fouls per card against a divisional 6.72 — he lets roughly a third more play run between bookings than the average Serie A referee. His penalty rate is close to divisional, 0.27 against 0.28. This season he has taken one match, which produced 2 cards in 17 fouls — 8.5 fouls per card, reproducing the ratio exactly on a sample of one. History: one Como match in the record, a 0–0 at Parma in October 2025 with a single card in the whole game. No Frosinone match, because Frosinone were in Serie B and Chiffi’s record here is a Serie A record.
  • Not sourced. No 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion figure exists for Frosinone, because Understat does not cover Serie B; half of this card’s xG comparison is missing rather than filled, and nothing is estimated. Fees are undisclosed for most of Frosinone’s fourteen arrivals — Desplanches, Akpoguma, Cittadini, Calvani, Fayed, Grillitsch, Schmid, Raimondo, El Azzouzi, Masini — and none is inferred; only Hasa (€2.5m) and Zerbin (€2m) are published. Loan fees for Como’s Couto, Kaiki, Ricci and Kambwala are not published either. No source reached characterises in words how Chiffi reads contact in the box. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division.

B · Style & early-season reality

Frosinone have arrived in Serie A and chosen the most physically demanding way to survive in it. Their PPDA is 7.39 — the second-most aggressive press on this slate, behind Liverpool at 7.06 — on 43.1% possession, meaning they harry constantly without the ball and rarely keep it. The cost is visible in two columns and both are extreme. They commit 16.50 fouls per match against a divisional 11.21, the highest figure of any team on this board, and they concede an xG of 2.18 per match, also the highest, against a divisional 1.71. What makes the record look better than the underlying numbers is the finishing at both ends: 7 scored and 4 conceded from 1.46 xG for and 2.18 against. In Serie B they were a possession-neutral side (49.6%) who out-cornered opponents 6.16 to 4.13; in Serie A that has inverted, 5.50 won against 7.50 conceded — the worst corner concession rate on the board. Their fouls (15.79 in Serie B against a second-tier line of 14.98) were already above average in a rougher division and have risen again here. There is no 2025–26 xG to set against the current 1.46/2.18, so the only modelled evidence about this club is four matches old.

Como are doing something unusual for a side that finished fourth: they have become more dominant and less aggressive at the same time. Shots have risen from 14.45 to 22.25 per match and deep completions from 6.37 to 11.25 — both nearly doubled — while xG has gone from 1.79 to 2.32, among the six highest attacking figures on this board. Yet PPDA has risen from 7.71 to 11.20: they press considerably less, on essentially unchanged possession (61.6% to 60.8%). A side pressing less while creating more is holding the ball in the opponent’s half rather than winning it back high, and the corner column confirms it — 8.00 won against 3.00 conceded, an eight-corner-per-match attacking rate against a divisional 4.78. The discipline consequence is the cleanest set of numbers on the slate: 7.75 fouls and 0.50 cards per match, against 13.42 and 2.16 last season — a side that does not need to foul because it does not need to chase. The defensive record has loosened in exchange: xG conceded 1.04 to 1.41, goals 0.76 to 1.00, and both teams have scored in all four league matches after 42.1% last season. The 4–1 against Leipzig in midweek is the same profile at a higher level.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Frosinone’s entire 2025–26 record was made in Serie B, where they lost three matches in thirty-eight; nothing in it is comparable to this division, and no xG exists for it at all. Como’s record is a Serie A record but belongs to a squad that has since added Kean, Chalobah and a permanent Nico Paz for roughly €125m in commitments. Four league matches each, an unbeaten away side that has played three of four on the road, and a promoted home side on 7 points from 1.46 xG. Serie A’s home-win rate is 40.0% against 38.9%. The result is the least evidenced thing here.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Both sides’ margins are narrow and both sides’ underlying figures say the margins should not be. Frosinone’s four are −1, +3, +1, 0 from an xG line of 1.46 for and 2.18 against; Como’s are 0, +1, +3, +1 from 2.32 and 1.41. On expected goals Como out-create Frosinone by 0.86 per match, the second-widest gap on this board, while on actual results the two clubs are separated by three points. A handicap has to price a gap that the counted record and the modelled record disagree about by nearly a goal, on four matches, with one side’s prior season played in a different division.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — the highest combined expected output on the slate, with the counted record agreeing
Both readings point the same way for once, and both are extreme. Combined xG is 3.78 per match — Como 2.32 for and Frosinone 2.18 against, and Frosinone 1.46 against Como’s 1.41 — against a divisional 3.42 for two average teams. Counted: Como have gone over 2.5 in 75% of four matches and scored 2.25 per match; Frosinone have produced 11 goals in four (7 for, 4 against) and 50%. The mechanism is on the page rather than assumed: Como take 22.25 shots and complete 11.25 passes deep per match, Frosinone press at 7.39 PPDA with 43.1% of the ball and concede the highest xG on the board. The division is at 2.96 against 2.43 last season. The caveat is four matches each and no Frosinone xG history.
BTTSWell-suited — Como’s side of it is the firmest single fact on this card
Both teams have scored in all four of Como’s league matches, and Como have neither kept a clean sheet nor failed to score — 0.0% and 0.0%. That is a complete reversal of last season, when they kept clean sheets in 50% of matches and had the division’s meanest defence, and the reversal has a stated cause in section B: PPDA up from 7.71 to 11.20 and xG conceded from 1.04 to 1.41 while the attack nearly doubled its volume. On the other side, Frosinone have scored in three of four and concede 2.18 xG per match, having failed to score in only 7.9% of 38 matches in Serie B. The divisional BTTS rate is 60.0% against 45.3% last season — the largest move of the five leagues. Four independent facts, all pointing one way.
Team goalsSplit — Como’s volume is exceptionally well evidenced, Frosinone’s is not
Como’s attacking series is the strongest on this board: 22.25 shots and 7.00 on target per match against 14.45 and 5.11 last season, 11.25 deep completions against 6.37, 2.32 xG against 1.79, and 8.00 corners won against 4.53 — five columns, all moving hard in the same direction, with €125m of forward and attacking-midfield commitment behind them and a 4–1 against Leipzig to show at a higher level. Frosinone are the opposite case: 1.75 goals per match from 1.46 xG, no 2025–26 modelled record to compare it to, and their most experienced midfield signing injured. One team-goals line here is about as evidenced as this board gets; the other rests on four matches and a second-tier history.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited on first-half goals — the most extreme divisional gap on the slate
Frosinone score 1.25 and concede 0.25 before the break; Como score 1.50 and concede 0.75 — a combined 3.75 first-half goals per match against a divisional 1.16. That is more than three times the league rate and it is not one match: Frosinone have led at half-time in three of four (2–0 at Fiorentina, 2–0 v Venezia, 1–0 at Genoa) and Como in three of four (2–1 at Napoli, 3–1 at Genoa, 1–0 v Parma). Both figures are also up sharply on their own prior seasons (Frosinone 0.89 in Serie B, Como 0.71). The first-half result is a different and much weaker proposition, inheriting every 1X2 objection on a shorter sample. Eight matches, one direction, a gap this wide against the baseline is unusual enough to be readable.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — the widest foul gap on the board, pointing into the most lenient whistle on it
The two sides give this official completely opposite material and the official is the most permissive appointed today. Frosinone commit 16.50 fouls per match — the highest figure on this board, against a divisional 11.21 — with a 7.39 PPDA to explain it, and 15.79 in Serie B before that. Como commit 7.75, the third-lowest figure on this board, down from 13.42 last season, with PPDA up from 7.71 to 11.20 to explain that. Chiffi books 2.60 per match against a divisional 3.77 last season and 3.22 this, and allows 8.7 fouls per card against 6.72 — a third more play between bookings than average, reproduced at 8.5 over his one match this season. The foul count is structurally established on both sides and asymmetric; the conversion into cards runs through an official who converts more slowly than anyone else on the slate.
CornersWell-suited — asymmetric, large, and mechanically explained on both sides
This is the widest corner asymmetry on the board and both halves have a cause on the page. Como win 8.00 corners and concede 3.00; Frosinone win 5.50 and concede 7.50 — against a divisional 4.78. Como’s figure sits on 22.25 shots and 11.25 deep completions per match at 60.8% possession, nearly double last season’s territorial volume; Frosinone’s concession sits on 43.1% possession and the highest xG conceded on the board. The one caution is that both series have moved a long way from their own prior seasons — Como from 4.53 won, Frosinone from 6.16 won in Serie B — so neither has 38 matches behind it in its current form. Four matches, but four matches where the corner count, the shot count, the deep-completion count and the possession share all agree.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Frosinone’s entire 2025–26 record is a Serie B record and its modelled half does not exist. 2.00 goals per match, 60.5% over 2.5 and three defeats in thirty-eight were produced in a division scoring 2.54 per game and booking at 4.52. Understat does not cover Serie B, so there is no xG, PPDA or deep-completion baseline for this club at all — four Serie A matches are the only modelled evidence that exists.
  • Como’s record looks continuous and its squad is not. Fourth place and 29 conceded were achieved before Kean (€35m committed), Chalobah (€30m) and a permanent Nico Paz (€60m) arrived. The clean-sheet rate has already gone from 50.0% to 0.0% and xG conceded from 1.04 to 1.41 — the part of the side that produced last season’s headline number is the part that has changed most in behaviour.
  • Como have played three of four league matches away and won two of them; this is only their second home fixture’s worth of evidence in reverse. The 22.25 shots and 8.00 corners were accumulated mostly on the road, which normally suppresses both. Whether the figure is a venue artefact cannot be separated at four matches.
  • Frosinone’s most experienced signing is unavailable in the position his signing was meant to solve. Grillitsch was brought from Braga as a defensive midfielder for a promoted side pressing at 7.39 PPDA; he and Terzić are out. The 16.50 fouls per match is what that midfield does at full strength.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and anything resting on Frosinone’s attacking level — a promoted side on 7 points from 1.46 xG, with no prior modelled record, is the definition of an unsettled input. Least sensitive: Corners and first-half goals — both sit on gaps far larger than their own variance, with the shot, possession and deep-completion columns agreeing on the mechanism.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because the asymmetry is four corners per match wide and has three other series behind it. Como’s 8.00 won against Frosinone’s 7.50 conceded sits on 22.25 shots, 11.25 deep completions and 60.8% possession against 43.1%. The result, by contrast, rests on a Serie B season with no xG and a Serie A season played by a squad that has since spent €125m.
  • Totals is more robust than Team goals on the Frosinone side because the combined figure survives the missing half of the evidence. Frosinone’s 2.18 xG conceded and Como’s 2.32 xG for are both counted from this season; the total does not need Frosinone’s attacking history, which does not exist. A Frosinone team-goals line does need it.
  • BTTS is more robust than the clean-sheet lines because it is carried by the side whose change is explained. Como have gone from 50.0% clean sheets to 0.0% with PPDA 7.71→11.20 and xG conceded 1.04→1.41 to say why; a clean-sheet line asks which of two sides stops conceding, and neither has stopped in four matches.
  • The foul count is more robust than the card count, and the referee is the reason. Frosinone’s 16.50 and Como’s 7.75 are the two most extreme foul figures on this board and both have pressing data behind them. Chiffi converts at 8.7 fouls per card against a divisional 6.72 and books 2.60 per match against 3.22 this season — the same fouls produce fewer cards in front of him than in front of anyone else appointed today.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Como’s 22.25 shots per match is a level or a road artefact. It is nearly double last season’s 14.45, it was accumulated in three away matches and one at home, and it is the number underneath the corner reading, the totals reading and the team-goals reading simultaneously. What would settle it is two or three more home matches of shot and deep-completion data; at four matches, with three of them away, the venue and the change cannot be separated.
