A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Parma finished 13th in 2025–26 on 45 points — 11 wins, 12 draws, 15 defeats, 28 scored, 46 conceded — and the 28 goals is the joint-fewest of any club carded on this slate, alongside Lecce. They went over 2.5 in 28.9% of matches against a divisional 45.8%. Genoa finished 16th on 41 points — 10 wins, 11 draws, 17 defeats, 41 scored, 51 conceded. The structural headline is that there isn’t one, and that is itself the finding: this is the only fixture on the whole slate where neither club changed head coach and neither is promoted. Carlos Cuesta continues at Parma, into his second season, having been appointed in 2025 as the youngest head coach in Serie A history when Cristian Chivu left for Inter; Daniele De Rossi continues at Genoa, whose unchanged status this board recorded on 4 September (fx_12_f3) as “the only fixture on that card where neither club changed manager”. That absence of a separator is precisely why triage scored this fixture zero for structural distinctiveness and placed it out of the researched group. With the tier overridden, what the research finds instead is two clubs whose scoring records are among the lowest on the board and whose summers left them largely as they were.

  • Parma — turnover medium, no managerial change; Cuesta into his second season. The 2026 window, as far as it could be established: in — Hans Nicolussi Caviglia from Venezia, Benjamin Cremaschi from Inter Miami, Roberto Scaglione from Genoa (today’s opponent) and Christian Ordoñez. Out, the headline: Mateo Pellegrino to Fiorentina for €25m plus €5m in add-ons — recorded from the buying club’s side on card f1, and by some distance the largest fee in this fixture. A club that scored 28 goals in a season sold its centre-forward for €30m and did not replace him with a comparable fee. Gap: the sources reached for Parma mix the 2025 and 2026 windows, and several arrivals named in them (Bonny to Inter, Leoni to Liverpool) belong to summer 2025, not this one; only the moves that could be dated to 2026 are listed above, and the rest are excluded rather than reported uncertainly. Leoni, sold to Liverpool, is currently injured there and appears on card f4.
  • Genoa — turnover high in bodies, no managerial change; De Rossi continuing. The fullest account reached is this board’s own from 15 September (fx_0915_f1), which records a sold centre-forward and a dozen summer arrivals, and from 4 September (fx_12_f3), which records a window characterised as a long list of arrivals without a headline fee, set against Como’s €35m Kean deal in the same card. Out, established here: Roberto Scaglione to Parma, today’s opponent. No itemised 2026 fee list for Genoa was obtainable from any source reached, so the named-with-fees requirement is not satisfied for this club and nothing is estimated. What is established is that the recruitment was broad and cheap and the coach is the same one who finished 16th.
  • Suspensions / absences. Genoa are without Johan Vásquez, suspended — a centre-back, in a back three the probable lineup rebuilds around Marcandalli, Ostigard and Otoa in front of Bijlow. Parma’s Adrián Bernabé is a doubt and the sources reached contradict each other on him: one published preview lists him as out, another reports confidence that he starts, with Sierro named as the alternative. Both readings are reported; neither is preferred, and no other absentee is named for Parma in anything reached. Parma’s probable shape is a 3-4-2-1 with Delprato, Diego Carlos and Troilo behind Britschgi, Keita and Valeri, and Lontani and El Bilal Touré supporting Romero.
  • Sample. Parma: six competitive matches — Serie A 0–1 v Cagliari (22 August), 0–2 at Juventus (29 August) — Juventus play later today — 1–1 v Monza (6 September), 1–2 at Como (14 September), Como also on this slate; Coppa Italia 2–0 v Catania (14 August) and 0–2 v Cremonese (1 September). Eighteenth on 1 point from four league matches, 2 scored and 6 conceded, no win in any competition since 14 August, and they are still looking for their first Serie A victory of the season. Genoa: six competitive matches — Serie A 0–2 v Napoli (22 August), 0–1 at Lazio (30 August), 1–4 v Como (4 September), 1–1 v Frosinone (12 September) — three of those four opponents play on this slate; Coppa Italia 4–1 v Ascoli (16 August) and 1–0 v Südtirol (15 September). Nineteenth on 1 point, 2 scored and 8 conceded, and three of their four league matches were at home. Between them these two clubs have four goals and two points from eight league matches.
  • Referee. Maurizio Mariani of the Aprilia section, confirmed in the AIA designations published on 16 September. Across 15 Serie A matches in 2025–26: 3.87 cards per match (57 yellows, 1 red), 24.1 fouls per match, 6.2 fouls per card, and 0.40 penalties per match. Against the division that is close to average on booking — 3.87 cards against 3.77, 6.2 fouls per card against 6.72, so a marginally shorter leash — and notably high on penalties: 0.40 against a divisional 0.28 last season and 0.11 this season, the second-highest rate of any official appointed on this slate. This season he has taken one match, Juventus 1–1 Milan on 6 September, with 4 cards in 21 fouls. History with both clubs: Parma 1–1 Atalanta in August 2025 with four cards, and AC Milan 1–1 Genoa in January 2026 with five cards and a penalty. One match each, both draws, both with a card count close to his average.
  • Not sourced. No itemised 2026 transfer ledger with fees exists for Genoa in anything reached, and the Parma sources reached conflate the 2025 and 2026 windows, so only the moves that could be dated confidently are stated and the rest are omitted rather than reported as uncertain — the transfer half of the spec is partly satisfied on one side and thin on the other. The Bernabé contradiction above is unresolved. No Parma injury list beyond that doubt was obtainable. No source characterises in words how Mariani reads contact in the box; the 0.40 penalties per match is counted from 15 matches. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division.

B · Style & early-season reality

Parma have taken a low-scoring identity and made it more extreme in every column. Possession has fallen from 44.0% to 42.9%, PPDA has loosened from 13.33 to 15.22, shots are down from 10.71 to 9.00 and xG from 0.95 to 0.85 — among the six lowest attacking figures on this board. The one series that has risen is deep completions, 3.63 to 5.75, which has produced nothing: two goals in four league matches, none of them before half-time. The first-half figure is the card’s signature and it is not a four-match artefact: 0.00 first-half goals this season, and 0.26 per match across thirty-eight last season, which is the lowest first-half scoring rate of any club on this board across a full campaign. The defensive side has deteriorated sharply on the model — xG conceded from 1.63 to 2.24, the third-worst on this board — while the counted rate has held at 1.50. They sold a €30m centre-forward and have scored twice.

Genoa are the mirror image with the damage at the other end. Possession 45.2% against 48.7%, PPDA loosened from 10.05 to 13.59 — a 35% drop in pressing intensity, among the larger proportional loosenings on this board — and shots roughly flat at 11.75 against 11.45. The attacking model has fallen from 1.27 to 1.00 xG, with non-penalty xG at 0.81, and shots on target have collapsed from 3.76 to 2.50 per match, among the five lowest figures on this board. The concession has risen from 1.34 to 2.00 goals on an xG against of 1.76. Their first-half concession is the more striking number: 1.25 per match against 0.61 last season, and they have trailed at half-time in three of four. Both clubs now read 0.0% clean sheets and 50.0% failed-to-score — identical on both halves of that column, which is an unusual coincidence and the clearest single statement of what this fixture is. Neither has kept a clean sheet; both have been blanked twice.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Eighteenth against nineteenth, one point each, four goals between them in eight league matches. The usual objection on this slate — a new coach — does not apply here, which removes one source of noise and supplies no signal in its place: both clubs have the same coach and roughly the same squad as the sides that finished 13th and 16th, and both are performing worse than those records. Serie A’s home-win rate is 40.0% against 38.9%. Last season these two drew 0–0 twice, home and away, without a goal in either. Two sides that cannot score, cannot keep a clean sheet and have one point each is a result no series on this card speaks to.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Parma’s four margins are −1, −2, 0, −1; Genoa’s are −2, −1, −3, 0. Between them, seven of eight league matches have been lost or drawn and neither club has produced a positive margin all season. The two previous meetings in the record both finished 0–0. Neither side has won a league match, so a handicap has to price the gap between two teams with no wins and a combined goal difference of −10, on four matches each, where the models differ by 0.15 xG.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — every counted and modelled series agrees, and one has two seasons behind it
This is the strongest reading on the card by a distance. Both clubs have gone over 2.5 in 25.0% of four matches against a divisional 55.6% — matched only by Nice and Lille on this board, and bettered only by Getafe’s 16.7% — and their eight league matches have produced 12 goals in total. Parma’s 28.9% last season, across thirty-eight matches against a divisional 45.8%, is the single most durable low-scoring rate on this slate. The models agree: combined xG 1.85 per match, against a divisional 3.42 for two average sides — the lowest combined attacking figure of any fixture on this board. Both clubs’ shots on target are 3.00 and 2.50 per match, both among the five lowest here. The one counterweight is stated: Serie A has gone from 2.43 goals a game to 2.96 and both defensive xG figures are poor (2.24 and 1.76), so the concession side pulls the other way.
BTTSSplit — both clubs concede reliably and neither scores reliably
The two sides are identical on this column and that is the problem as much as the evidence. Both read 50.0% BTTS, 0.0% clean sheets and 50.0% failed to score over four matches. The conceding half is firm: neither has kept a clean sheet in any league match, Parma concede 2.24 xG and Genoa 1.76. The scoring half is the weakest on this board: two goals each in four matches, 0.85 and 1.00 xG, 3.00 and 2.50 shots on target per match, and Parma have sold their centre-forward. Last season they were at 39.5% and 52.6% against a divisional 45.3%, so neither club has a strong prior either way. Both halves of the market are live for opposite reasons, which is what a split verdict means.
Team goalsDangerous
Neither attacking line has enough behind it to read. Parma: 0.50 goals per match from 0.85 xG on 9.00 shots and 3.00 on target — and 0.74 goals per match across thirty-eight matches last season. Genoa: 0.50 from 1.00 xG on 11.75 shots and 2.50 on target, among the five lowest on-target counts on this board. Both have failed to score in half their matches. Parma sold Mateo Pellegrino for €30m; Genoa’s centre-forward departure is recorded on this board’s earlier card without a fee. A team-goals line on either side is a line on whether a team that has scored twice in four matches scores once, with no volume trend to support it in either direction.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — Parma’s first-half record is the most durable figure here, Genoa’s the most volatile
Parma have not scored a first-half goal in four league matches, and scored 0.26 per match across thirty-eight last season — a two-season figure, not a four-match one, and the lowest sustained first-half scoring rate in this dataset. That is unusually well evidenced. Genoa’s side is the opposite: 1.25 first-half goals conceded against 0.61 last season, having trailed at the break in three of four — a doubling on four matches with a loosening press (PPDA 10.05→13.59) as the candidate cause. The combined 2.00 first-half goals per match sits above a divisional 1.16, but it is almost entirely Genoa’s concession. The first-half result is not readable: neither side has led at half-time in a league match this season.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — two quiet, falling foul counts meeting a slightly strict, penalty-prone official
The teams and the referee point in opposite directions and both are reasonably well evidenced. Both clubs foul well below the divisional rate and are falling: Parma 9.50 per match against 10.87 last season, Genoa 9.00 against 12.37 — a fall of more than three fouls a match — against a divisional 11.21. Card rates are 1.25 and 2.00 per team against a divisional 1.61. The division has itself fallen from 3.77 cards to 3.22. Mariani is the counterweight: 3.87 cards per match against a divisional 3.77, a 6.2 fouls-per-card leash against 6.72, and 4.00 cards in his one match this season. His penalty rate is the row’s live number — 0.40 per match against a divisional 0.11 this season — and Vásquez’s suspension means Genoa field a reshuffled back three in front of it.
CornersOK — a consistent two-season direction on both sides, without a large gap
This is one of the few columns on the board where both clubs have been stable across two seasons and both point the same way. Parma win 3.75 and concede 6.50 against 3.87 and 5.00 last season; Genoa win 5.00 and concede 5.50 against 3.68 and 4.89 — both negative differentials in both seasons, against a divisional 4.78. Parma’s concession has risen by a corner and a half with an xG conceded up from 1.63 to 2.24, which is consistent. The combined 12.00 corners conceded per match between them is among the highest on this board. What holds this at OK rather than higher is that neither club’s own figure is far from the divisional line, and the change that has happened is on the concession side, which is the harder half to attribute.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • This is the only fixture on the slate where neither club changed coach or was promoted, which cuts both ways. The 2025–26 records here are more comparable to the current side than anywhere else on the board — but both clubs are performing well below those records, and there is no structural event to explain why.
  • Parma sold their centre-forward for €30m and have scored twice in four matches. Mateo Pellegrino went to Fiorentina for €25m plus €5m; no comparable replacement is recorded in anything reached. A side that managed 28 goals with him is at 0.50 per match without him.
  • Genoa’s transfer ledger could not be itemised and Parma’s sources mix two windows. The only 2026 moves stated here are those that could be dated; the rest are omitted. That leaves the squad-change half of this card thinner than the counted half, on the one fixture where squad continuity is the premise.
  • Every 26/27 figure rests on four league matches, and three of Genoa’s four were at home. Their 2.00 goals-conceded rate and 1.25 first-half concession were accumulated mostly at the Ferraris, which normally suppresses both.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and both team-goals lines — two winless sides with four goals between them and no volume trend on either side. Least sensitive: Totals — the only reading here carried by a thirty-eight-match rate (Parma’s 28.9%) as well as a four-match one, with the two lowest combined xG figures on the board behind it.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than everything else on this card because it is the one family with a full-season rate behind it. Parma went over 2.5 in 28.9% of thirty-eight matches against a divisional 45.8%, and in 25.0% of four against 55.6%. The combined xG of 1.85 is the lowest on this board. The result, by contrast, has two winless sides and two 0–0 draws in the head-to-head record.
  • Parma’s first-half scoring is more robust than any other single number here, and it is a two-season fact. 0.00 goals before the break in four matches, 0.26 per match across thirty-eight. Genoa’s first-half concession, by contrast, has doubled on four matches.
  • The conceding half of BTTS is more robust than the scoring half, by a wide margin. Neither club has kept a clean sheet in any league match and both concede above 1.75 xG. Both have also been blanked twice in four, with the two lowest on-target counts on this board.
  • The penalty line is the live part of Discipline, not the card line. Mariani awards 0.40 penalties per match against a divisional 0.28 last season and 0.11 this — the second-highest rate on this slate — while both clubs’ foul counts have fallen by one to three per match and the division books a half-card less than last season.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Parma field Bernabé. The sources reached contradict each other on him, he is their creative midfielder, and this is a side with 0.85 xG, no first-half goal all season and no replacement for a €30m centre-forward. The confirmed eleven, published an hour before kickoff, settles it — and it is the only piece of team news on this card that is genuinely in doubt.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Leverkusen finished 6th in 2025–26 on 59 points — 17 wins, 8 draws, 9 defeats, 68 scored, 47 conceded — and missed the Champions League, which cost the manager his job. Leipzig finished 3rd on 65 points — 20 wins, 5 draws, 9 defeats, 66 scored, 47 conceded — which returned them to the Champions League, and cost their manager his job too. The structural headline is that both clubs sacked a coach who had just delivered a European place, three weeks apart, and that the two squads then produced almost identical underlying numbers in the first three matchdays. Carles Martínez Novell was appointed at Leverkusen on 4 June 2026 on a two-year contract to June 2028, starting work on 1 July, arriving from three seasons at Toulouse and replacing Kasper Hjulmand, who left after nine months and 48 matches (24 wins, 12 draws, 12 defeats). Martín Demichelis was appointed at Leipzig on 24 June 2026 on a contract to June 2028, Leipzig triggering a €2.5m release clause at Mallorca, replacing Ole Werner after a single season. Leverkusen are on their third head coach in roughly twelve months.

  • Correction to this board’s own archive. The card of 5 September (fx_13_f2) recorded that Leverkusen replaced Ole Werner with Carles Martínez, while the card of 10 September (fx_15_f4) recorded that Leipzig dismissed Ole Werner and appointed Demichelis. Both named the same departing coach at different clubs, and the conflict was flagged in triage_0920.md. It is now resolved from the Bundesliga’s own announcement: Martínez Novell replaced Kasper Hjulmand at Leverkusen, and Ole Werner was Leipzig’s coach. fx_13_f2 named the wrong predecessor; fx_15_f4 was right. Neither error changed a triage score, because Leverkusen’s change falls inside the window either way.
  • Leverkusen — turnover high and concentrated on both full-back positions and the wide attack; manager changed 4 June 2026. Out, the headline: Piero Hincapié to Arsenal, €40m — a centre-back, to a club that won the Premier League; Arsenal made a season-long loan permanent on 1 July 2026 for a reported £34.5m plus add-ons on a five-year deal, which is the same transaction seen in sterling — and Álex Grimaldo to Atlético Madrid, €20m, who play on this slate. Full-back, where the replacement money went: in — Guéla Doué from Strasbourg, reported at €30m rising to €37.5m on a five-year deal, and Miguel Gutiérrez from Napoli, €26m on a five-year deal, also from a club on this slate. Wide attack: in — Afonso Moreira from Lyon, €29.5m. Roughly €85m committed against €60m received, with the centre-back who left for €40m replaced by two full-backs and a winger. One of the two, Doué, is injured for this match.
  • RB Leipzig — turnover high in bodies, spread across midfield and the flanks; manager changed 24 June 2026. In: Brajan Gruda from Brighton, Rocco Reitz from Borussia Mönchengladbach, Ayodele Thomas from PSV Eindhoven, Abdoul Koné from Reims and Suleiman Sani from Trenčín. Reitz, one of the two most prominent arrivals, is out with a hamstring injury for this fixture. The window was broader and cheaper than Leverkusen’s and no single fee approaches the €40m Hincapié move on the other side. Published fees for Leipzig’s window were not obtainable from any source reached and are not estimated — see the gap note below.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Leverkusen are missing four: Guéla Doué (muscle — the €30m right-back signed this summer), Nathan Tella (back), Ibrahim Maza (illness) and Montrell Culbreath (foot). Leipzig are missing four: Rocco Reitz (hamstring — a summer signing), Chris Baumgartner (hamstring), Assan Ouédraogo (shoulder) and Leopold Zingerle (not eligible). Both clubs are therefore without one of their own summer arrivals, and on Leverkusen’s side the missing player is the direct replacement for a €40m departure.
  • Sample. Leverkusen: five competitive matches — Bundesliga 2–3 at Elversberg (29 August), 4–0 v Union Berlin (5 September), 2–2 at Augsburg (12 September); DFB-Pokal 4–0 at Wehen Wiesbaden (22 August); Europa League 2–0 v Celje (16 September). Four points from three league matches, 8 scored and 5 conceded — and the opening-day defeat was at Elversberg, a promoted side who play later on this slate. Leipzig: five competitive matches — Bundesliga 3–0 v Mönchengladbach (29 August), 1–3 at Werder Bremen (5 September), 5–0 v Hamburg (13 September); DFB-Pokal 6–0 at Eintracht Trier (22 August); Champions League 1–4 at Como (10 September), and Como also play on this slate. Six points from three, 9 scored and 3 conceded, with both home matches won 3–0 and 5–0 and the only away match lost 1–3. ESPN’s standings place Leverkusen 10th on 4 points and Leipzig 7th on 6; match previews list them 8th and 5th — the tables disagree on a tie-break, not on the points.
  • Referee. Robert Schröder, confirmed in the DFB appointments, with Johann Pfeifer on VAR and Harm Osmers as fourth official. Across 9 Bundesliga matches in 2025–26: 3.89 cards per match (33 yellows, 2 reds), 22.6 fouls per match, 5.8 fouls per card, and 0.44 penalties per match. The card and foul figures are close to divisional — 3.89 against 3.82, 5.8 fouls per card against 5.46 — so on booking he is unremarkable. The penalty rate is not: 0.44 per match against a divisional 0.36 last season and 0.18 this season, so he has awarded penalties at roughly a fifth above the completed-season rate and more than double the current one. Nine matches is a modest record and the penalty figure rests on four awards in nine; it is reported as counted, not as a tendency. This season he has taken one match — Schalke 0–0 Bayern on 5 September — with 4 cards in 17 fouls, a 4.2 fouls-per-card ratio well below his own. History: two Leverkusen matches last season, both away defeats-or-wins for them (1–3 at Frankfurt, 3–1 at Stuttgart, the latter with a penalty), and no Leipzig match at all.

No published fee was obtainable for any of RB Leipzig’s five summer arrivals — Gruda, Reitz, Thomas, Koné and Sani are named but their fees are not published in any source reached, and none is estimated; that leaves the transfer ledger on this card asymmetric, with Leverkusen’s window fully priced and Leipzig’s not. Leipzig’s outgoing list was likewise not obtainable. No source reached characterises in words how Schröder reads contact in the box; the 0.44 penalties per match is counted from 9 matches. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division: the Bundesliga carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data per match, and the official for every fixture.

B · Style & early-season reality

Leverkusen under Martínez Novell have become one of the most territorially dominant sides on this board, and the numbers behind it are the largest single-season jumps anywhere on the slate. Shots have risen from 15.18 to 24.00 per match, deep completions from 8.65 to 18.00 — more than double — corners won from 5.59 to 9.33 against 2.33 conceded, and possession from 59.2% to 64.8%, while PPDA has fallen from 12.75 to 9.13. Five columns, all moving hard in the same direction: they press more, hold more, get deeper more often and take a third more shots. The modelled consequence matches: xG 2.63 against 2.21, xG conceded 0.83 against 1.50. The counted consequence only partly matches — 2.67 scored and 1.67 conceded — because the 1.67 rests on an opening-day 2–3 at Elversberg that no other match resembles. The 14–2 corner count at Augsburg and the 11–1 against Union Berlin are the two most lopsided territorial matches in this dataset. All of it is three matches old, and the coach has been in the job since 1 July.

Leipzig under Demichelis have arrived at nearly the same place by a quieter route. xG is 2.61 against Leverkusen’s 2.63, and xG conceded 0.87 against 0.83 — the two sides are within 0.04 of each other on both halves of the model. Their shot count is up from 15.79 to 21.00, deep completions from 9.88 to 11.33, possession from 53.9% to 60.7%. The difference is in the press: Leipzig’s PPDA has barely moved, 12.88 to 11.77, against Leverkusen’s 12.75 to 9.13 — so Leipzig are dominating through the ball rather than through pressure, and their fouls have stayed at 9.67 per match against Leverkusen’s rise from 8.59 to 12.67. The result distribution is bimodal in a way that matters: two home wins by 3–0 and 5–0, one away defeat 1–3, and a 1–4 at Como in the Champions League — eight goals scored at home in two matches, two conceded away in one plus four in Italy. Their 0.67 cards per match is the second-lowest figure on this board. Both clubs’ 25/26 records are near-identical too — 68 and 66 goals scored, 47 conceded each, 2.21 xG each — which is why the 2026–27 separation of six points against four rests on three matches and very little else.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs sacked a coach who had delivered European football, three weeks apart, and both replacements have three league matches of record. The 2025–26 seasons that would separate them do not: 68 goals against 66, 47 conceded each, 2.21 xG each — these were two functionally identical teams. This season’s separation is six points to four, from three matches in which Leipzig won both at home and lost their only away game, and Leverkusen lost at a promoted side and drew at Augsburg while out-shooting them 30–8. The Bundesliga’s home-win rate is 51.5% on 33 matches against 43.8% last season. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
The margins are large and they are large in both directions on both sides. Leverkusen’s three are −1, +4, 0; Leipzig’s are +3, −2, +5, with a −3 in Europe on top. Five of the six league matches between them produced a margin of two or more, and the two sides’ expected-goal figures are separated by 0.02. A handicap has to price a gap between two teams whose models are indistinguishable, on results that swing from 5–0 to 1–3, with three matches each. This is the widest result variance on the slate sitting on the narrowest underlying gap on it.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — every input agrees, though every input is short
This is the most unanimous reading on the board. All six of the two clubs’ league matches this season have gone over 2.5 — 100% each — and the combined xG is 5.24 per match (2.63 and 2.61), the highest of any fixture carded on this slate by a distance. The division agrees emphatically: the Bundesliga is at 3.97 goals per match and 81.8% over 2.5, against 3.24 and 63.7% last season, the most extreme divisional figures anywhere on this board — and the divisional xG line has risen with it, 1.70 to 2.01, which is what stops the run being dismissed as finishing variance. Last season both clubs were also above the over-2.5 line (58.8% and 70.6% against 63.7%). The caveat, stated plainly: three league matches each and 33 divisional matches. Every number here is unanimous and every number here is young.
BTTSOK — the one place the two sides’ records diverge
The 25/26 evidence is strong and identical — 64.7% and 61.8% against a divisional 61.8%, with failed-to-score rates of 11.8% and 8.8%: neither of these clubs stops scoring — and it is confirmed this season, where both have failed to score in 0% of matches. What splits them is the other half: Leverkusen have conceded in two of three (66.7% BTTS), Leipzig in one of three (33.3%, with 66.7% clean sheets and both home matches won to nil). Leipzig’s clean sheets came at home, against Mönchengladbach and Hamburg; their away record is 1–3 at Bremen and 1–4 at Como. The scoring half of this market is as well evidenced as anything on the slate; the conceding half rests on a two-match home run.
Team goalsWell-suited — both sides’ volume is documented across five independent columns
Rare on this board: both team-goals lines have real evidence, and it is the same evidence on each side. Leverkusen: 24.00 shots, 6.33 on target, 18.00 deep completions, 9.33 corners won and 2.63 xG, against 15.18, 5.18, 8.65, 5.59 and 2.21 last season. Leipzig: 21.00 shots, 7.33 on target, 11.33 deep completions, 7.33 corners and 2.61 xG, against 15.79, 5.97, 9.88, 5.29 and 2.21. Both clubs scored 66–68 goals last season and neither failed to score in a league match this season. The divisional attacking baseline has risen from 1.70 to 2.01 xG per team, so both are above a line that has itself moved up. Three matches, but five agreeing columns per side.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — on first-half goals, and mostly on the division rather than the clubs
Both clubs score exactly 1.00 before the break and concede 0.67 and 0.33 — a combined 3.00 first-half goals per match against a divisional 1.45, which is the second-widest gap on the slate. Behind it, though, the club-level movement is small and in the wrong direction: Leverkusen were at 1.18 first-half goals last season and are at 1.00; Leipzig were at 0.94 and are at 1.00. The lift is coming from the division — 1.45 per match against 1.44 last season, on a 3.97 goals-per-game backdrop — rather than from anything either club has changed. The first-half result is not readable at all, inheriting the 1X2 problem on a three-match sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous
Three of the four inputs are unusually low and the fourth is short. Leipzig take 0.67 cards per match on 9.67 fouls, the second-cleanest figures on this board and continuous with 1.53 and 9.26 last season. Leverkusen have gone the other way — 12.67 fouls against 8.59 last season, with PPDA 12.75→9.13 to explain it — but their cards have fallen anyway, 1.67 against 2.00. The division has fallen from 3.82 cards to 3.67. Robert Schröder is close to divisional on both card axes (3.89 and 5.8 fouls per card against 3.82 and 5.46), so the official adds nothing either way — and his record is only nine matches. One genuine structural change, Leverkusen’s foul rise, pointing into a card count that has fallen, with three matches on each side and a nine-match referee. Nothing here converges.
CornersSplit — Leverkusen’s side is extraordinary and three matches old
Leverkusen have won 9.33 corners per match and conceded 2.33 — a seven-corner differential, the second-largest on this board behind Juventus — against 5.59 and 4.12 last season and a divisional 5.42. The mechanism is fully documented: possession 59.2%→64.8%, shots 15.18→24.00, deep completions 8.65→18.00. The individual matches are as lopsided as the average: 11–1 against Union Berlin, 14–2 at Augsburg. Leipzig’s 7.33 for and 5.00 against is a smaller move from 5.29 and 4.32. What holds this back from well-suited is that Leverkusen’s figure rests on three matches, two of them against sides currently 17th and 4th, and a 9.33 corner rate is nearly double what the same club produced across 34 matches last season. A very large effect with a very short record and a named cause.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Three league matches each is the thinnest sample on this slate. Every 2026–27 figure on this card — the 100% over-2.5 rates, the 9.33 corners, the 2.63 xG, the 0.67 cards — rests on three matches. The Bundesliga has played 33 matches in total, so the divisional baseline it is read against is the youngest of the five as well.
  • Both clubs’ 2025–26 records were produced by coaches who were sacked for producing them. Hjulmand left Leverkusen after nine months and 48 matches; Werner left Leipzig after one season and a third-place finish. Martínez Novell started on 1 July, Demichelis in late June. Leverkusen are on their third coach in twelve months.
  • Leverkusen’s transfer ledger is priced and Leipzig’s is not. €40m out for Hincapié, €20m for Grimaldo, roughly €85m in for Doué, Gutiérrez and Moreira; no fee was obtainable for any of Leipzig’s five arrivals. Any comparison of investment across this fixture is therefore half-sourced and is not completed by estimation.
  • Both clubs are missing one of their own summer signings, and Leverkusen’s is the replacement for their biggest sale. Doué (€30m, right-back) is out injured; Reitz is out for Leipzig. The back line that has conceded 0.83 xG per match is not the back line that was bought.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and Discipline — the first two because two statistically indistinguishable sides are separated only by which venue they happened to play at, the third because the one real structural change points opposite to the card count. Least sensitive: Totals and Team goals — six from six over 2.5, a combined 5.24 xG, and a division running at 3.97 goals per match with its own xG line up from 1.70 to 2.01.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than 1X2 here because the two clubs agree about goals and disagree about nothing else. Both are at 100% over 2.5 with 2.63 and 2.61 xG; both were above the divisional over-2.5 line last season under different coaches; the division itself is at 81.8%. The result, by contrast, has to separate two teams whose xG differs by 0.02 and whose last seasons differed by two goals scored and none conceded.
  • Team goals is more robust than BTTS because the scoring half of both records is firm and the conceding half of one is not. Neither club has failed to score in a league match this season and neither did so in more than 11.8% of matches last season. Leipzig’s 66.7% clean-sheet rate, by contrast, is two home wins — their away matches this season read 1–3 at Bremen and 1–4 at Como.
  • Corners reads better than Discipline despite both resting on three matches, because the corner change has five columns behind it and the card change has none. Leverkusen’s 5.59→9.33 sits with possession 59.2→64.8, shots 15.18→24.00 and deep completions 8.65→18.00. Their fouls rose 8.59→12.67 and their cards fell 2.00→1.67, which is the opposite of what the pressing change predicts.
  • Any penalty-dependent line is the one place the official matters here. Schröder is average on cards (3.89 against 3.82) and above his division on penalties (0.44 against 0.36 last season, 0.18 this) — but on nine matches and four awards, which is the shortest referee record of any appointment on this slate.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Leverkusen’s 24 shots and 18 deep completions per match survive a fourth and fifth league match. It is the number underneath the corner reading, the team-goals reading and half the totals reading, it is a doubling of last season’s territorial volume, and it was accumulated in two matches against sides currently in the bottom half plus one defeat at a promoted club. What would settle it is two more matchdays of shot and deep-completion data against opposition of this quality — today’s fixture is itself the first such test.

Spain · LaLiga · Jornada 7

Atlético Madrid – Real Madrid

16:15 CEST · Riyadh Air Metropolitano, Madrid · a Real Madrid running at 3.21 expected goals per match under a manager appointed in June, against an Atlético who have not conceded a single first-half goal all season — and the first Madrid-committee referee ever appointed to this derby

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Atlético finished 4th in 2025–26 on 69 points — 21 wins, 6 draws, 11 defeats, 62 scored, 44 conceded — a season split violently by venue: 15-1-3 at home, 6-5-8 away. Real Madrid finished 2nd on 86 points — 27 wins, 5 draws, 6 defeats, 77 scored, 35 conceded, eight behind Barcelona. The structural headline is that one club changed everything at the top and the other changed almost nothing. Real Madrid dismissed Álvaro Arbeloa on 9 June 2026, announced José Mourinho on 11 June on a contract to 2029, and then rebuilt the back line: Ibrahima Konaté arrived after being released by Liverpool, alongside Marc Cucurella and Denzel Dumfries, with Bernardo Silva joining free from Manchester City. Diego Simeóne is in his fifteenth season at Atlético, which makes this one of only three fixtures on the slate where a club’s entire 2025–26 record belongs to the man picking the team — and Atlético spent anyway, replacing Antoine Griezmann and a full-back line. Three of Real Madrid’s four significant arrivals came free or from clubs playing elsewhere on this same slate — Konaté released by Liverpool (f4), Bernardo Silva free from Manchester City (f6) — and Atlético’s left-back came from Leverkusen (f9).

  • Real Madrid — turnover high and concentrated entirely on the defence; manager changed 11 June 2026. Arbeloa was dismissed on 9 June and Mourinho announced on 11 June on a deal to 2029. Centre-back: in — Ibrahima Konaté, released by Liverpool and signed free. Full-back: in — Marc Cucurella and Denzel Dumfries, the latter expected to start at right-back ahead of Trent Alexander-Arnold. Midfield: in — Bernardo Silva free from Manchester City, having been released at the end of his contract. An entire back line assembled in one window, two of its three principal components on free transfers, in front of a squad that conceded 35 goals last season. The fees for Cucurella and Dumfries were not obtainable from any source reached and are not estimated.
  • Atlético Madrid — turnover high in bodies, heavy in the midfield and the flanks; no managerial change, Simeóne in his fifteenth season. Defensive midfield: in — Morten Hjulmand from Sporting CP, reported at around €40m on a contract to 2031, the largest fee on this side. Centre-back: in — Cristian Romero. Left-back: in — Álex Grimaldo from Bayer Leverkusen, €20m, from a club on this slate. Attack: in — Kang-In Lee, expected to start. Out, the headline: Antoine Griezmann, leaving free at the end of his second spell for Orlando City in MLS, plus Nahuel Molina, Matteo Ruggeri, Thiago Almada and three others. The club replaced a departing full-back line and its most identifiable forward, and bought a €40m holding midfielder and a centre-back on top.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Atlético have three attacking doubts and one of them is their principal forward: Julián Álvarez is a major doubt with a persistent muscular strain that has already cost him the last two fixtures, and Alexander Sørloth and Pablo Barrios face late fitness tests with muscular problems. Jonathan David is expected to lead the line in Álvarez’s place after scoring against Osasuna. Real Madrid are without three: Éder Militão, Rodrygo and Ferland Mendy. Militão’s absence sits directly on top of the rebuilt back line — a defence already carrying Konaté, Cucurella and Dumfries as new arrivals is missing one of its incumbents. Kylian Mbappé leads LaLiga’s scoring charts with seven goals and is available.
  • Sample. Atlético: seven competitive matches — LaLiga 2–0 v Málaga (19 August, a club on this slate), 2–2 v Villarreal (23 August, also on this slate), 3–1 at Sevilla (29 August), 0–3 at Athletic Club (5 September), 3–0 at Real Sociedad (13 September, also on this slate), 4–0 v Osasuna (16 September); Champions League 1–2 at Liverpool (9 September, also on this slate). Fourth on 13 points, 14 scored and 6 conceded, with two consecutive clean-sheet wins by 3–0 and 4–0 after consecutive defeats. Real Madrid: seven competitive matches — LaLiga 2–1 at Espanyol (22 August), 4–1 v Real Sociedad (26 August), 4–0 v Málaga (30 August), 0–1 at Real Betis (4 September, also on this slate), 4–1 v Rayo Vallecano (12 September), 3–2 at Elche (15 September); Champions League 2–1 v Inter (8 September). Second on 15 points, 17 scored and 6 conceded, five wins from six with the only defeat away at Betis. Both clubs have faced four of the other clubs carded or listed on this slate.
  • Referee. Miguel Ángel Ortiz Arias of the Madrid committee, confirmed — and the appointment is itself the story. The CTA has broken, for the first time, the long-standing territoriality rule that kept a Madrid-based official away from a fixture between the two Madrid clubs. Across 20 LaLiga matches in 2025–26: 4.15 cards per match (79 yellows, 4 reds), 22.8 fouls per match, 5.5 fouls per card, and 0.20 penalties per match. Against the division that is a close-to-average booking profile and a notably low penalty one: 4.15 cards against 4.52, 5.5 fouls per card against 5.57 — almost exactly divisional — and 0.20 penalties against 0.35, well under. This season he has taken three matches at 3.67 cards, 18.3 fouls, 5.0 fouls per card and no penalty at all. History: one Atlético match in the record, a 1–0 home win over Getafe in March 2026 with seven cards and no penalty, and no Real Madrid match at all in either season.
  • Not sourced. Fees were not obtainable for Marc Cucurella, Denzel Dumfries or Cristian Romero, nor for most of Atlético’s seven departures, and none is estimated — the transfer ledger on this card is named but only partly priced on both sides. The exact date of Mourinho’s formal start, as against the 11 June announcement, was not established. No source reached characterises in words how Ortiz Arias reads contact in the box; the 0.20 penalties per match is counted from 20 matches. Everything else the spec asks for is published: LaLiga carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot, foul, card and corner data per match, and the official for every fixture.

B · Style & early-season reality

Real Madrid under Mourinho have produced the single largest attacking jump on this board, and it has arrived alongside a press that has gone in the opposite direction. xG has risen from 2.14 to 3.21 per match — a full goal of expected output — with non-penalty xG at 2.96, shots from 17.50 to 20.67, shots on target from 6.66 to 8.33, deep completions from 10.03 to 16.17 and corners won from 6.34 to 7.83. At the same time PPDA has risen from 10.77 to 13.38 and possession has fallen from 59.4% to 54.8%. That combination — less of the ball, less pressing, dramatically more territory and chance volume — is the clearest system signature on the slate: this is a side attacking in fewer, longer sequences and getting deeper when it does. The defensive half has improved on the model (1.18 to 0.90 xG conceded) without improving on the scoreboard (0.92 to 1.00 goals), and the clean-sheet rate has collapsed from 36.8% to 16.7%. Their fouls are the second-lowest on this board at 8.83 per match against a divisional 12.41.

Atlético have changed much less and what has changed is concentrated in one place. xG 1.88 against 1.79, xG conceded 1.34 against 1.40, PPDA 10.67 against 11.00, deep completions 10.50 against 9.24 — four series essentially static across the summer, which is what fifteen seasons of the same coach looks like. Possession is up modestly, 53.3% to 56.6%, and shots from 13.11 to 16.83. The one figure that is not static is the first half, and it is extreme: Atlético have conceded no first-half goal at all in six league matches, against 0.58 per match last season, while scoring 0.67 before the break against 0.76. Their goals have arrived late and their concessions later still. The corner column has moved the other way from Real Madrid’s — 6.50 won last season to 5.33 now, and 4.29 conceded to 3.33 — fewer at both ends. The headline number, 2.33 goals per match against 1.63 last season, is running well ahead of an xG figure that has barely moved, which is the gap to hold in mind: 14 goals from 11.3 expected.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Real Madrid changed manager in June and rebuilt an entire back line around two free transfers, and are missing Militão from it. Atlético’s last-season record is the most venue-split on the slate — 15-1-3 at home, 6-5-8 away — which argues for the venue and against reading their away form, but their principal forward is a major doubt and two more attackers face late fitness tests. The two clubs are separated by two points after six matches. LaLiga’s home-win rate is 43.8% against 48.9%. A derby between a side whose xG has risen by a full goal under a new manager and one whose team sheet may be missing three forwards is not a readable result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Both sides’ margins are wide and bimodal. Atlético’s six run +2, 0, +2, −3, +3, +4 — two of their six matches decided by three or more in each direction. Real Madrid’s run +1, +3, +4, −1, +3, +1. Between them, seven of twelve league matches finished with a margin of three or more. Last season’s two meetings were 5–2 and 3–2, both with five or more goals and neither close. A handicap has to price a gap between two clubs separated by two points whose expected-goal figures differ by 1.33 per match — the widest xG gap on this slate between two sides of comparable league standing.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — counted, modelled and divisional evidence all agree, on six-match samples
Every series points the same way and the samples clear the five-match line on both sides. Both clubs have gone over 2.5 in 83.3% of six league matches against a divisional 56.2%; combined they have scored 31 goals in twelve matches; and the combined xG is 5.09 per match (3.21 and 1.88) against a divisional 3.24 for two average sides. Real Madrid’s 3.21 is the highest attacking figure on this board and its non-penalty component is 2.96, so it is not a penalty artefact. The division has moved the same way, 3.05 goals per game against 2.69 and 56.2% over 2.5 against 50.0%. The two previous derbies in the record, 5–2 and 3–2, both cleared the line comfortably. The counterweight is that Atlético’s own xG is 1.88, close to last season’s 1.79, so half the combined figure comes from one side.
BTTSSplit — the two clubs’ records point in opposite directions and both are six matches
Atlético read 33.3% and Real Madrid 66.7%, against a divisional 56.2%, and the components explain the gap rather than resolving it. Atlético have kept clean sheets in 50% of matches this season against 36.8% last, including consecutive 3–0 and 4–0 wins immediately before this fixture. Real Madrid’s clean-sheet rate has collapsed from 36.8% to 16.7% while their xG conceded improved from 1.18 to 0.90 — a counted record and a modelled record disagreeing inside one club. Both sides fail to score in 16.7% of matches. Last season’s two derbies both saw both teams score. Readable as a structure; the deciding input is Atlético’s attack, which may be without Álvarez and Sørloth.
Team goalsSplit — Real Madrid’s volume is the best evidenced on the slate, Atlético’s is a doubt list
Real Madrid’s attacking series is the strongest on this board: 3.21 xG, 2.96 non-penalty, 20.67 shots, 8.33 on target, 16.17 deep completions and 7.83 corners per match, against 2.14, 1.87, 17.50, 6.66, 10.03 and 6.34 last season — six columns, all up, with Mbappé on seven league goals. Atlético’s is the opposite: 2.33 goals per match from 1.88 xG — 14 scored against 11.3 expected — with Julián Álvarez a major doubt, Sørloth and Barrios facing late tests, and Griezmann gone. One team-goals line is about as evidenced as this board gets; the other depends on which forwards are fit.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited on first-half goals — the most striking asymmetry on the slate
This is the single most unusual counted fact on the board. Atlético have not conceded a first-half goal in six league matches — 0.00 per match, against 0.58 last season — while Real Madrid score 1.67 before the break, up from 0.87, and have led at half-time in four of six (1–1 at Espanyol, 1–1 v Sociedad, 3–0 v Málaga, 3–0 v Rayo, 2–0 at Elche). Two six-match series moving hard in opposite directions, both well clear of their own prior seasons and of a divisional 1.42. The combined 2.67 first-half goals per match is nearly double the divisional rate. The first-half result remains weaker — it inherits the 1X2 problem — but the goal side of this family is as directly evidenced as anything here.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — two of the cleanest sides in Spain, an average official, and a derby
The team evidence and the fixture type disagree, and both deserve stating. Both clubs are well under the divisional foul rate: Real Madrid 8.83 per match, the second-lowest on this board, against a divisional 12.41; Atlético 11.17. Card rates are 1.67 and 1.83 per team against a divisional 2.22. Both were similarly low last season (10.00 and 11.18 fouls), so this is method, not a run. Ortiz Arias is almost exactly divisional — 4.15 cards against 4.52, 5.5 fouls per card against 5.57 — and lower still this season at 3.67, so the official adds nothing in either direction. Against all of that: the two previous derbies in this record produced ten cards between them, Atlético took 5 red cards across last season, and the appointment has broken a territoriality convention that existed precisely because this fixture is contentious. Structurally quiet, contextually not.
CornersOK — asymmetric and moving, with only one side’s change explained
Real Madrid win 7.83 corners per match and concede 4.33, against 6.34 and 3.68 last season; Atlético win 5.33 and concede 3.33, against 6.50 and 4.29. The divisional line is 4.82. Real Madrid’s rise has a documented cause — deep completions 10.03 to 16.17, shots 17.50 to 20.67 — while Atlético’s fall has none stated: their possession is up, their shots are up, and their corners at both ends are down. Atlético’s 3.33 conceded is the second-lowest on this board and would be the relevant half of the market here. A real gap, six matches per side, and one of the two movements without a mechanism.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Real Madrid’s 2025–26 record belongs to Arbeloa and to a defence that has been replaced. Mourinho was announced on 11 June; Konaté, Cucurella and Dumfries all arrived, two of them free; Militão is injured. The 35 goals conceded last season were conceded by a different back four, and this season’s clean-sheet rate is 16.7% against 36.8%.
  • Atlético’s goals are running ahead of their expected goals by a clear margin. 14 scored against 11.3 expected across six matches, on an xG line (1.88) barely moved from last season’s 1.79. The 2.33 goals per match is the number most likely to be a short-run artefact on this card.
  • Atlético’s attacking team news is unresolved at the time of writing. Julián Álvarez is a major doubt, Sørloth and Barrios face late fitness tests, and Griezmann left free in the summer. Every attacking reading on this card — team goals, BTTS, half the totals — depends on a team sheet that is not published until an hour before kickoff.
  • The first-half figures are the most extreme on the slate and rest on six matches each. Atlético’s zero first-half goals conceded is a remarkable counted fact, but zero is the hardest figure to read: it has no variance to measure, and one goal changes the rate to 0.14.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and any Atlético team-goals line — the first two because two clubs two points apart have expected-goal figures 1.33 apart, the third because three of Atlético’s forwards are doubtful. Least sensitive: Totals and first-half goals — both rest on six-match samples on each side, agreeing counted and modelled series, and a divisional baseline moving the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals is more robust than 1X2 here because both clubs clear the five-match line and agree, while the result depends on a team sheet. 83.3% over 2.5 each against a divisional 56.2%, 31 goals in twelve matches, a combined 5.09 xG, and two previous derbies of 5–2 and 3–2. The result has to separate sides two points apart, one of them possibly without its top three forwards.
  • First-half goals is more robust than the first-half result because both halves of it are counted and neither needs a winner. Atlético’s 0.00 conceded and Real Madrid’s 1.67 scored are six-match rates that have both moved sharply from their own prior seasons (0.58 and 0.87) and sit either side of a divisional 1.42.
  • A Real Madrid team-goals line is more robust than an Atlético one, and the reason is six columns against a doubt list. Real Madrid: xG 2.14→3.21, npxG 1.87→2.96, shots 17.50→20.67, on target 6.66→8.33, deep 10.03→16.17, corners 6.34→7.83, with Mbappé on seven. Atlético: 14 goals from 11.3 expected, with Álvarez, Sørloth and Barrios all doubtful.
  • Discipline is the family where the counted evidence and the fixture context diverge most sharply. Both clubs foul below the divisional rate in both seasons (8.83 and 11.17 against 12.41) and the official is dead average (4.15 against 4.52). That is a structural reading. It sits against a derby that produced ten cards across its last two meetings and an appointment that broke a territoriality rule written for this fixture.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Julián Álvarez plays. He has missed the last two fixtures with a muscular strain, Atlético are scoring 2.33 per match from 1.88 xG, and Sørloth is a late test behind him. With him the totals and BTTS readings rest on two functioning attacks; without him and Sørloth they rest on Real Madrid’s 3.21 xG alone. The confirmed lineup, published an hour before kickoff, is what settles it.

France · Ligue 1 · Journée 5

OGC Nice – LOSC Lille

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted, but Nice came closer to going down than any club carded on this slate: they finished 16th in 2025–26 on 32 points — 7 wins, 11 draws, 16 defeats, 37 scored, 60 conceded — and survived only through a relegation play-off against Saint-Étienne, having also lost a Coupe de France final. The 60 goals conceded and the 17.6% clean-sheet rate are among the worst figures on this board, second in both cases to Real Sociedad. Lille finished 3rd on 61 points — 18 wins, 7 draws, 9 defeats, 52 scored, 37 conceded — and qualified for the Champions League, where they lost 3–2 at home to Real Betis on 8 September, a club that also plays on this slate. The structural headline is two coaching changes made three weeks apart in June, at opposite ends of the table and for opposite reasons. Nice appointed Olivier Pantaloni on 17 June 2026, after Claude Puel had been called in as an emergency measure following Franck Haise’s departure and was not retained — that is three men in one season. Lille appointed Davide Ancelotti on 1 June 2026 to succeed Bruno Génésio, who left for Marseille and appears later on this same slate at f18.

  • Nice — deep reconstruction after a play-off survival; third head coach inside twelve months, Pantaloni appointed 17 June 2026. Franck Haise left, Claude Puel was brought in urgently to rescue the season and did not continue, and Olivier Pantaloni was appointed on 17 June for a rebuild the club itself has described as deep. This board’s card of 30 August (fx_10_f4) recorded the change at the time without a date; it is dated here. No itemised summer 2026 transfer ledger with fees for Nice was obtainable from any source reached, so the named-signings requirement of the RESEARCH SPEC is not satisfied for this club and nothing is estimated. What is established is the context: a club that conceded 60 goals, lost a cup final, survived a play-off and is now on its third coach.
  • Lille — turnover high in defence, replaced numerically rather than upgraded; manager changed 1 June 2026. Davide Ancelotti was appointed on 1 June 2026 to replace Bruno Génésio, and this board’s card of 28 August (fx_8_f3) recorded a summer of seven arrivals against twelve departures. Defence: out — Thomas Meunier and Aïssa Mandi, both at the end of their contracts; Meunier signed for Sunderland, who play earlier today at f6. In — Loun Srdanovic and Tanguy Nianzou, described in the French press as numerical replacements rather than upgrades. Wide: in — Basar Önal from Nijmegen. Out, the headline: Ayyoub Bouaddi to Manchester City. Contradiction, now resolved: the French press reported this deal at close to €130m; Manchester City’s own ledger, recorded on card f6, prices it at £86m. The club figure is the correct one: £81.3m fixed plus £4.3m in add-ons, on a five-year contract to 2031, confirmed in August 2026 — a record for a teenager in the Premier League. The €130m figure is not supported by anything reached and is not used. The squad is otherwise described as little disturbed, including 40-year-old Olivier Giroud.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club was obtainable from any source reached, and none is invented. The Ligue 1 appointments page confirms the officials and the kickoff but carries no team news, and neither club’s published preview material reached here names an absentee. Lille played in the Champions League on 8 September, twelve days ago, and have had a normal week; Nice have played nothing but league football all season.
  • Sample. Nice: four competitive matches, all league — 0–0 v Lorient (22 August), 0–3 at Paris FC (30 August), 1–1 v Le Mans (5 September), 0–1 at Auxerre (12 September) — Auxerre play earlier on this same slate. Eighteenth and bottom on 2 points from two draws, one goal scored and five conceded. They have failed to score in three of four, and both away matches finished with nothing scored. Lille: five competitive matches — Ligue 1 2–0 at Angers (23 August), 2–2 v Paris Saint-Germain (28 August) — PSG play later today — 1–0 at Toulouse (3 September), 2–0 v Troyes (13 September); Champions League 2–3 v Real Betis (8 September), also on this slate. Fourth on 10 points from three wins and a draw, 7 scored and 2 conceded, with three clean sheets in four and a first-half lead in every league match. Both of Lille’s goals conceded came in the same match, the 2–2 with PSG.
  • Referee. Willy Delajod, confirmed by the Ligue 1 appointments, with Erwan Finjean and Valentin Evrard on the lines, Mickaël Leleu as fourth official and Cyril Gringore and Victoria Beyer on video. Across 17 Ligue 1 matches in 2025–26: 3.12 cards per match (49 yellows, 4 reds), 24.8 fouls per match, 8.0 fouls per card, and 0.53 penalties per match. The profile is distinctive and both halves matter here. On booking he is the most permissive official appointed on this slate bar one: 3.12 cards against a divisional 3.91, and 8.0 fouls per card against 6.18 — he lets about a third more play run between bookings than the Ligue 1 average. On penalties he is the opposite: 0.53 per match against a divisional 0.36, the second-highest rate of any official appointed today, including a three-penalty match. This season he has taken two matches at 3.50 cards and a much shorter 5.6 fouls per card, with no penalty. History, and an unusual amount of it: four Nice matches last season (2–2 at Monaco with three penalties, 0–2 at Lens, 0–4 at home to PSG with a penalty, 1–1 at Marseille with a penalty) and four Lille matches (1–0 v Monaco with a penalty, 0–1 at Le Havre, 1–4 v Strasbourg, 0–2 v Auxerre). Five of those eight matches produced a penalty.
  • Not sourced. Nice’s entire summer 2026 transfer ledger was not obtainable, and no injury or suspension information exists for either club in anything reached — two RESEARCH SPEC fields left empty. Fees are undisclosed for Srdanovic, Nianzou and Önal into Lille. The Bouaddi fee contradiction above is now resolved at £86m — £81.3m fixed plus £4.3m in add-ons — matching Manchester City’s ledger on card f6; the French press figure of close to €130m is not supported by anything reached and is not used. No source characterises in words how Delajod reads contact in the box, though his 0.53 penalties per match over 17 matches is the strongest counted statement available. Ligue 1’s 2025–26 record in the ESPN feed holds 305 of 306 matches.

B · Style & early-season reality

Nice have changed shape more than any club on this board and have scored one goal doing it. Possession has risen from 46.3% to 60.7% — fourteen points, the second-largest move on this slate — PPDA has fallen from 13.29 to 10.35, and deep completions have risen from 5.53 to 7.50. They hold the ball, press harder and get into the final third more often. The output is 0.88 xG per match, the third-lowest on this board, and 0.25 goals — one in four matches — from 12.25 shots and just 2.75 on target, the second-lowest on-target count here. They are creating volume without quality and converting none of it. The corner column confirms the territorial change and the lack of end product at once: 6.50 won against 2.50 conceded, having won 5.09 and conceded 5.74 last season — a differential that has swung by more than four corners a match and produced a single goal. Their fouls have fallen from 12.35 to 8.25, the third-lowest on this board, which is what possession does. The defensive figure remains poor on the model, 1.97 xG conceded, though the counted rate has improved from 1.76 to 1.25.

Lille under Ancelotti have done something unusual: they have become considerably worse on the model and considerably better on the scoreboard. PPDA has risen from 10.17 to 16.01 — the loosest press of any club carded on this slate — possession has fallen from 55.8% to 45.1%, shots from 13.59 to 10.75, deep completions from 9.44 to 7.00, and xG conceded has risen from 1.14 to 1.74. Five series, all moving the wrong way. And they have conceded two goals in four matches, kept three clean sheets, and not conceded before half-time at all. Their attacking conversion is carrying it: 6.00 shots on target per match from 10.75 shots, a 56% on-target rate, producing 1.75 goals from 1.37 xG. The corner figure is the most extreme on the card in the other direction: 1.75 won per match, the lowest of any club anywhere on this board, against 5.71 last season. A side that had the lowest over-2.5 rate in Ligue 1 last season at 38.2% has made its matches smaller still, from a structure that the underlying numbers say should be leaking.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Nice are on their third head coach in twelve months after surviving a relegation play-off, their transfer window could not be sourced, and they have scored once in four matches. Lille changed coach on 1 June, lost both first-choice centre-backs at contract end, and have a counted defensive record (0.50 conceded) that their own model (1.74 xG against) contradicts by more than a goal a match. Bottom against fourth, two points against ten — and Ligue 1’s home-win rate has fallen to 38.1% from 46.2% with a 31.0% draw rate. The two previous meetings in the record were 2–0 to Nice and 0–0, both under different coaches at both clubs.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Nice’s four margins are 0, −3, 0, −1 and Lille’s are +2, 0, +1, +2 — and every one of Lille’s positive margins came against a side currently below them. The modelled gap is far narrower than the table: Lille 1.37 xG for and 1.74 against, Nice 0.88 and 1.97 — a difference of about half a goal a match, against eight points in the table. Lille’s entire defensive record rests on conceding twice in four, both in one match. A handicap has to price a gap that the points and the models disagree about by a wide margin, on four matches each.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — the strongest low-scoring evidence on this board after f8
Every series agrees and one has a full season behind it. Both clubs have gone over 2.5 in 25.0% of four matches against a divisional 57.1%, and their eight league matches have produced 14 goals. Lille’s 38.2% over-2.5 rate across thirty-four matches last season was the lowest in Ligue 1, against a divisional 52.8%. The models agree at the attacking end: combined xG 2.25 per match against a divisional 3.30 — the second-lowest combined figure on this board. Nice have scored once in four; Lille have conceded twice. The counterweight is stated: both clubs’ defensive models are poor (1.97 and 1.74 xG conceded), so the concession side of the ledger argues the other way, and four matches cannot arbitrate.
BTTSWell-suited — both sides at 25%, carried by two independent and unusually firm records
Both clubs read 25.0% against a divisional 57.1%, and each half of the market is held down by a different club. Nice have failed to score in 75.0% of four league matches — the second-highest blank rate on this board, behind Paderborn at 100% — on 0.88 xG and 2.75 shots on target per match, with one goal all season. Lille have kept clean sheets in 75.0% of four matches and conceded only in the PSG draw, continuing a 38.2% BTTS rate across thirty-four matches last season that was the division’s lowest. Both figures also held last season on Lille’s side. The one contrary fact belongs on the card: Nice’s clean-sheet rate last season was 17.6%, the second-worst on this board, so if anyone scores it is more likely to be both.
Team goalsDangerous
Nice’s attacking line has no readable level at all: one goal in four matches, 0.88 xG, 2.75 shots on target from 12.25 shots, and a transfer window that could not be sourced — so it cannot even be established who is supposed to be scoring. Lille’s is readable but in the wrong direction for a goals line: 1.75 goals per match from 1.37 xG on 10.75 shots, down from 13.59, with 1.75 corners won — the lowest on this board — and their two centre-backs replaced numerically. One side’s output is one goal; the other’s is running ahead of a falling model on four matches.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — a remarkable Lille record on four matches against a divisional line it clears easily
Lille have not conceded a first-half goal in any league match this season — 0.00 per match against 0.41 last season — and have scored 1.00 before the break against 0.44, leading at half-time in all four. Nice score 0.25 and concede 0.50. The combined 1.75 first-half goals per match sits above a divisional 1.19, and every goal in it is Lille’s. The first-half result is unusually well evidenced for this board — four from four — but a zero has no variance to measure and reverts on a single goal, and Lille’s 16.01 PPDA is the loosest press here, which is not the profile of a side that keeps first halves quiet. Nice’s 0.25 first-half scoring rate is the more durable half of the row.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — two falling foul counts, a lenient booker, and the second-highest penalty rate on the slate
The card side and the penalty side of this row point in opposite directions and both are well evidenced. On cards: Delajod books 3.12 per match against a divisional 3.91 and allows 8.0 fouls per card against 6.18 — among the longest leashes appointed today — while Nice have cut their fouls from 12.35 to 8.25 and Lille from 10.88 to 11.00 with cards down from 2.41 to 1.25. Four low readings converging. On penalties: 0.53 per match across 17 matches against a divisional 0.36, and five of his eight previous matches involving these two clubs produced one, including a three-penalty match at Monaco involving Nice. This season he has given none in two. The bookings row reads quiet; the penalty row is the live one, and it is the second-highest rate on this slate.
CornersWell-suited — the widest corner asymmetry on the board, both sides inverted with a mechanism
Nice win 6.50 corners and concede 2.50; Lille win 1.75 and concede 6.00 — a gap of 4.75 corners a match, against a divisional 4.79, and Lille’s 1.75 is the lowest corners-won figure of any club anywhere on this board. Both have inverted from last season, when Nice won 5.09 and conceded 5.74 and Lille won 5.71 and conceded 3.79. Both inversions have a stated mechanism: Nice’s possession has risen from 46.3% to 60.7% with deep completions 5.53→7.50, while Lille’s has fallen from 55.8% to 45.1% with shots 13.59→10.75 and deep completions 9.44→7.00. Two clubs, two complete reversals, four supporting series each, all in the direction the possession data predicts. Four matches per side is the limit, and both clubs changed coach in the same window.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Nice’s transfer window could not be sourced at all, and their style has changed more than almost any club on the board. Possession up fourteen points, PPDA down three, corners inverted — under the third head coach in twelve months, at a club that survived a relegation play-off. There is no ledger reached here to say who is executing it.
  • Lille’s defensive record and their defensive model disagree by more than a goal a match. 0.50 conceded against 1.74 xG conceded, with PPDA loosened from 10.17 to 16.01 and both first-choice centre-backs gone at contract end, replaced numerically. Three clean sheets in four is the thinnest kind of defensive evidence.
  • Four league matches is Nice’s entire competitive season. No cup tie, no European fixture, nothing to corroborate. That is the sample-depth reason this fixture was triaged out, and it is unchanged by researching it.
  • Neither club’s team news could be established. On a fixture where one side has scored once all season, whether their centre-forward is fit is not a detail — and no source reached names an absentee either way.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and both team-goals lines — the table says eight points and the models say half a goal, and one attack has produced a single goal. Least sensitive: Corners and BTTS — the first has two complete inversions with four supporting series each, the second has a 75% blank rate on one side and a 75% clean-sheet rate on the other, with a thirty-four-match precedent behind Lille’s half.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because both movements are large and both have possession data behind them. Nice 5.09/5.74 to 6.50/2.50 with possession 46.3%→60.7%; Lille 5.71/3.79 to 1.75/6.00 with possession 55.8%→45.1% and shots 13.59→10.75. The result, by contrast, has to reconcile an eight-point table gap with a half-goal xG gap.
  • BTTS is more robust than Totals because it is carried by two records rather than one. Nice’s 75% blank rate and Lille’s 75% clean-sheet rate are independent, and Lille’s 38.2% BTTS across thirty-four matches last season was the lowest in the division. Totals depends additionally on two poor defensive models (1.97 and 1.74) that argue the other way.
  • Nice’s first-half scoring is more robust than Lille’s first-half clean sheets, despite both being four matches old. Nice score 0.25 before the break on 0.88 xG and one goal all season — a rate with a cause. Lille’s 0.00 conceded sits on a 16.01 PPDA, the loosest press on this board, which is not what usually produces it.
  • The penalty line is the live half of Discipline, and by some distance. Delajod gives 0.53 per match against a divisional 0.36 — five penalties across his eight previous matches involving these two clubs, including three in one Nice match — while his card rate (3.12) and leash (8.0 fouls per card) are both among the most permissive appointed today and both clubs are fouling less than last season.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Lille’s defence is as good as its results or as ordinary as its model. Two goals conceded in four matches against 1.74 xG conceded, with Meunier and Mandi replaced by two numerical signings and the press loosened by six PPDA points. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth match of xG-against data — and Nice, with one goal all season, is the least informative possible test of it.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Fulham finished 11th in 2025–26 on 52 points — 15 wins, 7 draws, 16 defeats, 47 scored, 51 conceded — with a home-away split as wide as any on the slate: 11-2-6 at Craven Cottage, 4-5-10 on the road. Manchester United finished 3rd on 71 points — 20 wins, 11 draws, 7 defeats, 69 scored, 50 conceded — and had the highest both-teams-to-score rate in the division at 71.1%. The structural headline is that both clubs resolved a managerial question and then spent heavily on one unit each, and both are currently below where those investments were meant to put them. Fulham replaced Marco Silva with Álvaro Arbeloa on 7 July 2026 — the same Arbeloa Real Madrid had dismissed on 9 June, five weeks earlier, and whose replacement there, José Mourinho, is managing in the fixture two hours before this one. Manchester United made Michael Carrick permanent after he replaced Rúben Amorim in January 2026 and took 39 points from 17 matches. Fulham then spent £78.1m against nothing, three of the signings following Arbeloa from Real Madrid; United spent £163m against £38m, and £155m of it went on three central midfielders. Fulham are bottom of the table on one point; United are 13th on four.

  • Fulham — turnover very high in both directions; manager changed 7 July 2026. Marco Silva left and Álvaro Arbeloa was appointed on 7 July, having been dismissed by Real Madrid on 9 June. Centre-forward: in — Gonzalo García from Real Madrid, £34.3m, and Jonah Kusi-Asare from Bayern Munich, £5.2m; out — Raúl Jiménez free to Wolves. Central midfield: in — Shea Charles from Southampton, £30m, Manuel Ángel from Real Madrid (undisclosed) and Hugo Larsson on loan from Eintracht Frankfurt; out — Saša Lukić to Ipswich, undisclosed. Attacking midfield: in — César Palacios from Real Madrid, £8.6m; out — Harry Wilson free to Leeds United.   • Manchester United — turnover very high in money and concentrated almost entirely in central midfield; head coach made permanent, not changed. Michael Carrick replaced Rúben Amorim in January 2026, took 39 points from 17 matches and was given a permanent two-year contract with an option before this season — a change of status rather than a change of coach, which is why this fixture does not carry the manager-change flag that most of the slate does. Central midfield, where £155m went: Carlos Baleba from Brighton, £70m, Andrey Santos from Chelsea, £50m and Youri Tielemans from Aston Villa, £35m; out — Casemiro free and Toby Collyer to West Bromwich Albion. Across 22 Premier League matches in 2025–26: 4.00 cards per match (84 yellows, 4 reds), 22.5 fouls per match, 5.6 fouls per card, and 0.27 penalties per match.
