B · Style & early-season reality

Fulham under Arbeloa have become a more expansive side and a worse one, and both halves are visible in the same four matches. Possession is up from 51.7% to 57.1%, deep completions from 7.76 to 11.00, shots from 12.74 to 15.00 and corners won from 4.95 to 6.25 against 3.25 conceded — a club that conceded more corners than it won last season now wins twice as many as it concedes. PPDA has fallen from 12.65 to 10.84, so they press harder too. The attacking model has improved with it: xG from 1.30 to 1.56. What has collapsed is the defence: xG conceded from 1.60 to 2.08, the second-worst figure on this board, and goals conceded from 1.34 to 1.75. The counted record is worse than either: one point from four, with 2–3 and 2–3 home defeats in which they led or were level at the break. A side creating more, pressing more and holding more, and losing. Arbeloa arrived on 7 July with three Real Madrid players behind him and the squad has visibly changed shape; four matches say it has not yet changed results.

United under a permanent Carrick are the more dominant side by every territorial measure and are converting none of it. Shots have risen from 15.68 to 21.00 per match, possession from 51.8% to 60.3%, deep completions from 7.50 to 10.25, xG from 1.90 to 2.12 and PPDA has fallen from 10.83 to 9.56. Against that: 1.75 goals per match against 1.82, 0.25 first-half goals against 0.76, no clean sheet in four, and a failed-to-score rate of 50.0% against 10.5% last season — one of the largest single deteriorations in any counted series on this board. The explanation is at least partly structural rather than statistical: they sold Højlund for £38m and bought no centre-forward, spending £155m on three central midfielders instead, one of whom (Baleba) is a late fitness call and another of whom (Ugarte) is out. Their two heaviest attacking returns came against Ipswich (5–2, 33 shots) and Sabah in Europe (4–0); in the other four competitive matches they have scored four times. The 2–3 EFL Cup exit to Brighton on Wednesday, from 2–0 up, is the most recent data point and the only one in this set that is three days old.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Bottom of the table against 13th, one point against four, with both clubs having spent heavily and neither having made it work. Fulham’s 11th-place record belongs to Marco Silva; United’s third-place record belongs to seventeen matches of an interim Carrick and a departed Amorim between them. The one asymmetry that matters — Fulham’s 11-2-6 home record last season against 4-5-10 away — has already broken this season, with both home matches lost 2–3. United have lost three of six in all competitions and surrendered a 2–0 lead in the cup on Wednesday. The division’s home-win rate is 37.0% against 42.6%.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Both sides’ margins are narrow, and the two clubs’ underlying figures disagree with their results in opposite directions. Fulham’s four are −1, −1, −1, 0 — three one-goal defeats and a goalless draw, from an xG line of 1.56 for and 2.08 against. United’s are −2, +3, 0, −1, from 2.12 and 1.38. United out-create Fulham by 0.56 xG per match and are separated from them by three points; Fulham have lost every match by exactly one goal. A handicap has to price a gap between a side whose model is better than its results and one whose results are worse than its model, on four matches each.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the models say high, the scoreboards say average
The two readings diverge and both are sourced. Modelled: a combined 3.68 xG per match (2.12 and 1.56) against a divisional 3.26, with Fulham’s 2.08 xG conceded the second-worst on this board and United taking 21.00 shots a match. Counted: both clubs sit at exactly 50.0% over 2.5 against a divisional 54.3%, and both have failed to score in half their league matches. Last season the two were 50.0% and 63.2%. The gap has a candidate explanation on each side — United sold their striker and bought midfielders; Fulham have scored four goals in four — and four matches cannot arbitrate. The division is at 2.83 against 2.75, the most stable baseline on the slate, so the reference point at least is firm.
BTTSSplit — strong prior on one side, broken this season on both
United carried the strongest BTTS prior in the division: 71.1% across 38 matches last season against a divisional 56.1%, with a failed-to-score rate of 10.5% and a clean-sheet rate of 21.1% — they scored in almost every match and conceded in most. Half of that has held and half has inverted: they still have no clean sheet in four (0.0%), but they have failed to score in 50% of matches after 10.5%. Fulham are the same shape — 25.0% clean sheets, 50.0% failed to score — so the market now turns on whether either side scores rather than whether either concedes. Both clubs conceding is close to settled; both clubs scoring is the open question, and both have blanked twice in four.
Team goalsSplit — United’s volume is documented, their conversion is the problem
United’s territorial series is emphatic and its output is not: 21.00 shots per match against 15.68, 60.3% possession against 51.8%, 10.25 deep completions against 7.50, 2.12 xG against 1.90 — and 1.75 goals against 1.82, with 0.25 first-half goals against 0.76 and blanks in half their matches. The structural candidate is stated rather than inferred: Højlund sold for £38m, no centre-forward bought, £155m spent on three central midfielders, Ugarte out and Baleba a late call. Fulham’s attacking line is better than last season on every measure (1.30→1.56 xG, 12.74→15.00 shots, 7.76→11.00 deep) and has produced four goals in four. Two sides whose creation and conversion point in opposite directions.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Dangerous
The first-half numbers are low, moving, and contradicted by the way the matches have actually unfolded. United score 0.25 before the break against 0.76 last season — a two-thirds fall — and concede 0.75 against 0.42. Fulham are level at 0.75 and 0.75 against 0.47 and 0.58. The combined 2.50 first-half goals per match is roughly double a divisional 1.26, which pulls one way; United’s collapse in first-half scoring pulls the other. Both of Fulham’s home defeats were 2–3 after being level or ahead at half-time, and United led Brighton 2–0 on Wednesday before losing 2–3 — a pattern of first-half positions not surviving, which is precisely what a first-half market cannot price. Four matches, contradictory signals.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — two quiet teams, a strict official, and a large history gap
The team evidence and the referee evidence disagree, and the referee’s record with these two clubs disagrees with his own average. Both clubs are quiet: Fulham 1.25 cards and 10.25 fouls per match, United 1.50 and 11.00, against a divisional 1.96 per team and 11.40 fouls — and both were similarly quiet last season (2.00/10.92 and 1.71/10.29). Against that, Bankes books 4.00 per match against a divisional 3.86, is running at 4.50 this season, and across his four previous matches involving these two clubs averaged 5.5 cards — including six Chelsea cards in a United match and a penalty in another. The fouls side is structurally quiet on both teams; the card side depends on an official whose record with these clubs runs 40% above his own rate on a four-match history.
CornersOK — both differentials have inverted, one with a clear mechanism
This is the series where both clubs have changed in the same direction, which is unusual. Fulham won 4.95 and conceded 5.53 last season; they now win 6.25 and concede 3.25. United won 4.79 and conceded 4.89; they now win 5.75 and concede 4.00. Both have turned a negative differential positive, against a divisional 4.68. Fulham’s has the fuller mechanism — possession 51.7%→57.1%, deep completions 7.76→11.00, shots 12.74→15.00, PPDA 12.65→10.84 — four columns moving with it; United’s sits on possession 51.8%→60.3% and 21.00 shots. The caution is the usual one: four matches, and Fulham’s figure was accumulated in matches they were losing, which inflates corner counts for the chasing side.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • United sold their centre-forward and did not replace him, and it is the most likely explanation for the one series that has collapsed. Højlund left for £38m; £155m went on Baleba, Santos and Tielemans. Shots are up a third and the failed-to-score rate has gone from 10.5% to 50.0%. Reading last season’s 69 goals into this fixture reads a forward line that no longer exists.
  • Fulham’s home record was the club’s defining asymmetry and it has already broken. 11-2-6 at Craven Cottage last season against 4-5-10 away; this season both home matches lost 2–3, both after being level or ahead at the break. The venue advantage that would normally carry a bottom-placed side is the thing that has stopped working.
  • Both clubs’ underlying numbers are better than last season and both records are worse. Fulham: xG 1.30→1.56, possession +5.4 points, one point from four. United: xG 1.90→2.12, shots 15.68→21.00, four points from four. When the model and the scoreboard disagree this consistently on both sides of a fixture, four matches cannot say which is leading.
  • The most expensive player in this fixture may not play. Baleba (£70m) is a late ankle call and Ugarte is out — two of the central midfield unit that £155m was spent on. De Ligt and Amad Diallo are also out, and Shaw is a late call.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and the halves markets — both clubs have shown they cannot hold a first-half position, and the venue signal that would normally settle the result has inverted. Least sensitive: the corner differentials and the fouls half of discipline — both rest on possession and pressing changes that are visible in four independent columns per club.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because both differentials inverted for reasons the possession data states. Fulham 4.95/5.53 to 6.25/3.25 with possession 51.7→57.1 and deep completions 7.76→11.00; United 4.79/4.89 to 5.75/4.00 with possession 51.8→60.3 and 21.00 shots. The result, meanwhile, needs a home advantage that has produced two 2–3 defeats.
  • The conceding half of BTTS is more robust than the scoring half, which is the reverse of last season. Neither club has kept a clean sheet more than once in four (United none at all), and Fulham’s 2.08 xG conceded is the second-worst on this board. But both have failed to score in half their matches, and United’s blank rate has gone from 10.5% to 50.0% after selling their striker.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and the referee is why. Fulham 10.25 and United 11.00 fouls per match are both near their own 2025–26 figures and just under a divisional 11.40. Bankes converts at exactly the divisional 5.6 fouls per card over 22 matches, but averaged 5.5 cards in his four previous matches involving these two clubs against his own 4.00.
  • A United team-goals line is less robust than their shot count suggests, because the volume and the output have separated. 21.00 shots, 2.12 xG and 10.25 deep completions against 1.75 goals, 0.25 first-half goals and blanks in half their matches — with no centre-forward bought after a £38m sale. The volume is documented; the finisher is the open question.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Carlos Baleba is fit. He is a late ankle call, he cost £70m, Ugarte is already out of the same unit, and United’s entire summer was that unit. A midfield missing two of its three principal pieces changes the shot volume that the totals, team-goals and corner readings all rest on. The confirmed lineup is published an hour before kickoff.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Both clubs are promoted, and this is the only fixture on the slate where that is true of both sides. They came up together from the 2025–26 2. Bundesliga: Schalke as champions on 70 points from 34 matches (21-7-6, 50 scored, 31 conceded), Elversberg as runners-up on 62 (18-8-8, 64 scored, 39 conceded), with Paderborn third on the same points and also in this division. Schalke return after three seasons away; Elversberg are playing top-flight football for the first time in their history, as the 59th club ever to appear in the Bundesliga. The structural headline is that both kept the coach who did it and neither could afford to change much else. Miron Muslić remains at Schalke and Vincent Wagner at Elversberg — Wagner, 40, came from three years with Hoffenheim’s second team and a promotion to the third tier in 2025. Schalke spent almost nothing in fees and took in €200,000 from a single sale; Elversberg invested about €12.1m and sold nobody of consequence. The promoted club with the bigger name is the one that spent less. How the division usually treats sides like these is the relevant frame, and this season it is treating them inconsistently: Elversberg beat Bayer Leverkusen on the opening day and Schalke held Bayern Munich to a goalless draw, while both have conceded at rates well outside anything in their 2. Bundesliga records.

  • Schalke 04 — turnover low by necessity, almost entirely loans and free transfers; no managerial change, Muslić continuing. The window in one line: roughly €2.6m in fees out and €200,000 in, from one sale — one of the most financially constrained promotions in the division. Paid arrivals: Satoshi Tanaka from Fortuna Düsseldorf, €1m; Junior Dina Ebimbe from Eintracht Frankfurt, €800,000; Junior Adamu from SC Freiburg, €800,000. Free and loan: Maximilian Wöber free, Robin Gosens on loan and Hwang Hee-chan on loan — the two most recognisable names in the squad are both borrowed. No goalkeeper and no centre-forward was bought for a fee; the promotion squad is broadly the squad, and the reinforcements are rented. The carried note from this board’s card of 11 September (fx_16_f1) — low turnover by design, largest fee on a defensive midfielder, no managerial change, squad market value €67.33m — is consistent with everything found here.
  • Elversberg — turnover low-to-medium and, for this club, unprecedented in money; no managerial change, Wagner continuing. About €12.1m invested, roughly five times Schalke’s outlay, with no key player sold. The record signing: Cole Campbell from Borussia Dortmund, €6m — the largest fee in the club’s existence. Also in: Maurice Krattenmacher from Bayern Munich, €2m, and Łukasz Poręba from Hamburger SV, €1.6m. A club playing its first top-flight season has outspent a three-time European finalist by a factor of five and kept its promotion side intact.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club was obtainable from any source reached for this fixture, and none is invented. The DFB match record confirms the officials and the kickoff but carries no team news, and neither club’s published preview material reached here names an absentee. This is a genuine gap on a card that otherwise has full data, and it is the one RESEARCH SPEC field this fixture leaves empty. Both clubs have small squads by Bundesliga standards — Schalke’s carries a market value around €67m — so an unreported absence would matter more here than on most cards.
  • Sample. Schalke: four competitive matches — Bundesliga 0–3 at Augsburg (30 August), 0–0 v Bayern Munich (5 September), 3–1 at Union Berlin (11 September); DFB-Pokal 5–2 at Hallescher FC (24 August). Eleventh on 4 points, 3 scored and 4 conceded, and they have failed to score in two of their three league matches. The Bayern match is the single most extreme performance in this dataset: Schalke had 4 shots and 0 corners to Bayern’s 15 and 11, and drew 0–0. Elversberg: four competitive matches — Bundesliga 3–2 v Bayer Leverkusen (29 August), 4–3 at Mönchengladbach (5 September), 1–2 v Bayern Munich (13 September); DFB-Pokal 3–1 at MSV Duisburg (22 August). Sixth-to-eighth on 6 points, 8 scored and 7 conceded, and both teams have scored in every single match. Elversberg beat Leverkusen, who play earlier on this slate, on matchday one.
  • Referee. Dr. Robin Braun, confirmed by the DFB match record, with Patrick Hanslbauer on VAR, Florian Heft and Asmir Osmanagic on the lines and Dr. Florian Exner as fourth official. He has a record in both divisions and they are different. Bundesliga 2025–26, 9 matches: 4.11 cards per match (35 yellows, 2 reds), 22.4 fouls, 5.5 fouls per card, and 0.67 penalties per match — six penalties in nine matches, against a divisional 0.36, the highest penalty rate of any official appointed on this slate. 2. Bundesliga 2025–26, 6 matches: 5.67 cards per match, 25.8 fouls, 4.6 fouls per card, 0.17 penalties — he booked far more often in the division these two clubs came from, which is partly the division (4.65 cards against the Bundesliga’s 3.82) and partly him. This season he has taken one Bundesliga match, Leverkusen 4–0 Union Berlin, with 4 cards in 23 fouls and no penalty. History, and it is exact: Braun refereed this same fixture at this same ground in the 2. Bundesliga on 8 November 2025 — Schalke 04 1–0 Elversberg, six cards, 29 fouls, no penalty. He also took Schalke 2–2 Hannover in March 2026 (with a penalty) and Dynamo Dresden 1–2 Elversberg in February. Fifteen matches across two divisions, three of them involving one of these two clubs.
  • Not sourced. No 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion figure exists for either club — Understat does not cover the 2. Bundesliga, so on this card, uniquely, both sides’ modelled history is missing and three league matches are the entire modelled record for each. No injury or suspension information was obtainable for either club. Fees are undisclosed for Schalke’s loans (Gosens, Hwang) and for their single sale beyond its €200,000 value, and for several of Elversberg’s smaller moves. Nothing is estimated. No source reached characterises in words how Braun reads contact in the box, though his 0.67 penalties per match over nine Bundesliga matches is the most striking counted referee figure on this slate.

B · Style & early-season reality

Schalke have responded to promotion by abandoning the ball almost entirely, and the numbers are the most extreme of their kind on this board. Possession has fallen from 45.8% in the 2. Bundesliga to 34.4%, and PPDA has risen from a pressing figure to 26.88 — meaning they allow nearly twenty-seven opposition passes for every defensive action they attempt. For comparison, the most aggressive side on this slate, Liverpool, sits at 7.06, and no other club carded here is above 16. Everything downstream follows: 7.00 fouls per match, the lowest figure of any club on this board, against a divisional 11.32; 2.67 corners won against 8.67 conceded, the widest negative corner differential here; and 11.33 shots against a divisional 15.58. It is a deep block that does not engage, and against Bayern it produced a 0–0 from four shots and no corners. The cost is in the model rather than the scoreboard so far: 3.17 xG conceded per match, the worst figure on this board, against 1.33 goals actually conceded. Their attacking side is a curiosity — 5.33 shots on target from 11.33 shots, a 47% on-target rate that is the highest here — producing 1.77 xG but only 1.00 goal, and two blanks in three.

Elversberg have done the opposite of adapting and it has been more entertaining and no more solid. Possession has fallen from 53.4% to 36.5% — a bigger absolute drop than Schalke’s — but their PPDA is 16.11, still engaged where Schalke’s is not, and their fouls have risen from 10.88 to 12.00. The result is a side that concedes territory and contests it anyway: 8.33 corners conceded against 3.67 won, and 2.85 xG conceded, the third-worst on this board. What separates them from Schalke is what happens at the other end. Every one of their three league matches has finished with both teams scoring and more than 2.5 goals: 3–2 against Leverkusen, 4–3 at Mönchengladbach, 1–2 against Bayern. They have scored 2.67 per match against a 2. Bundesliga rate of 1.88, from 1.82 xG and only 10.00 shots — a conversion rate that is running well ahead of the chance volume, on three matches. Their 5.67 shots on target from 10.00 shots is a 57% on-target rate, higher even than Schalke’s, which on two clubs at once is more likely to be a three-match artefact than a shared method. Neither club has a 2025–26 xG line to test any of this against.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs’ entire 2025–26 records were made in the 2. Bundesliga, a division scoring 2.93 per match against this one’s 3.97 and booking at 4.65 against 3.82; neither has any modelled history at all. Three league matches each is the joint-thinnest sample on the slate, and the results inside it are wild in both directions: Schalke held Bayern to 0–0 with four shots and lost 0–3 at Augsburg; Elversberg beat Leverkusen and won 4–3 at Mönchengladbach. Schalke won both 2. Bundesliga meetings last season, 1–0 and 2–1, but that was a different division. The Bundesliga home-win rate is 51.5% on 33 matches against 43.8% last season. Nothing here separates them.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Three matches each, and five of the six were decided by one goal or three. Schalke’s run −3, 0, +2; Elversberg’s +1, +1, −1. The underlying figures refuse to separate them either: Schalke 1.77 xG for and 3.17 against, Elversberg 1.82 and 2.85 — the two attacking figures are within 0.05 and the two defensive figures are both catastrophic by divisional standards. A handicap needs a reliable margin between two teams; these two are statistically the same team playing opposite styles, with no prior-season model to break the tie.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — the defensive evidence is overwhelming and it is the division’s strongest suit
This is the clearest structural reading on the card, and it rests on concession rather than creation. These two clubs have the worst and third-worst defensive xG figures on this entire board — 3.17 and 2.85 conceded per match, against a divisional 2.01 — and the counted rates broadly agree: Elversberg concede 2.33 goals per match and have gone over 2.5 in 100% of matches. The Bundesliga itself is at 3.97 goals per match and 81.8% over 2.5, against 3.24 and 63.7% last season, the most extreme divisional figures on this slate, and its xG line has risen with it (1.70 to 2.01), which is what stops the run being read as finishing variance. Against: Schalke are at 66.7% and their 34.4% possession, 26.9 PPDA structure is explicitly built to make matches small — the 0–0 with Bayern is what it looks like when it works. Three matches each is the limit here.
BTTSSplit — one side has hit 100%, the other has blanked in two of three
The two records are at opposite extremes and both are three matches old. Elversberg: both teams have scored in 100% of matches, they have neither kept a clean sheet nor failed to score (0.0% and 0.0%), and they were at 55.9% BTTS in the 2. Bundesliga. Schalke: 33.3% BTTS, and a failed-to-score rate of 66.7% — they have been blanked in two of three, against 17.6% in the 2. Bundesliga. The scoring half of this market turns entirely on Schalke, who generate 1.77 xG but have scored once, and who set out not to have the ball. The conceding half is close to settled: between them these two have conceded 3.17 and 2.85 expected goals per match and Elversberg have not kept a clean sheet at any level of this season.
Team goalsSplit — both sides are converting above their chance volume on three matches
Both attacks are producing more than their shot counts suggest, which is the same warning twice. Elversberg: 2.67 goals per match from 1.82 xG and only 10.00 shots, a 57% shots-on-target rate. Schalke: 1.00 goal from 1.77 xG and 11.33 shots, a 47% on-target rate — one club running well ahead of its model and the other well behind, on the same unusually high on-target ratios. Neither has a 2025–26 xG series to compare against, because the 2. Bundesliga has none. What is firm is the volume, and it is low on both sides: 10.00 and 11.33 shots per match against a divisional 15.58, the two lowest figures on this board.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — two opposite first halves, both three matches old
Elversberg score 1.00 and concede 1.00 before the break; Schalke score 0.33 and concede 0.33. The combined 2.67 first-half goals per match sits above a divisional 1.45, but the entire excess comes from one side, and Schalke’s 0.33/0.33 is the second-lowest combined first-half figure on this board. Both clubs were higher in the 2. Bundesliga (Schalke 0.76/0.35, Elversberg 0.82/0.56), so Schalke’s has more than halved and Elversberg’s has risen at both ends. The first-half result is not readable: Schalke’s deep block produced a goalless first half against Bayern and a 1–0 lead at Union Berlin from a structure that does not chase the game early.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — a wide foul gap between two teams, meeting a strict short-leash official
The two sides give this referee opposite material and the referee is the more interesting half. Schalke commit 7.00 fouls per match — the lowest figure of any club on this board, against a divisional 11.32 — which is the direct consequence of a 26.88 PPDA: a team that never engages cannot foul. Elversberg commit 12.00, up from 10.88 in the 2. Bundesliga. Card rates are 1.33 and 2.00 per team against a divisional 1.84. Braun is strict on both axes and stricter still in the division these clubs came from: 4.11 cards and 5.5 fouls per card in the Bundesliga, 5.67 and 4.6 in the 2. Bundesliga, and he produced six cards in 29 fouls when he took this exact fixture at this ground in November. Both clubs’ 2. Bundesliga card rates were set in a division booking at 4.65 per match against this one’s 3.67, so their prior figures overstate rather than understate.
CornersWell-suited — the most one-directional series on the card, with a stated mechanism on both sides
Both clubs sit at the same extreme and it is the extreme their styles predict. Schalke win 2.67 corners and concede 8.67; Elversberg win 3.67 and concede 8.33 — the two lowest corners-won and two highest corners-conceded figures on this entire board, against a divisional 5.42. The mechanism is documented for each: Schalke at 34.4% possession and 26.88 PPDA, Elversberg at 36.5% and 16.11, with 11.33 and 10.00 shots per match, the two lowest shot counts here. Both were roughly neutral in the 2. Bundesliga (Schalke 4.82/4.47, Elversberg 6.15/4.12), so the inversion is a division effect with two independent clubs showing it simultaneously. Three matches each is the caveat — but the same signal appearing in two separate clubs, with possession and shot data behind both, is the strongest form this board’s evidence takes at a three-match sample.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both clubs’ entire prior records are second-tier records, and on this card, uniquely, neither has any xG history at all. Understat covers no 2. Bundesliga, so Schalke’s 1.77/3.17 and Elversberg’s 1.82/2.85 have nothing to be measured against. The division they came from scored 2.93 per match and booked at 4.65; this one scores 3.97 and books at 3.67.
  • No team news exists for this fixture in anything reached. Neither an injury nor a suspension could be sourced for either club. On squads this small — Schalke’s market value is around €67m and two of their most recognisable players are on loan — a single unreported absence is a larger proportion of the side than on any other card here.
  • Schalke’s style is the most extreme on the board and three matches old. 34.4% possession, 26.88 PPDA, 7.00 fouls, 2.67 corners won. It has produced one point-earning 0–0 against Bayern from four shots, one 0–3 and one 3–1 win. Whether that is a plan or a survival reflex cannot be told from three matches, and the 3.17 xG conceded suggests the scoreboard has flattered it.
  • Both attacks are converting above their chance volume. Elversberg have 2.67 goals from 1.82 xG; the on-target rates of 57% and 47% are the two highest on this board and neither has a prior-season figure to anchor it. Goals-per-match on either side is the least durable number on this card.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and any Schalke team-goals line — two statistically indistinguishable promoted sides with no modelled history, and a home side that has been blanked twice in three. Least sensitive: Corners — the same extreme signal in two independent clubs, each with possession, PPDA and shot data to explain it, and both inverted from neutral second-tier figures.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because the same signal appears independently in both clubs and both have a mechanism. Schalke 2.67 won and 8.67 conceded at 34.4% possession and 26.88 PPDA; Elversberg 3.67 and 8.33 at 36.5% and 16.11. Both were near-neutral in the 2. Bundesliga. The result, by contrast, has to separate two sides whose xG figures differ by 0.05.
  • The conceding half of Totals is more robust than the scoring half, and it is what carries the family. 3.17 and 2.85 xG conceded are the two worst figures on this board against a divisional 2.01, and the Bundesliga is running at 3.97 goals per match with its own xG line up from 1.70 to 2.01. The scoring half depends on two attacks converting above their volume from 10.00 and 11.33 shots.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and Schalke’s figure is the reason. 7.00 fouls per match is the lowest on this board and it is a direct arithmetic consequence of a 26.88 PPDA — a side that does not engage cannot foul. Turning that into a card count runs through Braun, who books 4.11 per match and allowed 5.5 fouls per card in the Bundesliga but 4.6 in the division these two just left.
  • Any penalty-dependent line is the one place this official is the dominant input. Braun awarded six penalties in nine Bundesliga matches last season — 0.67 per match against a divisional 0.36, the highest rate of any referee appointed on this slate — though he gave none in his one match this season, and none when he took this exact fixture in November.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Schalke keep playing at 34% possession against a side that also does not want the ball. Their 26.88 PPDA was set against Augsburg, Bayern and Union Berlin, all of whom take the ball; Elversberg have 36.5% possession themselves. Two teams built to defend deep meeting each other is the one configuration none of the three matches tested, and it bears directly on the corner and totals readings. What would settle it is a fourth and fifth match of possession and PPDA data — today’s fixture is itself the first test.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Juventus finished 6th in 2025–26 on 69 points — 19 wins, 12 draws, 7 defeats, 61 scored, 34 conceded — and went over 2.5 goals in only 39.5% of matches against a divisional 45.8%. Atalanta finished 7th on 59 points — 15 wins, 14 draws, 9 defeats, 51 scored, 36 conceded — with a 36.8% over-2.5 rate, the lowest of the two and among the lowest in the division. The structural headline is that one club changed manager in mid-season and the other in the summer, and the two changes have produced opposite effects on the same measures. Luciano Spalletti was appointed at Juventus on 30 October 2025 after Igor Tudor was sacked with the club 8th, and took 15 wins, 9 draws and 5 defeats from the remainder — so his record at the club now runs to a full season and is not a four-match sample. Maurizio Sarri left Lazio for Atalanta, announced on 15 June 2026 on a three-year contract to June 2029, the same move that put Gennaro Gattuso in the Lazio dugout. Juventus made nine signings and Atalanta’s principal addition arrived on a loan; the triage note that placed this fixture out was not about either club’s structure but about Juventus having received a full card three days ago (fx_0917_f6).

  • Juventus — turnover high, nine signings, manager changed 30 October 2025 and now eleven months in post. Igor Tudor was sacked on 27 October 2025 with the club 8th; Luciano Spalletti was appointed on 30 October and went 15-9-5 in the league afterwards — carried from this board’s card of 17 September (fx_0917_f6) rather than re-sourced. Centre-forward: in — Nick Woltemade on loan from Newcastle United, loan fee €4m. Midfield: in — Pape Matar Sarr on loan, loan fee €2m. Nine new signings in total under Spalletti, with the two principal attacking and midfield additions both arriving on paid loans rather than permanent transfers — an unusual structure for a club of this size and one that limits how much of the squad is contractually theirs. Also on this slate: João Mário left on loan to Fiorentina for a €1.8m fee and Michele Di Gregorio on loan to Bournemouth, both recorded from the receiving clubs’ sides on cards f1 and f4. Fees for the other seven arrivals were not obtainable and are not estimated.
  • Atalanta — turnover with heavy defensive outflow and no published headline fee; manager changed 15 June 2026. Maurizio Sarri left Lazio for Atalanta, announced on 15 June 2026 on a contract to June 2029 — carried from this board’s card of 31 August (fx_11_f3), which also recorded the window as heavy defensive outflow with no published fee anywhere. In, the one addition established here: Jonathan Rowe from Bologna, on a €5m loan. No further itemised 2026 ledger with fees for Atalanta was obtainable from any source reached, which leaves the transfer half of the spec thin on this side; nothing is estimated. A club that conceded 36 goals under one manager has changed to a coach whose Lazio sides were built on possession, and its principal summer addition is a loan.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club was obtainable from any source reached for this fixture, and none is invented. The AIA designations confirm the official and the kickoff but carry no team news. What is on record is recent workload: both clubs played on Thursday, three days ago — Juventus won 5–0 against NEC Nijmegen in the Europa League at home, and Atalanta completed a Conference League play-off tie against Hapoel Tel Aviv earlier in the window (0–0 and 1–0). This board’s card of 17 September (fx_0917_f6) recorded Juventus as depleted by injuries and carrying a replaced front line; that was three days ago and no update was obtainable.

Eleventh on 6 points, 5 scored and 5 conceded, with three of their four league matches at home and two straight home defeats to close.   • Referee. Luca Zufferli of the Udine section, confirmed in the AIA designations published on 16 September. Across 18 Serie A matches in 2025–26: 3.00 cards per match (53 yellows, 1 red), 24.5 fouls per match, 8.2 fouls per card, and 0.50 penalties per match. The profile is sharply drawn at both ends. On booking he is the most permissive official appointed anywhere on this slate: 3.00 cards against a 2025–26 divisional 3.77 and a 2026–27 divisional 3.22, and 8.2 fouls per card against 6.72 — he allows about a fifth more play between bookings than the Serie A average, on a foul count slightly above it. On penalties he is the opposite and the most extreme on the slate: 0.50 per match, nine awards in eighteen matches, against a divisional 0.28 last season and 0.11 this. This season he has taken one match, Torino 1–2 Milan on 23 August, with a single card in 19 fouls. History, and an unusual amount of it: three Juventus matches last season (0–0 v Torino, 0–3 at Sassuolo, 2–2 at Torino) and two Atalanta matches (1–1 v Como, 2–3 at Milan with a penalty). Across those five he averaged 2.6 cards, below even his own rate.

B · Style & early-season reality

Juventus have taken an already controlling side and pushed it to the edge of the dataset. PPDA has fallen from 10.58 to 8.19 — a 23% increase in pressing intensity — on possession barely moved at 57.1% against 57.5%, with shots up from 16.82 to 18.50. The territorial consequence is the most extreme single figure on the board: 9.75 corners won per match against 2.25 conceded, a +7.50 differential, having been +1.71 last season. They took 12 corners at Frosinone, 10 at home to Parma, 7 against Milan and 10 at Sassuolo. The defensive model is correspondingly severe: 0.40 xG conceded per match, the best figure of any club anywhere on this board, against 0.95 last season — though the counted rate has gone the other way, 0.89 to 1.00, because three of the four goals they have conceded came in one match at Sassuolo. The cost is in fouls: 16.00 per match against 12.13 last season and a divisional 11.21, the highest figure on this board — which is what a press at 8.19 PPDA produces. And the scoring has gone quiet at the start of matches: 0.25 first-half goals against 0.63, with none conceded before the break at all.

Atalanta under Sarri have moved in exactly the opposite direction on every one of those measures. PPDA has risen from 10.63 to 21.55 — the most passive figure of any club on this board bar Schalke, and a doubling of the number of opposition passes allowed per defensive action — while possession has fallen from 55.2% to 48.1% and deep completions from 9.34 to 6.00. The attacking model has more than halved: 0.84 xG per match against 1.81 last season, the fourth-lowest on this board, on shots down from 14.79 to 12.00. The defensive model has gone the same way: 2.20 xG conceded against 1.36. The corner column matches: 2.25 won against 5.68 last season and 5.50 conceded against 4.21. Their card rate is the third-lowest on this board at 0.75 per match, on 9.50 fouls — a side that does not press does not foul — and against Roma they were out-shot 39–6. Two clubs that finished 6th and 7th with almost identical records have, in one summer, arrived at opposite ends of every territorial series in this dataset.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Atalanta changed manager in June and their underlying numbers have collapsed on both sides of the ball — xG 1.81 to 0.84, xG conceded 1.36 to 2.20 — while their points are 6 from 4. Juventus have a manager eleven months in post, which is unusually settled for this slate, but nine new signings and two of their principal additions on loan. The counted separation is one point; the modelled separation is about two and a half goals per match, the widest on this board. Serie A’s home-win rate is 40.0% against 38.9%. The two previous meetings in the record were 1–1 and 0–1, both tight, both under different coaches at Atalanta. Four matches each cannot arbitrate a gap that wide between the two sources.
Asian handicap / DNBSplit — the widest modelled gap on the board sitting on the narrowest counted one
Unusually for this slate there is a real directional signal, and it belongs entirely to the models. Juventus 1.83 xG for and 0.40 against; Atalanta 0.84 and 2.20 — a combined modelled margin of about 2.4 goals a match, by far the widest on this board. The counted margins refuse to follow: Juventus +1, +2, 0, −1 and Atalanta +1, +1, −1, −1, with one point between the clubs. Atalanta’s two most recent league matches were both home defeats, and at Roma they were out-shot 39–6. A handicap can read the direction here more confidently than on almost any other card; what it cannot read is the level, because the scoreboard has ignored the model for four matches running.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — two low full-season rates against two poor current defensive models
The two seasons disagree and both are sourced. Counted, last season: Juventus 39.5% over 2.5 and Atalanta 36.8%, against a divisional 45.8% — two of the lowest rates in the division across thirty-eight matches each. Counted, this season: Juventus 25.0% and Atalanta 75.0%, and between them their eight league matches have produced 22 goals. Modelled, this season: a combined 2.67 xG per match against a divisional 3.42 — below average — because Juventus’s 0.40 xG conceded and Atalanta’s 0.84 xG for cancel most of the total. The division has moved from 2.43 goals a game to 2.96. Two full-season low rates and a below-average combined model point one way; Atalanta’s 75% and a 2.20 xG conceded point the other.
BTTSSplit — both sides score reliably, only one concedes reliably
The scoring half is settled and the conceding half is not. Neither club has failed to score in a league match this season — 0.0% each, against 21.1% for both last season — which is the firmest shared fact on this card. The conceding half splits hard: Juventus have kept clean sheets in 50.0% of matches on 0.40 xG conceded, the best defensive model on the board; Atalanta in 25.0% on 2.20 xG conceded. Last season the two clubs read 44.7% and 52.6% BTTS against a divisional 45.3%, so neither carries a strong prior. The market therefore turns almost entirely on whether an Atalanta attack producing 0.84 xG per match scores against the best defensive model on this board.
Team goalsSplit — Juventus’s volume is documented, Atalanta’s has halved
Juventus’s attacking series is coherent and slightly below its own record: 18.50 shots per match against 16.82 last season, 6.00 on target against 5.97, 9.00 deep completions against 10.18, 1.83 xG against 1.95 — more shots, marginally worse chances — producing 1.50 goals against 1.61, with the centre-forward on loan. Atalanta’s has collapsed: 0.84 xG against 1.81, shots 12.00 against 14.79, deep completions 6.00 against 9.34, corners 2.25 against 5.68 — four columns more than halved or close to it — while producing 1.25 goals against 1.34. One team-goals line is readable and stable; the other is a side scoring materially above a model that has fallen by more than half.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited on first-half goals — the lowest combined figure on this board
Juventus score 0.25 before the break and have conceded none at all in four league matches; Atalanta score 0.50 and concede 0.25 — a combined 1.00 first-half goal per match, the lowest of any fixture on this entire board, against a divisional 1.16. Both clubs have fallen from their own prior seasons (Juventus 0.63 scored, Atalanta 0.63), and Juventus’s 0.00 conceded continues a 0.39 rate that was already among the division’s best. The mechanism is on the page for both: Juventus press at 8.19 PPDA and concede 0.40 xG; Atalanta sit at 21.55 PPDA and produce 0.84 xG. Four separate series, two clubs, one direction — and the zero, as always, has no variance to measure and reverts on a single goal.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — the widest foul gap on the board meeting the most permissive whistle on it
The two clubs give this official completely opposite material and the official converts less of it than anyone appointed today. Juventus commit 16.00 fouls per match — the second-highest figure on this board behind Frosinone’s 16.50, against 12.13 last season and a divisional 11.21 — with PPDA 10.58→8.19 to explain it. Atalanta commit 9.50 and take 0.75 cards, the lowest card rate here, with PPDA 10.63→21.55 to explain that. Zufferli books 3.00 per match against a divisional 3.22 this season and 3.77 last, and allows 8.2 fouls per card against 6.72 — the longest leash on the slate — and produced 2.6 cards a match across his five previous fixtures involving these clubs. The penalty row is the live one: 0.50 per match, nine in eighteen, against a divisional 0.11 this season.
CornersWell-suited — the largest corner asymmetry anywhere on this board, mechanically explained on both sides
Juventus win 9.75 corners per match and concede 2.25 — a +7.50 differential, the largest on this board by a distance. Atalanta win 2.25 and concede 5.50, a −3.25. Against a divisional 4.78, that is two clubs at opposite extremes of the same column, and both moved there this summer: Juventus from 5.47 and 3.76, Atalanta from 5.68 and 4.21. Both movements have full mechanisms: Juventus’s PPDA 10.58→8.19 and shots 16.82→18.50; Atalanta’s PPDA 10.63→21.55, possession 55.2%→48.1%, deep completions 9.34→6.00 and shots 14.79→12.00. The individual matches are as lopsided as the averages: Juventus 12–3 at Frosinone and 10–2 v Parma; Atalanta 1–14 at Roma. Four matches each is the caveat.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Atalanta’s underlying numbers have halved in one direction and doubled in the other, and their points have barely moved. xG 1.81→0.84, xG conceded 1.36→2.20, PPDA 10.63→21.55, corners 5.68→2.25 — four collapses under a manager appointed on 15 June — producing 6 points from 4. One of those two records is misleading and four matches cannot say which.
  • Juventus’s 0.40 xG conceded is the best defensive model on the board and their counted rate is worse than last season. 1.00 goals conceded against 0.89, with three of the four coming at Sassuolo. A defensive reading here depends on which of those two figures is believed.
  • No team news exists for either club. Juventus were recorded three days ago, on this board’s own card, as depleted by injuries with a replaced front line; no update was obtainable, and their centre-forward is on loan. Atalanta’s position is unknown entirely.
  • Both clubs played on Thursday. Juventus won 5–0 at home in the Europa League three days ago; that match is outside every league rate on this card and is their most recent evidence.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2 and both team-goals lines — one point separates two clubs whose models differ by about two and a half goals a match. Least sensitive: Corners and first-half goals — both rest on gaps far larger than their own variance, with pressing, possession, shot and deep-completion data agreeing on the mechanism at both ends.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because the asymmetry is seven and a half corners wide and every supporting series agrees. Juventus 9.75 won to 2.25 conceded with PPDA 8.19; Atalanta 2.25 to 5.50 with PPDA 21.55 and possession down seven points. The result, by contrast, has to reconcile a one-point table gap with the widest modelled gap on the board.
  • First-half goals is more robust than full-time totals because it is the one place both clubs and both models agree. A combined 1.00 per match, the lowest on this board; Juventus have conceded none before the break; both clubs have fallen from their own prior figures. Full-time totals has two low full-season rates pointing one way and Atalanta’s 75% and 2.20 xG conceded pointing the other.
  • The scoring half of BTTS is more robust than the conceding half. Neither club has failed to score in a league match this season, against 21.1% each last season. But Atalanta’s 0.84 xG has to get past the best defensive model on this board, and Juventus have kept clean sheets in half their matches.
  • The penalty line is the live part of Discipline, not the card line. Zufferli gave nine penalties in eighteen matches — 0.50 per match against a divisional 0.11 this season — while booking at 3.00 per match with an 8.2 fouls-per-card leash, the most permissive appointed today, into a fixture where Juventus foul 16.00 times a match and Atalanta take 0.75 cards.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Atalanta’s 21.55 PPDA is Sarri’s plan or a symptom. It is more than double last season’s figure, it sits underneath the corner, team-goals and first-half readings simultaneously, and it has coincided with their xG halving and their xG conceded rising by two-thirds. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth match of pressing data, or a published Atalanta team sheet showing whether the defensive outflow this board recorded in August has been replaced — and that ledger is exactly what could not be sourced.

César Soto Grado (Rioja) · 4.84 cards pg, 4.7 fouls per card, 0.37 penalties pg (LaLiga 25/26, 19 matches) · 6.50 cards pg and 3.4 fouls per card this season (2 matches) · confirmed, VAR José López Toca

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Deportivo are promoted and are back in the top flight after a long absence — they were out of LaLiga in 2024–25 and 2023–24 and had fallen as far as the third tier in the interim. They came up as runners-up in LaLiga 2 on 77 points from 42 matches (22-11-9, 65 scored, 44 conceded), behind Racing Santander on 82 and ahead of Almería and Málaga, both of whom are also in this division now. Real Betis finished 5th in 2025–26 on 60 points — 15 wins, 15 draws, 8 defeats, 59 scored, 48 conceded — and are in the Champions League for the first time in 21 years. The structural headline is that both clubs kept their coach and then spent in opposite proportions relative to their size. Antonio Hidalgo’s contract renewed automatically on promotion and he continues at Deportivo; Manuel Pellegrini continues at Betis, which makes this one of the few fixtures on the slate where both clubs’ last-season records belong to the men picking the team — though one of those records was made in a division below. Deportivo then recruited ten senior players, including a €9m goalkeeper and a former Champions League-winning forward, while Betis’s window is not obtainable in any source reached.

  • Deportivo — turnover very high in bodies and unusually aggressive for a promoted club; no managerial change, Hidalgo continuing after an automatic renewal. Goalkeeper, the largest fee: Leo Román from RCD Mallorca, €9m — a promoted side’s biggest outlay spent on the one position the spec weights most heavily. Centre-forward: Pierre-Emerick Aubameyang from Marseille, reported at just over €1m — from a club playing later on this same slate. Centre-back: José María Giménez, bringing international experience to a promoted back line. Midfield: Lorenzo Amatucci from Fiorentina (also on this slate), Marc Casadó, Jonathan Asp Jensen from Bayern Munich II, Teun Gijselhart from De Graafschap. Wide: Adama Traoré, and Angeliño on loan from AS Roma — who is suspended for this match. Bright Ede from Motor Lublin completes the ten. Five of the arrivals are young or developmental and four — Aubameyang, Angeliño, Adama Traoré, Giménez — are experienced internationals, which is an unusually barbelled promotion window.
  • Real Betis — no managerial change, Pellegrini continuing; transfer window not obtainable. Pellegrini remains, so Betis are one of very few clubs on this slate whose complete 2025–26 record — 60 points, 59 scored, 48 conceded, and a remarkable 15 draws in 38 — was produced by the coach selecting today’s team. No source reached publishes Betis’s summer 2026 ins and outs with fees, so the transfer half of the RESEARCH SPEC is not satisfied for this club and nothing is estimated — the named-signings requirement is met for Deportivo and not for Betis. What is established is the competitive context: they qualified for the Champions League for the first time since 2005 and opened it by winning 2–3 at Lille on 8 September, a club that also appears on this slate.
  • Suspensions / absences. Deportivo are without Angeliño, sent off in the 0–1 home defeat to Sevilla on Wednesday and suspended for this fixture — a loan signing from Roma and one of the four experienced arrivals the promotion window was built around. No further injury list was obtainable for either club from any source reached, and none is invented. Deportivo have taken 2 red cards in six league matches, the joint-most of any club carded on this slate, which makes the suspension a recurring rather than isolated cost.
  • Sample. Deportivo: six competitive matches, all league — 1–1 v Elche (17 August), 1–1 at Málaga (24 August, a club on this slate), 3–1 v Valencia (30 August, also on this slate), 3–2 at Villarreal (5 September, also on this slate), 1–1 at Getafe (13 September, also on this slate), 0–1 v Sevilla (16 September). Seventh-to-ninth on 9 points from 2 wins and 3 draws, 9 scored and 7 conceded — and they have not kept a clean sheet in any of the six. Five of their six opponents so far appear on this slate. Betis: seven competitive matches — LaLiga 1–0 v Real Sociedad (21 August), 1–0 at Valencia (25 August), 2–5 at Levante (29 August), 1–0 v Real Madrid (4 September), 2–1 at Villarreal (14 September), 1–0 v Getafe (17 September); Champions League 3–2 at Lille (8 September). Third on 15 points from five wins in six, 8 scored and 6 conceded — and four of those five wins were 1–0. Their three home league matches have finished 1–0, 1–0 and 1–0: three matches, three goals, three clean sheets. The one defeat was 2–5 at Levante, which accounts for five of the six goals they have conceded.
  • Referee. César Soto Grado of the Rioja committee, confirmed, with José López Toca of Cantabria on VAR. He is the heaviest-booking official appointed anywhere on this slate. Across 19 LaLiga matches in 2025–26: 4.84 cards per match (84 yellows and 8 reds), 22.7 fouls per match, 4.7 fouls per card, and 0.37 penalties per match. Against the division that is strict on cards and close to average on penalties: 4.84 cards against 4.52, 4.7 fouls per card against 5.57 — he books roughly one foul in five, the shortest leash on this slate — and 0.37 penalties against 0.35. The eight red cards in nineteen matches are the notable figure. This season he is running well above even that: 6.50 cards per match and 3.4 fouls per card across two matches, with no penalty. Two matches is a small sample and is reported as such, but it points the same way as nineteen. History: one Betis match in the record, a 1–1 home draw with Real Madrid in April 2026 with three cards, and no Deportivo match at this level — Deportivo were in LaLiga 2. He has previously refereed at Riazor and is described in the local press as a familiar face there.
  • Not sourced. Real Betis’s entire summer 2026 transfer ledger — ins, outs and fees — was not obtainable from any source reached, which is the single largest RESEARCH SPEC gap on this card. No 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion figure exists for Deportivo, because Understat does not cover LaLiga 2; nothing is estimated. Fees are undisclosed for Adama Traoré, Marc Casadó, José María Giménez and Amatucci into Deportivo, and for Deportivo’s outgoings, which were not obtainable at all. No injury list beyond Angeliño’s suspension was found for either club. No source characterises in words how Soto Grado reads contact in the box.

B · Style & early-season reality

Deportivo have come up and become the least productive attacking side on this board, and every column says the same thing. 7.83 shots per match — the lowest figure of any club carded on this slate, against a divisional 12.1 — 3.33 on target, 5.33 deep completions, and an xG of 1.08, also the lowest here. Possession is 47.1%, down from 51.1% in LaLiga 2, and PPDA is 13.80, a passive figure. And yet they have scored 1.50 per match, comfortably above their expected 1.08 and above their own 1.55 second-tier rate at a much higher level. Nine goals from 6.5 expected across six matches is the widest positive over-performance on this card, and it has been produced with Aubameyang leading a line behind the fewest shots in the division. At the other end they have conceded in every single match — a 0% clean-sheet rate against 26.2% in LaLiga 2 — from 1.58 xG against and 5.67 corners conceded per match. Their discipline has risen sharply: 13.50 fouls per match against 11.81 in the second tier, and two red cards in six. There is no 2025–26 modelled record to test any of this against.

Betis are the mirror image: enormous chance volume, almost no goals, and a clean-sheet record built on it. 18.50 shots per match against 14.24 last season, 6.83 on target against 4.84, deep completions 6.90 to 10.17, and xG from 1.61 to 2.03 — four series all up sharply under an unchanged coach, on possession that has moved only 50.1% to 53.6% and PPDA that has not moved at all (12.77 to 12.76). The output is 1.33 goals per match, down from 1.55: eight goals from 12.2 expected, among the widest negative gaps on this board. Four 1–0 wins, three of them at home, where they have scored three goals in three matches and conceded none. The defensive record is equally lopsided: 66.7% clean sheets against 26.3% last season, but an xG conceded that has gone the wrong way, 1.32 to 1.81, and a corner concession of 6.50 per match against 5.18. Five of their six league goals conceded came in a single match, the 2–5 at Levante. A side creating far more and scoring less, conceding far more chances and shipping fewer goals — both halves of their record are running against their own model.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Deportivo’s entire prior record is a LaLiga 2 record with no modelled component at all, and their current one is nine goals from 6.5 expected. Betis are third on 15 points from eight goals and 12.2 expected — five wins in six, four of them 1–0. Both clubs’ results are running hard against their own underlying numbers, in opposite directions, which is the configuration a result market reads worst. Deportivo are missing Angeliño to suspension; Betis played in Lille twelve days ago and have a Champions League calendar attached. LaLiga’s home-win rate is 43.8% against 48.9%.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Betis’s margins are the narrowest of any club on this board: +1, +1, −3, +1, +1, +1 — five of six decided by exactly one goal, four of them 1–0. Deportivo’s are 0, 0, +2, +1, 0, −1 — three draws and nothing wider than two. Between them, ten of twelve league matches finished within one goal. That distribution makes the handicap hardest to price at precisely the line it would be set at, and the DNB variant removes an outcome Deportivo have produced in half their matches.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited on the counted record — with an explicit warning that the models disagree
The counted evidence is the strongest on this card and unusually concordant. Both clubs sit at 33.3% over 2.5 across six league matches — among the lowest pairs on this board — against a divisional 56.2% that has itself risen from 50.0%. Between them they have produced 17 goals in twelve matches, and Betis’s six league matches contain four 1–0s and three goalless home halves of the ledger. Deportivo take 7.83 shots a match, the fewest here. The warning belongs on the card: the combined xG is 3.11 per match against a divisional 3.24, so the models say “average” where the scoreboards say “low” — and Betis’s 2.03 xG has produced 1.33 goals. Six matches each, two clubs, one direction on the counted series.
BTTSSplit — the two records are at opposite extremes and both have a mechanism
Deportivo: 83.3% BTTS, a 0.0% clean-sheet rate — they have conceded in all six league matches — and a 16.7% failed-to-score rate. Betis: 33.3% BTTS, 66.7% clean sheets, 0.0% failed to score. Both are large movements from their own priors (Deportivo 59.5% in LaLiga 2; Betis 68.4% last season, the second-highest in the division). The deciding question is whether Betis’s clean sheets are real: their xG conceded has risen from 1.32 to 1.81 and they concede 6.50 corners per match, so the model says the clean sheets are running ahead of the defending. Deportivo’s side is firmer — six matches, six concessions, and no clean sheet at any point this season.
Team goalsSplit — both sides’ goals are detached from their chance volume, in opposite directions
This is the clearest case on the slate of conversion running away from creation on both sides at once. Deportivo: 9 goals from 6.5 xG, on 7.83 shots per match — the lowest volume on this board — with no prior-season xG to anchor it. Betis: 8 goals from 12.2 xG, on 18.50 shots and 6.83 on target, up from 14.24 and 4.84 under the same coach. One of those two gaps will close and nothing here says which or when. What is firm is the volume: Betis create more than twice as much as Deportivo by every measure — shots, on-target, deep completions, xG — and have scored fewer goals.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — identical low figures on both sides, six matches each
The two clubs have produced exactly the same first-half line: 0.83 scored and 0.50 conceded each, a combined 2.67 first-half goals per match against a divisional 1.42. That combined figure is high, but it is the sum of two modest halves rather than one extreme one, and both clubs are close to their own priors (Deportivo 0.67/0.52 in LaLiga 2; Betis 0.68/0.71). Betis’s first-half record is the more informative: three home matches finishing 1–0 with the goal arriving in the first half in one of them, and a 2–2 at half-time in the Levante defeat. The first-half result inherits the 1X2 objections and is not readable.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited — the most extreme official on the slate meeting the more physical of two sides
Every input here is independently sourced and they converge. Soto Grado books 4.84 per match across 19 matches against a divisional 4.52, allows only 4.7 fouls per card against 5.57 — the shortest leash of any official appointed today — and showed 8 red cards in those 19 matches. This season he is at 6.50 cards and 3.4 fouls per card across two matches. Deportivo are the more exposed side: 13.50 fouls per match against a divisional 12.41 and against their own 11.81 in LaLiga 2, 2.00 cards per team, and two red cards in six matches — one of which, Angeliño’s, has them a player short of their first-choice side today. Betis are quiet at 9.83 fouls and 1.50 cards, as they were last season (10.63 and 1.95). A promoted side fouling above the divisional rate, with a red-card habit already established, in front of the strictest whistle on the slate, is as many agreeing inputs as this board has produced.
CornersOK — both clubs concede more than they win, which is unusual and consistent
Deportivo win 3.83 and concede 5.67; Betis win 4.67 and concede 6.50 — against a divisional 4.82. Both clubs have a negative differential, which only three of the clubs carded on this slate manage. Betis’s is continuous with last season (4.55 won, 5.18 conceded) despite their shot count rising from 14.24 to 18.50, which is itself odd and worth flagging: more shots, fewer corners relative to the opponent. Deportivo’s has inverted from LaLiga 2 (5.07 won, 4.33 conceded), which is the expected direction for a promoted side. The combined concession of 12.17 corners per match between them is among the highest totals on this board, and unlike that fixture, one side of it has 38 prior matches behind it.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Deportivo’s entire 2025–26 record is a LaLiga 2 record and its modelled half does not exist. 1.55 goals per match and 50.0% over 2.5 were produced in a division scoring 2.63 per game and booking at 5.31. Understat does not cover LaLiga 2, so six top-flight matches are the only modelled evidence for this club.
  • Betis’s transfer window could not be sourced at all. No ins, outs or fees were obtainable from any source reached, so the squad-change half of the RESEARCH SPEC is unsatisfied for one of the two clubs. Their 2025–26 record is attributed to Pellegrini, who continues — but whether the squad producing it is intact is not established here.
  • Both clubs’ results are detached from their models, in opposite directions and by similar amounts. Deportivo 9 goals from 6.5 expected; Betis 8 from 12.2. Betis’s clean-sheet rate has risen from 26.3% to 66.7% while their xG conceded rose from 1.32 to 1.81. Three separate counted series are running against three separate modelled ones across this one fixture.
  • Five of Betis’s six league goals conceded came in one match. The 2–5 at Levante is a single result carrying most of their defensive record; remove it and they have conceded once in five. Any defensive rate quoted for them is one match away from a different number.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and any team-goals line — ten of twelve league matches between them finished within a goal, and both attacks are converting at rates their shot counts do not support. Least sensitive: Discipline — a 19-match referee record, a two-match confirmation of it, a promoted side fouling above the divisional rate, and a red card already served.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Discipline is more robust than 1X2 here because the referee’s record is the longest series anywhere on this card. Soto Grado has 19 matches at 4.84 cards and 4.7 fouls per card with 8 reds, and two more this season at 6.50 and 3.4. Deportivo foul at 13.50 per match against a divisional 12.41 and have two reds in six. The result, by contrast, rests on two clubs whose goals and expected goals disagree by roughly four goals each over six matches.
  • The counted side of Totals is more robust than the modelled side, and the card says so rather than choosing. 33.3% over 2.5 each across twelve matches, four Betis 1–0s and the fewest shots on the board from Deportivo, against a combined 3.11 xG that is fractionally below a divisional 3.24. Both figures come from the same 64-match record.
  • Deportivo’s side of BTTS is more robust than Betis’s, because one is a six-from-six fact and the other contradicts its own model. Deportivo have conceded in all six league matches and kept no clean sheet at any point this season. Betis’s 66.7% clean-sheet rate sits on an xG conceded that rose from 1.32 to 1.81 and 6.50 corners conceded per match.
  • Corners reads better than Team goals because both corner series have a consistent direction and both goal series do not. Both clubs concede more corners than they win, Betis continuously with last season. Both clubs’ goal returns are running four goals away from expectation over six matches, in opposite directions.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Betis’s 2.03 xG starts producing goals. They have 18.50 shots and 6.83 on target per match — both up sharply under an unchanged coach — and 1.33 goals, with four 1–0 wins. If the conversion closes toward the model, the totals reading built on twelve low-scoring matches weakens immediately. What would settle it is two or three more matches of shot-to-goal data; at six, with a 2–5 defeat distorting one end and four 1–0s the other, it cannot be separated.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Villarreal finished 3rd in 2025–26 on 72 points — 22 wins, 6 draws, 10 defeats, 72 scored, 46 conceded — with one of the most venue-dependent records on this board: 15-1-3 at the Cerámica, 7-5-7 away. Levante finished 16th on 42 points — 11 wins, 9 draws, 18 defeats, 47 scored, 61 conceded — the joint-worst goals-against figure on this slate, level with Real Sociedad. The structural headline is one coaching change of very recent date and one of nine months’ standing, and a transfer relationship running directly between the two clubs. Íñigo Pérez was announced at Villarreal on 1 June 2026 on a contract to June 2029, arriving from two and a half years at Rayo Vallecano that included a Conference League final; Luís Castro has been at Levante since December 2025, appointed after Julián Calero was dismissed with the club bottom on nine points. Two players move between these clubs in this fixture’s own team sheets: Etta Eyong joined Levante from Villarreal this summer, and Daniel Requena went the other way on loan from Villarreal to Levante on 17 July. Both clubs are in the bottom six after six and five matches respectively, on five points each.

  • Villarreal — turnover high in departures and almost nil in arrivals; manager changed 1 June 2026. Íñigo Pérez replaced the previous coach on 1 June on a three-year deal, and the window that followed him was a window of subtraction. Out: Ramón Terrats to Getafe, €2.5m on 8 July — to a club on this slate, and the only departure generating meaningful revenue — Alfonso Pedraza to Lazio free on 3 July, Etta Eyong to Levante, today’s opponent, and Daniel Requena on loan to Levante on 17 July. In: Andero Kaares, the only senior arrival named in any source reached, and one brought in to develop rather than to start; Ilias Akhomach returned from a loan at Rayo Vallecano and Rafa Marín from Napoli. The midfield departures of Dani Parejo and Thomas Partey were not replaced. A club that finished third has changed coach, sold or released four midfielders and a full-back, and signed essentially nobody.
  • Levante — turnover very high in both directions and driven by a salary limit; no managerial change in this window, Castro continuing since December 2025. The sporting director Héctor Rodas described the window on 2 September as constrained by the wage ceiling and requiring sales to improve the club’s position. In: Etta Eyong from Villarreal, Unai Vencedor from Athletic Club, Ugo Raghouber from Lille (a club on this slate), Tai Abed from PSV Eindhoven, Mathew Ryan free, Petar Ratkov, and around nine more. Out: Jorge Cabello to Mirandés, Víctor Fernández to Valencia Mestalla, Paco Cortés to Cultural Leonesa, Sergio Lozano to Jagiellonia Białystok, Alfonso Pastor released, and around eleven more. Roughly fifteen in and sixteen out for a club that finished 16th — among the highest body-count turnovers on this slate — with no published fee obtainable for any of it.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Levante are missing three, all with physical problems: Etta Eyong, Hugo Sotelo and Alejandro Primo. Etta Eyong’s absence is the notable one — he joined from Villarreal this summer and would have faced his former club; he is recovering from a muscular injury to the rectus femoris of the right leg. Sotelo suffered a right-knee sprain against Barcelona and is out for several weeks. The rest of the Levante squad is available and the club has named a 25-man list. No injury list for Villarreal was obtainable from any source reached, and none is invented; their probable eleven is published and carries no obvious gap.
  • Sample. Villarreal: seven competitive matches — LaLiga 2–2 at Racing Santander (16 August), 2–2 at Atlético Madrid (23 August, on this slate), 0–1 at Alavés (28 August), 2–3 v Deportivo (5 September, on this slate), 1–2 v Real Betis (14 September, on this slate), 3–1 at Málaga (17 September, on this slate); Champions League 2–3 at Borussia Dortmund (8 September). Fifteenth on 5 points from one win, and they have not kept a clean sheet in any of the six league matches, against a 21.1% rate last season. Sixteenth on 5 points, 7 scored and 9 conceded — and the split is total: all seven goals came in their two home matches, and they have scored nothing at all in three away matches (0–3, 0–0, 0–0).
  • Referee. Mateo Busquets Ferrer of the Balearic committee, confirmed, with Álvaro Moreno on VAR, Valentín Pizarro as VAR assistant, Carlos Álvarez and Rubén Becerril on the lines and Gerard Rius as fourth official. He debuted in Segunda in 2022, was promoted to LaLiga in 2023 and has around 56 matches at this level. Across 19 LaLiga matches in 2025–26: 5.84 cards per match (105 yellows and 6 reds), 24.2 fouls per match, 4.1 fouls per card, and 0.37 penalties per match. That is the heaviest-booking record of any official appointed on this slate — 5.84 against a divisional 4.52 — and the shortest leash on it: 4.1 fouls per card against 5.57, meaning he books roughly one offence in four. This season he has been considerably calmer: 4.00 cards and 5.2 fouls per card across three matches, with no penalty. The two records disagree and both are reported. History with both clubs: two Villarreal matches last season (2–0 v Getafe with seven cards, 2–3 v Sevilla with three) and three Levante matches (1–1 at Getafe with seven cards, 0–0 at Espanyol with five, 1–2 at Betis with three). Across those five he averaged 5.0 cards a match.

B · Style & early-season reality

Villarreal have undergone the largest change in possession share of any club on this board, and it is measurable in five columns at once. Possession has risen from 44.5% to 61.6% — seventeen points, by far the biggest move on this slate — PPDA has fallen from 14.77 to 8.78, deep completions have more than doubled from 7.61 to 16.33, corners won have gone from 4.18 to 7.33 against 4.17 conceded, and xG from 1.81 to 2.55. A side that sat off and counter-attacked under the previous coach now presses high and holds the ball, and Íñigo Pérez’s Rayo Vallecano played exactly that way. The results have gone the opposite direction: one win in six, fifteenth, both home matches lost, and — the single most striking counted fact on this card — no clean sheet in six league matches against 21.1% last season, with xG conceded up from 1.41 to 1.75. They are scoring 1.67 per match from 2.55 expected, so the attack is under-converting as well. Ten goals scored and eleven conceded in six, from a club that scored 72 and conceded 46.

Levante have changed less and split in two. Possession is 40.5% against 42.8%, PPDA 15.38 against 12.78 — slightly less ball and a distinctly looser press — with deep completions down from 5.47 to 4.40 and shots from 12.53 to 11.60. The xG line has barely moved, 1.57 to 1.67 for and 1.75 to 1.80 against, which makes them one of the most continuous sides on this board under a coach now nine months into the job. What is not continuous is the venue split, and it is absolute: seven goals in two home matches (5–2 against Betis, 2–4 against Barcelona) and zero in three away (0–3, 0–0, 0–0). Their 60.0% failed-to-score rate is the fifth-highest on this board, and every one of those blanks came on the road; last season they were at 31.6% overall with a 4-4-11 away record. The discipline column has fallen sharply and without an obvious cause: 1.00 cards and 10.00 fouls per match against 2.34 and 12.26 last season, which on five matches is the kind of drop that usually reverts. Their first-half concession is 1.20 per match against 0.68 last season, among the largest first-half deteriorations on this board.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
