Levante’s record is continuous but bimodal to the point of uselessness: seven goals in two home matches, none in three away. Both clubs sit on five points in the bottom six. LaLiga’s home-win rate is 43.8% against 48.9%. A newly-promoted method at one end and a side that has not scored away from home at the other is a fixture where the result has no stable input on either side.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Villarreal’s six margins are 0, 0, −1, −1, −1, +2 — four matches inside a goal, no win by less than two, and a 2–3 in Europe on top. Levante’s are −3, 0, +3, 0, −2: two draws, two heavy defeats, one heavy win, nothing in between. Levante’s entire positive margin came in one home match against Betis; their away margins are −3, 0, 0. A handicap has to price a gap between a side out-creating everyone and losing, and a side whose distribution is three zeroes and two routs.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — counted and modelled agree on Villarreal’s side, which is doing the work
Villarreal have gone over 2.5 in 83.3% of six league matches against a divisional 56.2%, with 21 goals in their six matches, and their xG line supports it rather than contradicting it: 2.55 for and 1.75 against, a combined 4.30 per match from one club’s perspective. They have not kept a clean sheet in six. Levante’s 60.0% is also above the divisional line and their matches have produced 16 goals in five. The combined xG of 4.22 per match is the second-highest on this board and the division has risen to 3.05 goals per game from 2.69. The counterweight is Levante’s away record — 0–3, 0–0, 0–0, three matches, three blanks — which is exactly the half of the fixture this market needs, and is the reason this is not rated higher.
BTTSSplit — one side has conceded in every match, the other has not scored away all season
The two halves of this market sit on opposite sides of the fixture and each is firm on its own. Villarreal will concede: 0.0% clean sheets in six league matches against 21.1% last season, 1.75 xG conceded, and both home matches lost 2–3 and 1–2. Levante may not score: a 60.0% failed-to-score rate, the fifth-highest on this board, with all three blanks away from home and zero goals in three away matches. Villarreal’s 83.3% BTTS and Levante’s 40.0% are the same fixture seen from each end. Whether both teams score is, almost entirely, whether Levante score — and they have not done so on the road this season, with Etta Eyong, a summer signing from today’s opponent, unavailable.
Team goalsSplit — Villarreal’s volume is documented and under-converting, Levante’s is venue-dependent
Villarreal’s creation is the fourth-highest on this board and rising on every measure: 15.17 shots and 6.50 on target per match against 11.97 and 4.53 last season, 16.33 deep completions against 7.61, 2.55 xG against 1.81, 7.33 corners against 4.18 — five columns up under a new coach — producing 1.67 goals against an expected 2.55. Levante’s is the opposite of documented: 1.40 goals per match, all seven of their goals scored at home, 11.60 shots and a 4.40 deep-completion figure that is the second-lowest on this board. One team-goals line has a volume problem in conversion; the other has a volume problem in volume.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK on first-half goals — the widest combined figure on the board, carried by concession
Villarreal score 1.00 and concede 1.17 before the break; Levante score 0.40 and concede 1.20 — a combined 3.77 first-half goals per match against a divisional 1.42, the widest gap on this slate. Both clubs’ first-half concession has risen sharply from last season (Villarreal 0.47, Levante 0.68), and Villarreal’s first-half scoring is level with last season’s 1.03. The figure is therefore driven by defending rather than attacking on both sides, which is consistent with Villarreal’s zero clean sheets and Levante’s 61 goals conceded last season. The first-half result is not readable — Levante’s 0.40 scored is the second-lowest here and they have trailed at the break in three of five.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — the strictest record on the slate against two sides currently booking below their own rates
The referee and the teams point in opposite directions, and the referee’s own two samples disagree with each other. Busquets Ferrer booked 5.84 per match across 19 matches last season at 4.1 fouls per card — the highest card rate and shortest leash of any official appointed today — and averaged 5.0 cards across the five matches in that record involving these two clubs. This season he is at 4.00 cards and 5.2 fouls per card across three matches. Against that, both clubs are booking below their own 2025–26 rates: Villarreal 2.00 against 2.11 on fewer fouls (11.00 against 11.95), and Levante 1.00 against 2.34 on 10.00 fouls against 12.26 — a halving with no stated mechanism, on five matches. Two low team readings, one very high referee prior, one calmer referee present.
CornersWell-suited — a wide asymmetry with the fullest mechanism on this card
Villarreal win 7.33 corners and concede 4.17; Levante win 3.60 and concede 5.80 — against a divisional 4.82. Villarreal’s +3.17 differential is a complete inversion of last season’s −0.74 (4.18 won, 4.92 conceded), and the mechanism is the most fully documented on this board: possession 44.5%→61.6%, PPDA 14.77→8.78, deep completions 7.61→16.33, shots 11.97→15.17 — four columns moving with it, all consistent with a side that now holds territory instead of ceding it. Levante’s negative differential is continuous with last season’s (4.29 won, 5.92 conceded) at 40.5% possession. Six matches on one side and five on the other, but the change has a named cause in a change of coach and four supporting series.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Villarreal’s 2025–26 record was produced by a fundamentally different team. Seventeen points of possession, six points of PPDA and a doubling of deep completions separate this Villarreal from the one that finished third. Parejo and Partey left and were not replaced; the only named senior arrival is a developmental one. Reading 72 goals and 46 conceded into this fixture reads a different method.
  • The Cerámica advantage that defined Villarreal’s season has inverted. 15-1-3 at home last year; both home league matches lost this year, 2–3 and 1–2. Their remaining four league matches were away, where they took four of their five points.
  • Levante’s record is two records. Seven goals in two home matches, none in three away; a 60.0% failed-to-score rate concentrated entirely on the road. Every rate quoted for them averages a side that scores five against Betis and a side that has not scored away all season.
  • Levante’s transfer ledger has no prices at all and their squad turned over almost completely. Around fifteen in and sixteen out under a salary limit the sporting director described as binding; no fee was obtainable for any of it. Their continuity in the xG columns may therefore be more apparent than real.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and any Levante team-goals line — a side that has not scored in three away matches, missing the forward it signed from today’s opponent. Least sensitive: Corners — a 3.7-corner gap with a coaching change and four supporting series on one side, and 38 matches of continuity on the other.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because the change has a named cause and four independent columns behind it. Villarreal 4.18/4.92 to 7.33/4.17, alongside possession 44.5→61.6, PPDA 14.77→8.78 and deep completions 7.61→16.33 under a coach appointed 1 June. The result rests on a home record that has gone 15-1-3 to 0-0-2.
  • The conceding half of BTTS is more robust than the scoring half, and they sit on opposite sides of the pitch. Villarreal have conceded in all six league matches (0.0% clean sheets against 21.1%) on 1.75 xG against. Levante have scored nothing in three away matches and have a 60.0% blank rate — the fifth-highest on this board — with Etta Eyong unavailable.
  • Totals is more robust than Team goals because it survives the half of the fixture that is unreadable. Villarreal’s 83.3% over 2.5, 2.55 xG and zero clean sheets carry the line on their own; a Levante team-goals figure has to choose between five goals at home and none away.
  • The referee is the least settled input on this card, which is unusual. Busquets Ferrer’s two samples disagree by nearly two cards a match — 5.84 over nineteen last season against 4.00 over three this — and both clubs are currently booking below their own rates. Nineteen matches is the longer record, three is the more recent one, and the card does not choose between them.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Levante score away from home. Three away matches, no goals, 4.40 deep completions per match, 40.5% possession and their summer centre-forward signing injured. If they score, the BTTS and totals readings both resolve the same way; if they do not, Villarreal’s 0.0% clean-sheet rate becomes the only live input on the card. A fourth away match of shot and deep-completion data would settle it; at three, with two goalless draws, it cannot.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Paderborn are promoted, and this is the fourth fixture on the slate involving a promoted club. They came up third in the 2025–26 2. Bundesliga on 62 points from 34 matches — 18 wins, 8 draws, 8 defeats, 59 scored, 45 conceded — level on points with Elversberg and behind champions Schalke on 70, all three of whom are now in this division and two of whom meet each other earlier today at f13. Hoffenheim finished 5th in the Bundesliga on 61 points — 18 wins, 7 draws, 9 defeats, 65 scored, 52 conceded — the third-highest goals-for total of any club on this board, with a 70.6% over-2.5 rate. The structural headline is the relationship between the two clubs rather than anything inside either of them. Paderborn signed eighteen players this summer, more than any other Bundesliga club, for a total of about €8.7m — and three of those arrivals are on loan from Hoffenheim, today’s opponent. Hoffenheim, meanwhile, moved twenty players in and twenty-two out for roughly €65m, kept their head coach, and sold Bazoumana Touré to Newcastle for €47m. One club used the other as a loan pipeline, and they meet on matchday four.

  • Paderborn — turnover extreme in bodies and minimal in money; head coach not established. Eighteen arrivals and eleven departures, for a total outlay of about €8.7m — more players than any other club in the division and among the smallest sums. The most expensive signing cost €3m, eight arrived on free transfers and three on loan. The loans are the structural fact: three of them came from Hoffenheim, plus one from Stuttgart and one from Hamburger SV. Out, the headline: Mika Baur to Celtic for €6.3m, which accounts for practically all of the club’s incoming revenue. This board’s card of 29 August (fx_9_f4) recorded the same shape from the other end: a promotion spine dismantled — the goalkeeper, a centre-back and the centre-forward who scored 15 of their 59 goals all removed and replaced largely with loans, on a net balance of roughly −€450,000. Paderborn’s head coach could not be established from any source reached and is not named here rather than guessed — this was the club triage flagged as provisional on exactly that point.
  • Hoffenheim — turnover very high in both directions and heavily funded; no managerial change. Twenty in and twenty-two out, with roughly €65m spent. Out, the headline: Bazoumana Touré to Newcastle United for €47m — recorded on this board’s card of 17 September (fx_0917_f2) as a €50m sale that funded a midfield rebuild, and reported here at €47m; the two figures are close and both are reported. In, the headline: Nathan De Cat from Anderlecht, €20m. Also out: three loans to Paderborn, described in the German press as minor notes in a squad reduction. The coach is unchanged — this board recorded the same on 5 September (fx_13_f4), calling Hoffenheim “the only fixture on that slate where both clubs kept their coach, their shape and most of their squad” — which makes their 2025–26 record more comparable to today’s side than most on this board, with the caveat that forty-two players moved around it.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club was obtainable from any source reached, and none is invented. The DFB appointments confirm the officials and the kickoff but carry no team news. Hoffenheim played in the Europa League on Thursday, three days ago, losing 2–0 at OFI Crete — a fixture this board carded in full on 17 September (fx_0917_f2) — so they arrive on a European turnaround and Paderborn do not. The three Hoffenheim players on loan at Paderborn are the obvious eligibility question on this card, and no source reached states whether their loan agreements permit them to face the parent club; it is flagged rather than assumed either way.
  • Sample. Paderborn: four competitive matches — Bundesliga 0–0 at Mainz (29 August), 0–1 v Freiburg (5 September), 0–3 at Borussia Dortmund (12 September); DFB-Pokal 4–2 at Phönix Lübeck (23 August). Sixteenth on 1 point, and the league record is the starkest on this board: no goals scored in three matches, one shot on target per match, and 4 shots on target in 34 attempts across the three. They were out-cornered 11–2 at Mainz. Hoffenheim: five competitive matches — Bundesliga 2–3 at FC Köln (29 August), 2–3 v Borussia Dortmund (5 September), 2–1 v VfB Stuttgart (12 September); DFB-Pokal 4–0 at Erzgebirge Aue (22 August); Europa League 0–2 at OFI Crete (17 September). Thirteenth on 3 points, 6 scored and 7 conceded — and all three league matches finished with both teams scoring and more than 2.5 goals, every one of them 2–3 or 2–1.
  • Referee. Daniel Schlager, confirmed by the DFB appointments, with Sven Waschitzki-Günther and Tobias Fritsch on the lines, Felix Bickel as fourth official and Nicolas Winter on VAR. Across 14 Bundesliga matches in 2025–26: 4.14 cards per match (54 yellows, 4 reds), 20.8 fouls per match, 5.0 fouls per card, and 0.29 penalties per match. Against the division that is a strict booking profile on a light foul count: 4.14 cards against 3.82, and 5.0 fouls per card against 5.46 — the shortest leash of any official appointed on this slate bar Soto Grado and Busquets Ferrer, booking roughly one offence in five. His penalty rate of 0.29 is below the divisional 0.36. This season he has taken one Bundesliga match with 2 cards in 23 fouls — 11.5 fouls per card, more than double his own rate — and one match is not a record. History: two Hoffenheim matches last season (1–0 v Leverkusen with three cards, 2–2 at Augsburg with eight cards and a penalty) and one Paderborn match in the 2. Bundesliga (0–2 v Hannover, one card).
  • Not sourced. Paderborn’s head coach could not be established from any source reached and is not named. No 2025–26 xG, PPDA or deep-completion figure exists for Paderborn, because Understat does not cover the 2. Bundesliga; nothing is estimated. No injury or suspension information exists for either club, and the eligibility of the three Hoffenheim loanees to face their parent club is not stated in anything reached. Individual fees within Paderborn’s €8.7m and Hoffenheim’s €65m are not broken out beyond the three named figures, and the names of the three loanees were not obtainable. The Touré sale is reported at €47m here and at €50m on this board’s card of 17 September; both are stated. No source characterises in words how Schlager reads contact in the box.

B · Style & early-season reality

Paderborn’s numbers are the most extreme attacking collapse on this board and they are not a matter of pressing or possession. They hold 50.9% of the ball — down only marginally from 53.2% in the 2. Bundesliga — and press at 12.31 PPDA, which is unremarkable. They take 11.33 shots a match, which is low but not exceptional. The collapse is entirely in what those shots are worth: 1.00 shot on target per match, the lowest figure in this entire dataset by a wide margin, and 0.58 xG, also the lowest here. Four shots on target in three matches, and no goals. Their 5.33 deep completions is middling; their corner concession of 7.33 against 3.33 won has inverted from a near-neutral 5.09 and 4.56 in the second tier. The one thing that has held is the defence, in the counted sense: 1.33 goals conceded against a divisional 3.97 goals per match, including a goalless draw at Mainz. The model disagrees sharply at 1.96 xG conceded, and there is no 2025–26 modelled figure to test any of it against.

Hoffenheim are the opposite in every column and have kept the coach who made them that way. PPDA 8.78 against 8.25 last season — among the most aggressive presses on this board, and essentially unchanged — possession 53.8% against 55.2%, shots up from 14.12 to 17.67, deep completions from 8.68 to 11.67, and xG from 1.86 to 2.35. The defensive model has gone the other way: 2.34 xG conceded against 1.59, so their matches have become open at both ends — and the counted record follows it exactly: all three league matches finished 2–3, 2–3 and 2–1, with both teams scoring in every one and every one clearing 2.5. They have neither kept a clean sheet nor failed to score. Their fouls have risen from 13.94 to 14.00, the third-highest on this board, which their 8.78 PPDA explains, and they score 1.00 first-half goal a match while conceding none. Two clubs at the extremes of the same columns: one that cannot put a shot on target and one whose every match has been a 2–3.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Paderborn’s head coach could not be identified, which is the single largest gap on this board. Triage flagged this club as provisional on exactly that point and the research has not closed it.   • Paderborn have no modelled history and their current model is the lowest here. Understat does not cover the 2. Bundesliga, so 0.58 xG across three matches is the entire modelled record for this club.   • Three of Paderborn’s eighteen signings are on loan from today’s opponent, and their eligibility is not stated. No source reached says whether those loan agreements permit them to face Hoffenheim. On a squad assembled this way, that is not a marginal question.
  • Hoffenheim are three days off a 2–0 European defeat at OFI Crete. That match is outside every league rate on this card, it is their most recent evidence, and it is the one game this season in which they did not score.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, the Asian handicap and any Paderborn goals line — a club with no identified coach, no modelled history, no goals and one shot on target a match. Least sensitive: Discipline — a Hoffenheim foul rate that held across two seasons and two divisions of baseline, an unchanged press to explain it, and the shortest officiating leash on this division.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Discipline is more robust than 1X2 here because every input has a full season behind it and none of them depends on knowing who manages Paderborn. Hoffenheim foul 14.00 against 13.94 last season with PPDA 8.78 against 8.25; Schlager books 4.14 per match at 5.0 fouls per card across fourteen matches and showed eight cards in one of his two previous Hoffenheim games.
  • Totals is more robust than BTTS because it survives Paderborn contributing nothing. Hoffenheim alone have produced 2–3, 2–3 and 2–1, with 2.35 xG for and 2.34 against in a division running at 3.97 goals per match. BTTS requires Paderborn to score, and they have 0.58 xG and one shot on target a match.
  • Hoffenheim’s team-goals line is more robust than anything on the Paderborn side, because five columns agree and the coach did not change. Shots 14.12→17.67, on target 5.18→6.00, deep 8.68→11.67, xG 1.86→2.35, corners 5.85→7.33. Paderborn have no modelled history at all to set against their zero.
  • The first-half result is less robust than the first-half goals, despite Hoffenheim scoring first in all three. They led at the break in every match and lost two of them, which is the clearest possible demonstration that a first-half lead here has carried no information about the second.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether the three Hoffenheim loanees are eligible. Paderborn signed eighteen players for €8.7m, three of them on loan from today’s opponent, and have not scored a goal. Whether those three can play decides how much of the home eleven is even available — and no source reached states it. The confirmed team sheet, published before kickoff, is the only thing that settles it.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. PSG finished 1st in 2025–26 on 76 points — 24 wins, 4 draws, 6 defeats, 74 scored, 29 conceded — with a clean sheet in 52.9% of matches and a failed-to-score rate of 5.9%, both the best figures of any club on this board. Marseille finished 5th on 59 points — 18 wins, 5 draws, 11 defeats, 63 scored, 45 conceded — with a venue split of 11-4-2 at the Vélodrome against 7-1-9 away. The structural headline is that one club has continuity at the top and spent €150m anyway, while the other has had three head coaches since February and sold €112.5m of players against a single confirmed arrival. Luis Enrique’s contract was extended to 2030; Bruno Génésio is Marseille’s third head coach in five months, having replaced Habib Beye. Both clubs sit outside the top eight after four matches — PSG tenth on five points, Marseille fifteenth on three — in a division whose home-win rate has fallen from 46.2% to 38.1% with a 31.0% draw rate. The transfer flow out of Marseille reaches four other clubs on this slate: Gerónimo Rulli to Manchester City, Facundo Medina to Bayer Leverkusen, Hamed Traoré to Bournemouth and Pierre-Emerick Aubameyang to Deportivo.

  • Paris Saint-Germain — turnover high and concentrated entirely in the forward line; no managerial change, Luis Enrique extended to 2030. In, roughly €150.5m across four paid transfers: Maghnes Akliouche from AS Monaco, €50m on 6 August; Ferran Torres from Barcelona, €48.5m; Mika Godts from Ajax, €45m; Lucas Digne from Aston Villa, €7m at left-back; and Alessandro Longoni free from AC Milan in goal. Out: Gonçalo Ramos to AC Milan — a club on this slate — Kang-in Lee to Atlético Madrid, also on this slate and expected to start there today, and Randal Kolo Muani to Juventus, reported between €38m and €70m in total. Three forwards out, three forwards in, one full-back and one goalkeeper — and no centre-back and no defensive midfielder bought or sold. This is the third consecutive slate on which this board has recorded PSG’s window as forward-line turnover with the coach unchanged.
  • Marseille — turnover extreme in departures and, in everything reached, empty in arrivals; third head coach in five months. Bruno Génésio replaced Habib Beye and is the club’s third coach since February. Out, €112.5m confirmed: Mason Greenwood to Fenerbahçe, €39m (14 July); Facundo Medina to Bayer Leverkusen, €23m (11 August); Quinten Timber to Crystal Palace, €20m (2 September); Hamed Traoré to Bournemouth, €7.7m (1 July); Gerónimo Rulli to Manchester City, €3.5m (12 August) — which supplies the fee that City’s own ledger left undisclosed on card f6 — and Leonardo Balerdi on loan to AS Roma (30 August). Pierre-Emerick Aubameyang also left for Deportivo for just over €1m, as recorded on card f15, and does not appear in the Marseille ledger reached here. In: one confirmed arrival — Timothy Weah, whose loan from Juventus became permanent at a reported €14.4m. Beyond him no confirmed signing appears in any source reached; the club’s transfer table lists discussions and rumours only. Correction, now resolved: an earlier version of this card listed Timothy Weah as a €14.4m departure to Juventus on 1 July, from a transfer table that had the direction reversed. Weah moved the other way — Juventus to Marseille, on a loan with an obligation to buy that was triggered, making him a permanent Marseille player. He is therefore an arrival, not a departure, he is in this weekend’s probable eleven at right-back, and the confirmed departure total falls from €126.9m to €112.5m.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported on either side. Marseille are missing two central midfielders, Geoffrey Kondogbia and Tochukwu Nnadi, both injured at the end of August, though Kondogbia is reported as a possible return for this fixture. PSG are reported to have at least four absences, more than that likely, and the one named is Achraf Hakimi, who took a light right-thigh lesion at Brest last Sunday and was receiving treatment through midweek — his availability was still open at the time of writing. The four-plus figure is what the club press reports; the individual names beyond Hakimi were not obtainable and are not invented. Marseille additionally played in Istanbul on Thursday, three days before this fixture, and PSG played in the Champions League eleven days ago.
  • Sample. Marseille: five competitive matches — Ligue 1 4–0 v Strasbourg (21 August), 0–2 at Monaco (30 August), 2–3 v Paris FC (6 September), 0–1 at Rennes (11 September); Europa League 1–4 at Beşiktaş (17 September), three days ago. Fifteenth on 3 points from one win, 6 scored and 6 conceded — and all six goals came in their two home matches. They have scored nothing in two away league matches and conceded three. PSG: seven competitive matches — Ligue 1 2–2 at Rennes (23 August), 2–2 at Lille (28 August), 1–2 v Monaco (4 September), 1–0 at Brest (13 September); Champions League 6–1 v Slovan Bratislava (9 September); UEFA Super Cup 2–1 v Aston Villa (12 August); Trophée des Champions 0–1 v Lens (16 August). Tenth on 5 points from one win, 6 scored and 6 conceded, having lost their only home league match. Three of their four league matches have been away and they have taken five of their five points there.
  • Referee. François Letexier, confirmed, assisted by Cyril Mugnier and Mehdi Rahmouni, with Bastien Dechepy and Wilfried Bien on video. Across 18 Ligue 1 matches in 2025–26: 4.72 cards per match (79 yellows and 6 reds), 24.1 fouls per match, 5.1 fouls per card, and 0.39 penalties per match. Against the division that is strict on both booking axes — 4.72 cards against 3.91, and 5.1 fouls per card against 6.18, so he books roughly one offence in five where the divisional average is one in six — with a penalty rate close to divisional, 0.39 against 0.36. This season he has been dramatically calmer: 2.00 cards per match and 9.8 fouls per card across two matches, with no penalty. Eighteen matches and two matches disagree by more than two cards a game, and both are reported. History, weighted to the home side: three Marseille matches last season (1–0 defeat at Lyon, 1–0 home win over Monaco, 2–2 with Strasbourg including a penalty) and one PSG match (1–0 v Nice). Across those four he averaged 4.5 cards a match.
  • Not sourced. Marseille’s incoming business is established only as far as Timothy Weah, whose loan from Juventus was made permanent. Beyond him no confirmed arrival appears in any source reached, only discussions and rumours, so it is not clear whether the club signed nobody else or whether nothing reached here recorded it. The card states which it cannot tell. PSG’s absence list beyond Hakimi was not obtainable, despite the club press reporting at least four. The individual fees within the Kolo Muani, Ramos and Lee departures are reported only as a €38m–€70m range in aggregate and are not split here. The Timothy Weah direction error above is now corrected: he is a Marseille player, signed permanently from Juventus, not a departure. No source characterises in words how Letexier reads contact in the box. Ligue 1’s 2025–26 record in the ESPN feed holds 305 of 306 matches, as noted in baselines_0920.md, so the divisional lines quoted here are computed over 305.

B · Style & early-season reality

PSG are the same side territorially and a different one in front of goal, and the gap between those two statements is the most interesting thing on this card. Possession 67.5% against 68.8%, deep completions 11.75 against 11.79, shots 18.75 against 17.71 — three series essentially unchanged, with PPDA loosening modestly from 7.96 to 9.35. And yet xG has fallen from 2.23 to 1.57 per match, a drop of two-thirds of a goal, on the same shot count — the same volume producing materially worse chances, which is what selling three centre-forwards and buying three wide forwards looks like in the data. Goals have followed: 1.50 per match against 2.18, with a failed-to-score rate that remains 0.0%. The defensive side has not moved at all: 0.98 xG conceded against 0.99, among the two or three best figures on this board in both seasons — but the counted concession has risen from 0.85 to 1.50, and the clean-sheet rate has collapsed from 52.9% to 25.0%. The corner column is the most extreme number in this entire dataset: 0.75 conceded per match, from four matches reading 0, 0, 0 and 3. They foul 7.25 times a match, the second-lowest figure on this board, and did so last season too at 9.18.

Marseille have lost €127m of players and their numbers have fallen roughly in proportion. xG has dropped from 2.04 to 1.66 and non-penalty xG to 1.47, shots from 15.09 to 13.50, while xG conceded has risen from 1.46 to 1.87 — both ends worse, with possession actually up from 56.9% to 60.8% and PPDA barely moved (10.04 to 10.70). More territory, worse chances at both ends: the signature of a squad that has sold its output and kept its shape. The venue split that defined last season has sharpened into something absolute: six goals in two home matches (4–0 and 2–3) and none at all in two away, plus a 4–1 defeat in Istanbul on Thursday. Their 50.0% failed-to-score rate is entirely an away phenomenon. The discipline column has fallen sharply: 9.75 fouls and 1.25 cards per match against 12.71 and 2.44 last season — a side that was one of the division’s most-booked is now among its quietest, on four matches and with no stated mechanism. Both clubs’ first-half figures have moved the same way and the same amount: scoring roughly halved, concession roughly doubled.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
The reigning champions are tenth and the home side is fifteenth, and both are on identical goal records (6 scored, 6 conceded in four). PSG’s 76-point season was built on a forward line that has been sold and replaced wholesale; Marseille’s 59-point season belongs to two coaches ago. Ligue 1’s home-win rate has fallen to 38.1% from 46.2%, with a 31.0% draw rate — the highest on this slate — so the venue supplies less than usual, and Marseille’s Vélodrome record is the one thing pointing their way. PSG have taken five of five points away from home and lost their only home match. Marseille played in Istanbul on Thursday.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Both sides’ margins are wide and neither is consistent. Marseille’s four are +4, −2, −1, −1, plus a −3 in Istanbul; PSG’s are 0, 0, −1, +1. PSG have not won a league match by more than one goal all season, having won 24 of 34 last season; Marseille’s only positive margin is a 4–0 on the opening day. A handicap needs a gap and the two clubs’ counted records are identical to the goal. The only real separator is the xG conceded — 0.98 against 1.87 — and it has produced the same 1.50 goals conceded for both.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the models and the records disagree, and so do the two clubs
The combined xG is 3.23 per match against a divisional 3.30 — almost exactly average — while the counted records split: PSG have gone over 2.5 in 75% of four matches, Marseille in 50%, against a divisional 57.1%. Both clubs were at 61.8% last season in a division running at 52.8%. The structural facts point in opposite directions: PSG’s attacking xG has fallen two-thirds of a goal while their defensive xG is among the best on this board at 0.98; Marseille concede 1.87 but have scored nothing away and 4–0 and 2–3 at home. Ligue 1 is the only division on this slate scoring exactly at its own 2025–26 rate (2.83 both), so the baseline is stable and unhelpful. Four matches each.
BTTSSplit — one club has scored in every match, the other in half
The two records are far apart and each has a clear component. PSG: 75.0% BTTS, a 0.0% failed-to-score rate continuing last season’s 5.9%, and a clean-sheet rate down from 52.9% to 25.0%. Marseille: 25.0% BTTS and a 50.0% failed-to-score rate — both blanks away from home, where they have scored nothing in two league matches. The scoring half of this market is effectively settled on PSG’s side and entirely open on Marseille’s. The one fact that favours it is that Marseille’s scoring has all come at the Vélodrome — six goals in two home matches — which is where this fixture is played. The divisional BTTS rate has risen to 57.1% from 50.8%.
Team goalsSplit — PSG’s volume is intact and its chance quality is not
PSG present an unusual case: 18.75 shots, 6.50 on target and 11.75 deep completions per match, all at or above last season’s 17.71, 6.68 and 11.79 — and an xG of 1.57 against 2.23. The same territory producing materially worse chances, after Ramos, Kolo Muani and Lee left and Akliouche, Ferran Torres and Godts arrived for €143.5m. Marseille’s volume has fallen with their squad: 13.50 shots against 15.09, 1.66 xG against 2.04, 1.47 non-penalty — and a home-only scoring record. Both team-goals lines are readable in direction; neither is settled, and PSG’s depends on whether a new forward line finds the chance quality the old one had.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Dangerous
Both clubs have moved the same way and the combined figure sits close to the divisional line, which leaves nothing to read. Marseille score 0.25 and concede 0.75 before the break against 0.76 and 0.41 last season; PSG score 0.50 and concede 0.75 against 0.97 and 0.38. That is a halving of scoring and a doubling of concession on both sides simultaneously — four independent series all moving, on four matches each, with no mechanism established for any of them. The combined 2.25 first-half goals per match is only modestly above a divisional 1.19, and PSG trailed at half-time in two of their four league matches having done so rarely last season. Too much movement, too little sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — a strict 18-match record, a calm 2-match one, and two sides booking below their own rates
Every input has a counter-input. Letexier booked 4.72 per match across 18 matches at 5.1 fouls per card — strict against a divisional 3.91 and 6.18 — and averaged 4.5 across the four matches in that record involving these clubs. This season he is at 2.00 cards and 9.8 fouls per card across two. On the team side: PSG foul 7.25 times per match, the second-lowest on this board, continuing last season’s 9.18 and a 1.09 card rate that was the division’s cleanest. Marseille have fallen from 12.71 fouls and 2.44 cards to 9.75 and 1.25 — a halving with no mechanism, on four matches. The division has fallen from 3.91 cards to 3.21. Four low readings and one high referee prior that his own current season contradicts.
CornersWell-suited — the most extreme single figure in this entire dataset
PSG have conceded 0.75 corners per league match: the four matches read 0, 0, 0 and 3. Against a divisional 4.79 that is not a variation, it is a different order of magnitude, and it is the lowest figure of any club, any column, on this board. Last season they conceded 3.18, already the best in the division, so this is an extension of an established pattern rather than a new one. The mechanism is on the page: 67.5% possession, 11.75 deep completions, 7.25 fouls — a side that keeps the ball, defends high and concedes almost no set-piece territory. Their attacking figure is 5.75 won against 6.38 last season, and Marseille’s is 5.00 won and 4.00 conceded, both close to the divisional 4.79. Four matches, but the size of the gap and its continuity with 34 prior matches make it the firmest reading here.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • PSG’s 74 goals were scored by a forward line that has been sold. Ramos, Kolo Muani and Lee out; Akliouche, Ferran Torres and Godts in for €143.5m. The shot count and deep completions are unchanged and the xG has fallen from 2.23 to 1.57 — the clearest evidence on this slate that volume and chance quality are different things.
  • Marseille’s incoming window could not be established at all. €112.5m of confirmed departures against exactly one confirmed arrival in any source reached. Whether the club signed nobody or whether nothing reached here recorded it is not determined, and the probable eleven contains at least one player the same source says left in July.
  • Marseille played in Istanbul on Thursday and lost 4–1. That is three days before this fixture, and it is the most recent evidence about them; it sits outside the league sample every rate on this card is built from.
  • Both clubs’ discipline has halved with no mechanism. Marseille 12.71 fouls to 9.75 and 2.44 cards to 1.25; PSG 9.18 to 7.25. The division has fallen too, 3.91 cards to 3.21, and the appointed official’s own two samples disagree by more than two cards a match. Four separate low readings converging is a stack, not a finding.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and the halves markets — two clubs with identical four-match goal records, a division where home advantage has fallen to 38.1%, and four first-half series all in motion. Least sensitive: Corners — a 0.75-per-match concession continuous with 34 prior matches, and possession, deep-completion and foul data to explain it.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners is more robust than 1X2 here because one figure is an order of magnitude from the baseline and has 38 matches behind it. PSG concede 0.75 per match against a divisional 4.79, having conceded 3.18 last season when they were already the best in the division, with 67.5% possession and 11.75 deep completions to explain it. The result rests on two clubs with identical 6–6 goal records in a division where home wins have fallen to 38.1%.
  • PSG’s side of BTTS is more robust than Marseille’s because one is a two-season fact and the other is a venue artefact. PSG have failed to score in 0.0% of matches this season and 5.9% last. Marseille have failed to score in 50% — both blanks away from home, with all six of their goals at the Vélodrome, which is where this is played.
  • Team goals on PSG is less robust than their shot count suggests, and the xG says why. 18.75 shots and 11.75 deep completions are at or above last season’s figures; 1.57 xG is two-thirds of a goal below. Three centre-forwards were sold and three wide forwards bought for €143.5m.
  • The fouls half of Discipline is more robust than the cards half, and the referee is the whole reason. PSG’s 7.25 fouls continues 9.18 and a divisional-low card rate; Marseille’s 9.75 is a halving with no cause. Letexier books 4.72 per match over 18 matches and 2.00 over two this season — the widest internal disagreement of any official on this slate.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Marseille score at home again. Six goals in two home matches, none in two away, a €127m outflow, no confirmed arrival, two central midfielders injured and a 4–1 defeat in Istanbul three days ago. If the Vélodrome record holds, BTTS and totals both resolve one way; if the away profile travels home, PSG’s 0.98 xG conceded becomes the only live input. What would settle it is a confirmed eleven showing whether Kondogbia returns and who leads the line — published an hour before kickoff.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Milan finished 5th in 2025–26 on 70 points — 20 wins, 10 draws, 8 defeats, 53 scored, 35 conceded — an unusually low-scoring record for a top-five side, with a 44.7% over-2.5 rate against a divisional 45.8% and a better away record (11-5-3) than home (9-5-5). Lecce finished 17th on 38 points — 10 wins, 8 draws, 20 defeats, 28 scored, 50 conceded — the joint-fewest goals scored by any club carded on this slate, level with Parma, and an xG of 0.98 per match to match it. The structural headline is one club at the end of a managerial chain that runs through half this slate, and one club that spent less than anybody in the division. Rúben Amorim was appointed on 16 June 2026 on a three-year contract to 30 June 2029, having left Manchester United — he replaced Massimiliano Allegri, who was dismissed by Milan and is now managing Napoli, who played the opening fixture of this same slate. Eusebio Di Francesco continues at Lecce. Milan then spent about €81m in new-signing costs against €75m of outgoing effect, for a net negative of roughly €6.4m; Lecce spent €7.40m on ten players, the lowest outlay in Serie A.

  • Milan — turnover high and concentrated on the centre-forward and the centre of defence; manager changed 16 June 2026. Centre-forward, the headline: Gonçalo Ramos from Paris Saint-Germain, reported at around €70m on a contract to 30 June 2031 — bought from the club playing at the same time as this fixture on this slate — plus Andrej Kostic from Partizan Belgrade; out — Niclas Füllkrug, loan ended, back to West Ham. Centre-back: Mario Gila from Lazio, around €30m including bonuses, and Sankhoun Diawara from Troyes; out — David Odogu on loan to Toulouse and Zachary Athekame on loan to Lyon. Midfield: Luka Modrić extended, Diego Moreira from Strasbourg; out — Ismaël Bennacer, contract terminated. Out, the headline: Rafael Leão for €38m, producing a capital gain of roughly €27m — the only definitive sale with full effect on the current accounts. Amorim inherited a squad rebuilt at the two positions the spec weights most, and the club’s own accounts record a net negative of €6.38m across the window.
  • Lecce — turnover medium in bodies, lowest in Serie A by money; no managerial change, Di Francesco continuing. €7.40m spent on ten players under contract including loan returns — the smallest investment of any club in the division, and roughly a tenth of what Milan committed to one forward. Goalkeeper: in — Marco Bleve permanently from Carrarese; out — Christian Früchtl permanently to Salzburg with a sell-on percentage and Jasper Samooja on loan to HJK Helsinki with an option and a sell-on. Midfield: in — Ivan Ilić on loan with an option from Torino. Defence: in — Ali Dembélé on loan with an option from Torino. Attack: in — Joël Monteiro permanently from Young Boys, Willem Geubbels permanently from Paris FC — who is injured for this fixture — and Amar Fatah on loan with an option from Torino. Three of the six arrivals came from Torino on loans with options: a recruitment strategy built on borrowing from one club.
  • Suspensions / absences. Neither club has a suspension, and Milan report no injuries at all — the only club on this slate to do so. Lecce are missing three: M. Berisha, Gandelman and Willem Geubbels, the last of whom is one of only two permanent attacking signings the club made all summer. That leaves the visitors’ forward line short of a piece it bought specifically, while the home side is at full strength for the first time this season. On a slate where team news is the dominant uncertainty on most cards, this is the one fixture where the home side’s eleven is effectively settled.
  • Sample. Milan: five competitive matches — Serie A 2–1 at Torino (23 August), 2–0 v Venezia (28 August), 1–1 at Juventus (6 September), 2–2 at Lazio (12 September); Europa League 0–2 v Benfica at San Siro (16 September), four days ago and their only defeat. Sixth on 8 points, unbeaten in the league, 7 scored and 4 conceded — and three of their four league matches have been away, where they have taken 5 of their 8 points. This is only their second home league match of the season. Lecce: five competitive matches — Serie A 2–0 at Venezia (23 August), 0–4 v Roma (31 August), 0–1 at Cagliari (7 September), 3–2 v Monza (13 September); Coppa Italia 0–2 at Palermo (17 August). Twelfth on 6 points, 5 scored and 7 conceded, and they have failed to score in two of four league matches — the same 50% rate they posted across the whole of last season.
  • Referee. Federico La Penna of the Roma 1 section, confirmed in the AIA designations published on 16 September. Across 15 Serie A matches in 2025–26: 3.80 cards per match (52 yellows and 5 reds), 27.6 fouls per match, 7.3 fouls per card, and 0.20 penalties per match. The profile is distinctive: his card rate is dead on the 2025–26 divisional line (3.80 against 3.77) but his matches produce far more fouls than average — 27.6 against 25.3 — so he allows 7.3 fouls per card against a divisional 6.72, which is a lenient conversion on a busy match. His penalty rate of 0.20 is below the divisional 0.28. This season he has taken one match, Parma 1–1 Monza on 6 September, with 4 cards in 32 fouls — 8.0 fouls per card, extending the same pattern at a higher foul count. History: two Lecce matches last season (0–3 at home to Atalanta in April with two cards, and 2–3 at Sassuolo in May with five), and no Milan match in either season.
  • Not sourced. Fees are undisclosed for several of Milan’s smaller arrivals — Kostic, Diawara, Moreira — and the Ramos and Gila figures are reported as approximate (“around €70m”, “€30m including bonuses”) rather than exact; they are quoted as reported and not sharpened. Lecce’s individual fees within the €7.40m aggregate are not broken out in any source reached. The loan fees and option values on the three Torino deals are not published. No source characterises in words how La Penna reads contact in the box; the 0.20 penalties per match is counted from 15 matches. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division.

B · Style & early-season reality

Milan under Amorim have more of the ball, press slightly harder, and have moved all of their scoring into the second half. Possession has risen from 51.9% to 58.4% and PPDA has fallen from 14.62 to 12.51, while shots (13.50 against 13.24) and xG (1.80 against 1.71) are close to unchanged. That is a side controlling matches more without creating meaningfully more from it — and the corner column agrees, falling from 4.26 won to 3.00, among the five lowest corners-won figures on this slate, on 58.4% of the ball. The striking counted fact is the timing: Milan have scored 0.00 first-half goals in four league matches, against 0.53 last season. All seven of their goals have arrived after the interval, and they trailed 0–2 at half-time at Lazio before drawing 2–2. They concede 0.50 before the break. Defensively the model has loosened (1.21 to 1.43 xG conceded) while the counted rate has too (0.92 to 1.00), and the clean-sheet rate has fallen from 39.5% to 25.0%. Their most recent match is the 0–2 home defeat to Benfica in the Europa League four days ago, which sits outside every league rate quoted here.

Lecce are scoring more than last season and defending far worse, and the second half of that is the more extreme. xG conceded has risen from 1.71 to 3.08 per match — nearly doubling, and the second-worst defensive figure on this entire board — while PPDA has loosened from 11.93 to 14.09 and possession has stayed at 40.4% against 41.6%. They are pressing less and being opened up far more often, on unchanged territory. At the other end, xG has risen from 0.98 to 1.51 and goals from 0.74 to 1.25 per match, which is real improvement from a very low base — but the shot count has not moved (9.00 against 9.82, the second-lowest here) and deep completions are 3.25 against 3.42, the fourth-lowest on this board. The improvement is in chance quality rather than volume, from a club that spent €7.40m and borrowed three players from Torino. The pattern is bimodal: two comfortable results (2–0 at Venezia, 3–2 v Monza) and two in which they were taken apart (0–4 v Roma, 0–1 at Cagliari with 20 shots against). Their 50% failed-to-score rate has survived the improvement intact — identical to last season’s across a full campaign.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Milan’s 70-point season belongs to Allegri, who is now at Napoli; Amorim was appointed on 16 June and has four league matches. The counted separation is 8 points against 6, and Milan have played three of four away while this is only their second home league match. Serie A’s home-win rate is 40.0% against 38.9%. Lecce finished 17th and have already won at Venezia and beaten Monza. Milan are unbeaten in the league and lost 0–2 at home to Benfica four days ago, which is the most recent evidence and is not in any league rate on this card.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Milan’s four league margins are +1, +2, 0, 0 — two narrow wins and two draws, from a side that drew 10 of 38 last season. Lecce’s are +2, −4, −1, +1: bimodal, with one rout inside four matches. The xG gap is wide — Milan 1.80 for and 1.43 against, Lecce 1.51 and 3.08 — but the counted goal difference over four matches is +3 against −2, which is far narrower than the models imply. A handicap has to price a gap the two sources disagree about, on four matches, with the home side’s coach four matches into the job.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the counted records are low, the models are high, and the division has moved
Three sources point three ways. Counted, these are two of the lowest-scoring records on the board: 50.0% over 2.5 each this season, and 44.7% and 36.8% last season against a divisional 45.8%. Lecce scored 28 goals in 38 matches; Milan 53. Modelled, the combined xG is 4.59 per match — Lecce’s 3.08 conceded is the second-worst on this board — against a divisional 3.42. And the division has moved hard: 2.96 goals per match against 2.43, 55.6% over 2.5 against 45.8%, 60.0% BTTS against 45.3%. Two low counted records, one very high combined model, and a divisional baseline that has risen 22%. Four matches each cannot arbitrate, and the card does not pretend otherwise.
BTTSSplit — Milan have scored in every match, Lecce in half
The two halves sit on opposite sides. Milan: 75.0% BTTS, a 0.0% failed-to-score rate this season against 18.4% last, and they have conceded in three of four. Lecce: 25.0% BTTS, and a 50.0% failed-to-score rate that is identical to last season’s across a full 38 matches — the most durable single rate on this card. Whether both teams score is, close to entirely, whether Lecce score: they take 9.00 shots per match, complete 3.25 passes deep — both among the four or five lowest on this board — and are missing Geubbels, one of two permanent attacking signings. Against that, Milan’s 1.43 xG conceded and Lecce’s improvement from 0.98 to 1.51 xG both point the other way.
Team goalsSplit — Milan’s is readable, Lecce’s rests on quality without volume
Milan’s attacking series is stable and modest: 13.50 shots, 4.25 on target, 6.75 deep completions and 1.80 xG, all within noise of last season’s 13.24, 4.37, 7.71 and 1.71, on seven points more possession — with Gonçalo Ramos at roughly €70m replacing Füllkrug and Leão sold for €38m. Lecce’s is the more unusual case: xG up from 0.98 to 1.51 on a shot count that has not moved (9.82 to 9.00) and a deep-completion figure of 3.25, the fourth-lowest on this board. Better chances, no more of them, from a €7.40m window — and their principal summer forward is injured. One line has volume without much movement; the other has movement without volume.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited on the first half — the single most specific counted fact on this card
Milan have not scored a first-half goal in any of their four league matches. All seven of their goals have come after the break, and they trailed 0–2 at half-time at Lazio before drawing 2–2. Last season they scored 0.53 before the interval, itself low. They concede 0.50. Lecce are at 0.75 scored and 1.25 conceded, so the combined first-half figure of 2.50 per match sits above a divisional 1.16 — but every goal in it comes from one side. This is the rare case on this slate where the result half of this family is as informative as the goals half: a side that has not led at half-time in four attempts, against a side that has conceded 1.25 first-half goals per match. Four matches is the limit, and a zero has no variance to measure — one goal makes it 0.25.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — a high-foul, lenient-conversion official meeting two rising card rates
The inputs here are more coherent than on most cards. La Penna’s matches produce 27.6 fouls against a divisional 25.3 and he books at 3.80, dead on the divisional 3.77 — so 7.3 fouls per card against 6.72, a lenient conversion on a busy game, extended this season to 8.0 across 32 fouls in one match. Both clubs are booking above their own 2025–26 rates: Milan 2.00 against 1.66, Lecce 2.25 against 1.87, with Lecce at 12.00 fouls per match against a divisional 11.21 and Milan at 10.50. The division has fallen sharply, 3.22 cards against 3.77, which cuts against the club figures. In La Penna’s two previous Lecce matches the cards ran 2 and 5. A high-foul referee with a long leash, two sides booking above their own rate, and a division booking below its own.
CornersDangerous
Both clubs are below the divisional line at both ends and neither movement has a stated cause. Milan win 3.00 corners per match — among the five lowest on this slate — and concede 3.50, against 4.26 and 4.29 last season and a divisional 4.78. Lecce win 3.50 and concede 4.75, against 4.34 and 5.42. Milan’s fall is the odd one: their possession has risen seven points, from 51.9% to 58.4%, while their corners have fallen by more than a quarter, and their deep completions fell from 7.71 to 6.75. More ball, less final-third penetration, fewer corners — a coherent picture but one with four matches behind it and no prior Amorim season at this club to test it against. Four low numbers, no mechanism, no sample.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Milan’s 2025–26 record belongs to Allegri, who now manages Napoli — the club whose fixture opened this slate. Amorim arrived on 16 June, Leão was sold for €38m, Bennacer’s contract was terminated, Füllkrug’s loan ended, and Gonçalo Ramos arrived from PSG for roughly €70m. The 53 goals and 35 conceded describe a different attack and a different centre-back pairing.
  • Milan’s zero first-half goals is the most specific fact on this card and the least statistically robust. Four matches, seven goals, all after the break. A zero has no variance to estimate and reverts on a single goal; it is reported because it is counted, not because four matches establish a tendency.
  • Three of Milan’s four league matches have been away. They took five of their eight points on the road, and their only home league match was a 2–0 against Venezia. Last season their away record (11-5-3) was better than their home one (9-5-5), so venue does not resolve in the usual direction here either.
  • Lecce’s defensive model has nearly doubled in severity and their results have not followed yet. 3.08 xG conceded against 1.71 last season, on 1.75 goals conceded. Two of their four matches were comfortable and two were routs. A €7.40m window and three loans from a single club is thin cover for a gap that size.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, Asian handicap and Corners — the first two because the counted and modelled gaps disagree by a wide margin, the third because four sub-baseline readings have no mechanism. Least sensitive: the first-half family and the Lecce half of BTTS — a 50% failed-to-score rate that has now held across 42 matches, and a first-half scoring record that is specific, counted and repeated across four matches.

E · Short market-type guide

  • The first-half family is more robust than 1X2 here because it is the one thing four matches can establish and the result is not. Milan 0.00 first-half goals in four, all seven goals after the break, 0–2 down at half-time at Lazio; Lecce conceding 1.25 before the interval. The full-time result needs a coach with four matches, a squad missing Leão and a second home league match of the season.
  • Lecce’s side of BTTS is more robust than Milan’s because one rate has 42 matches behind it and the other has four. Lecce have failed to score in 50.0% of matches this season and 50.0% last, on 9.00 shots and 3.25 deep completions — the two lowest volume figures on this board — with Geubbels injured. Milan’s 0.0% blank rate is four matches long.
  • Discipline reads better than Corners because the referee supplies a mechanism and the corner count does not. La Penna’s matches run 27.6 fouls against a divisional 25.3 at 7.3 fouls per card, reproduced at 32 fouls and 8.0 this season; Lecce foul 12.00 and both clubs book above their own rates. Milan’s corners fell from 4.26 to 3.00 while their possession rose seven points, with nothing established to explain it.
  • Totals is the family where this card most explicitly refuses to choose, and the reason is worth stating. The counted records say low (36.8% and 44.7% last season, 50% each now), the combined xG says 4.59 per match against a divisional 3.42, and the division itself has gone from 2.43 goals per match to 2.96. Three sourced series, three directions, four matches each.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Milan score before half-time. It is the only zero in any counted column on this board, it sits on four matches, and it carries the first-half reading, part of the BTTS reading and the shape of the totals argument at once. What would settle it is a fifth and sixth league match of goal-timing data — and Milan’s most recent match, the 0–2 against Benfica four days ago, was also goalless at the interval.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted. Valencia finished 9th in 2025–26 on 49 points — 13 wins, 10 draws, 15 defeats, 46 scored, 55 conceded — with a 42.1% over-2.5 rate against a divisional 50.0%. Real Sociedad finished 10th on 46 points — 11 wins, 13 draws, 14 defeats, 59 scored, 61 conceded — and kept a clean sheet in 7.9% of matches, three in thirty-eight, the lowest rate of any club on this board, with a 78.9% BTTS rate that was the highest in the division. The structural headline is two managerial situations that are both unresolved in different ways. Valencia dismissed Carlos Corberán on Sunday 13 September 2026 — the first head coach removed in LaLiga this season — together with the football CEO Ron Gourlay and his working group, and Óscar Sánchez continues as interim coach for this match. Real Sociedad sacked Sergio Francisco on 14 December 2025 with the club 15th, and the replacement appointed then remains in post — a change nine months old rather than three months old. Neither club spent: Valencia’s largest signing cost €5m and Real Sociedad’s €1.5m.

  • Valencia — turnover low and net-negative; head coach dismissed 13 September 2026, interim in charge. Carlos Corberán was dismissed on Sunday 13 September with the club bottom on one point from five, alongside the football CEO and his staff — the first LaLiga dismissal of the season, recorded on this board’s card of 15 September (fx_0915_f3). Óscar Sánchez takes the team again today. In: Arnau Martínez, €5m — the largest signing of the window — and Ryunosuke Sato. Out: Cenk Özkacar. This board’s card of 11 September (fx_16_f4) characterised the same window as net-negative, with the biggest signing at €5m and no managerial change at the time; the managerial change came two days later. A club that scored 46 goals has spent €5m, sacked its coach and its football executive inside one week, and has an interim picking the team.
  • Real Sociedad — turnover high in bodies, negligible in money; no change in this window, coach in post since December 2025. Sergio Francisco was sacked on 14 December 2025 with the club 15th and the replacement has been in post nine months — carried from this board’s card of 17 September (fx_0917_f8), which also recorded the window as high turnover in bodies with negligible investment and the manager retained.   • Suspensions / absences. That list runs straight through the spine: Diakhaby, Copete and Tárrega are centre-backs, Foulquier a full-back, and Sadiq and Diego López attacking players — so an interim coach is picking a back line and a forward line largely from what is left. No suspension is reported. No injury or suspension list for Real Sociedad was obtainable from any source reached and none is invented, though they played on Wednesday and this is their third match in eight days.

Across 20 LaLiga matches in 2025–26: 4.15 cards per match (75 yellows and 8 reds), 26.6 fouls per match, 6.4 fouls per card, and 0.15 penalties per match. B · Style & early-season reality

Valencia have stopped creating anything at all, and the collapse is uniform across every column. Shots have fallen from 11.82 to 8.67 per match, shots on target from 3.24 to 2.33, deep completions from 5.03 to 2.83 — the second-lowest figure on this board, behind Málaga’s 1.83 — and xG from 1.42 to 0.69, less than half. Possession is down only marginally (47.6% to 46.5%) but PPDA has loosened dramatically, from 12.55 to 16.11, the third-most passive figure here: they neither press nor build. The result is two goals in six matches, a 66.7% failed-to-score rate and one first-half goal all season. The home record is the sharpest statement of it: three matches at Mestalla, 0–0, 0–1 and 0–5, no goal scored. The defensive side is the one place the counted record flatters them — 33.3% clean sheets, higher than last season’s 23.7% — and the model disagrees at 1.72 xG conceded. Both of their goals and all four of their points have come away from home, and the one win followed the coach’s dismissal.

Real Sociedad have kept their shape and lost their floor. Possession 49.6% against 51.3%, shots 12.17 against 12.34, and a PPDA that has loosened from 9.08 to 12.80 — a 40% drop in pressing intensity, among the largest proportional loosenings on this board. The attacking model has fallen with it, 1.17 xG against 1.57, while the defensive model has worsened to 1.88 against 1.59. What has not changed is the thing that defines this club: they kept clean sheets in 7.9% of thirty-eight matches last season — three all year — and in 16.7% of six this season. They concede. The one series moving in their favour is corners: 7.67 won against 3.83 conceded, up from 5.61 and 4.50, a +3.83 differential, among the six widest on this board, built on a lot of territory and 5.00 shots on target per match. Their discipline has eased from the division’s highest foul count — 14.24 per match last season, against a divisional 12.58 — to 12.50, which is what a 40% looser press produces. They have lost three of their last four and played on Wednesday.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Valencia are on an interim coach appointed a week ago, are missing seven players including three centre-backs, and have scored two goals in six matches. Real Sociedad have a coach nine months in post, have lost three of their last four in all competitions, and played on Wednesday. Nineteenth against thirteenth, four points against seven. The one asymmetry that would normally decide this — home advantage — runs the wrong way: Valencia have scored no goals at all in three matches at Mestalla and both their goals and all four of their points came away. LaLiga’s home-win rate is 43.8% against 48.9%. Last season these two produced 1–1 and 3–4.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Valencia’s six margins are 0, −1, −2, −5, −1, +1 and Real Sociedad’s are −1, −3, +1, 0, +1, −3 — both distributions contain a rout and several one-goal results, with nothing in between. Between them, four of twelve league matches were decided by three or more and six by one or none. The models are closer than the table: Valencia 0.69 xG for and 1.72 against, Real Sociedad 1.17 and 1.88 — about half a goal apart. A handicap has to price that with an interim coach on one side, seven absentees, and a −5 inside the sample.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — one of the lowest scoring records on the board against two poor defensive models
The attacking and defensive halves of this fixture point in opposite directions and both are six matches deep. Attacking: Valencia have scored 0.33 goals per match, the third-lowest rate on this board, on 0.69 xG, 8.67 shots and 2.83 deep completions — the second-lowest deep-completion figure here. Their 42.1% over-2.5 rate last season was below a divisional 50.0%. Defensive: both clubs concede above 1.70 xG, Real Sociedad kept three clean sheets in thirty-eight matches last season, and the division has risen from 2.69 goals a game to 3.05 with 56.2% over 2.5. The counted rates split 33.3% and 66.7%. Twelve league matches between them have produced 29 goals, which is close to the divisional average — and 18 of those 29 came in Real Sociedad’s matches.
BTTSSplit — the most durable prior on the board against the most extreme current record
The two sides carry opposite and unusually strong evidence. Real Sociedad: 78.9% BTTS across thirty-eight matches last season, the highest in LaLiga, with a 7.9% clean-sheet rate — three in thirty-eight — and a 13.2% failed-to-score rate. That is a full season, not a run. Valencia: 16.7% BTTS this season, a 66.7% failed-to-score rate, two goals in six matches and none at home in three. Last season Valencia were at 55.3%, close to the divisional line. The market resolves almost entirely on whether Valencia score — and they have managed it twice in six, both times away, with seven players out including two of the forwards. Against that, Real Sociedad have kept one clean sheet in six and three in the last forty-four.
Team goalsDangerous
Neither attacking line is readable and one is close to a zero. Valencia: 0.33 goals per match from 0.69 xG on 8.67 shots and 2.33 on target, with 2.83 deep completions — five of the lowest figures on this board — an interim coach, and Sadiq and Diego López among seven absentees. No goal at home all season. Real Sociedad: 1.00 goal per match from 1.17 xG, down from 1.55 and 1.57, with their three most senior departures unreplaced beyond a €1.5m signing and a press 40% looser. Both clubs’ attacking models have fallen materially from last season and neither has a volume trend pointing up. A team-goals line on either side is a line on a side that has stopped creating.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — Valencia’s first-half record is the more durable, and it is nearly a zero
Valencia have scored one first-half goal in six league matches — 0.17 per match, against 0.32 last season, itself the lowest in this dataset for a full campaign — and concede 0.67. Real Sociedad score 0.67 and concede 0.33. The combined 1.84 first-half goals per match sits above a divisional 1.42, and three-quarters of it belongs to the away side. Valencia’s figure is the unusual one because it holds across two seasons: 0.32 over thirty-eight matches and 0.17 over six, both far below any other club on this board. Real Sociedad’s first-half concession has improved from 0.74 to 0.33 on six matches, which is the sort of movement that reverts. The first-half result inherits the 1X2 problems on a shorter sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — the division’s highest foul count easing, into a referee whose two samples contradict each other
The team evidence is decent and the referee evidence is at war with itself. Real Sociedad were the most-fouling side in LaLiga last season at 14.24 per match against a divisional 12.58, with 2.42 cards per team — and have eased to 12.50 and 2.33 as their press loosened from 9.08 to 12.80 PPDA. Valencia are at 10.83 fouls and 2.00 cards, both below divisional. Munuera Montero booked 4.15 per match across twenty matches with 8 reds, on the highest foul count of any official appointed today (26.6), at a lenient 6.4 fouls per card — and has booked 3.00 per match across three this season. The penalty figure is where the contradiction is total: 0.15 per match across twenty matches last season, against 1.00 per match across three this season, all three coming in a single 7–2 match. Both are counted and neither is preferred.
CornersOK — one clear, recent inversion against one flat series
Real Sociedad win 7.67 corners per match and concede 3.83, a +3.84 differential against 5.61 and 4.50 last season — the third-widest on this board. The mechanism is only partly present: their shots are flat at 12.17 against 12.34 and possession is down, but their shots on target have risen from 4.61 to 5.00 and they have taken 7, 11, 7 and 9 corners in four of their six matches. Valencia are close to flat and close to the line: 4.00 won and 3.50 conceded against 5.16 and 4.79 last season, with a divisional 4.82 — both figures falling, which is consistent with 8.67 shots and 2.83 deep completions. One side has a real and wide differential with a partial explanation; the other has no signal beyond a general decline. Six matches each, which is more than most fixtures on this board.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Valencia are picking a team under an interim coach, a week after the club dismissed its head coach and its football CEO together. Óscar Sánchez has one match in charge, a 1–0 win at Alavés. Whether he continues beyond today, and whether a permanent appointment is imminent, could not be established.
  • Seven Valencia players are out and an eighth is doubtful — the longest absence list on this board. Three of them are centre-backs (Diakhaby, Copete, Tárrega), one a full-back (Foulquier) and two attacking players (Sadiq, Diego López). Any reading of either end of Valencia’s game is a reading of a side missing most of one unit and part of another.
  • Real Sociedad’s team news could not be sourced and they are playing their third match in eight days. Betis away, Atlético at home, Bournemouth at home, now Mestalla — and they have lost three of the last four.
  • Both clubs’ attacking models have fallen materially and neither replaced its departures. Valencia’s xG has halved (1.42 to 0.69) on a €5m window; Real Sociedad’s has fallen a quarter (1.57 to 1.17) having sold Brais Méndez, Carlos Fernández and Karrikaburu and signed one player for €1.5m.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, the Asian handicap and both team-goals lines — an interim coach with seven absentees against a side on its third match in eight days, with a −5 and a −3 inside the twelve-match sample. Least sensitive: the Real Sociedad half of BTTS — three clean sheets in thirty-eight matches last season and one in six this season is as durable a single fact as this board holds.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Real Sociedad’s side of BTTS is more robust than anything else on this card because it rests on a full season, not a start. 78.9% BTTS across thirty-eight matches, the highest in LaLiga; a 7.9% clean-sheet rate, three in thirty-eight; 16.7% and one in six this season. The result, by contrast, rests on an interim coach and seven absentees.
  • Valencia’s first-half scoring is more robust than their full-match scoring because it holds across two seasons. 0.32 first-half goals per match across thirty-eight and 0.17 across six, both far below any other club here. Their full-match figure of 0.33 has six matches behind it and an absence list attached.
  • Corners reads better on the Real Sociedad side than the Valencia side, and only there. 5.61/4.50 to 7.67/3.83, with shots on target up from 4.61 to 5.00 and four matches of seven or more corners. Valencia’s 4.00 and 3.50 are a general decline with no differential to read.
  • The card line in Discipline is more robust than the penalty line, and this is the one fixture on the slate where that is true. Real Sociedad’s 14.24→12.50 fouls has PPDA 9.08→12.80 behind it and Munuera Montero has a twenty-match booking record. His penalty rate reads 0.15 per match over twenty matches and 1.00 over three, and all three of this season’s came in one 7–2 game.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is whether Valencia score at Mestalla. Three home matches, no goals, 0.69 xG, 2.83 deep completions, an interim coach and seven players out. If they score, the BTTS and totals readings both resolve one way; if the home record holds, Real Sociedad’s 78.9% prior becomes irrelevant and this becomes a one-sided card. The confirmed eleven, published an hour before kickoff, is the only thing that settles it — and on this card it decides more than on any other.

Across the card

  • Best structurally supported today — Totals, rated well-suited on twelve of the twenty cards and Dangerous on none (f2, f3, f4, f7, f8, f9, f10, f11, f13, f15, f16, f17). In every case a goal rate survived something that should have broken it, and the twelve split evenly between high and low readings. High: Leverkusen and Leipzig have both gone over 2.5 in 100% of three with a combined 5.24 xG (f9); Schalke and Elversberg carry the two worst defensive xG figures on the board, 3.17 and 2.85 (f13); Auxerre and Brest are at 75% each with 23 goals in eight matches (f3). Low: Parma went over in 28.9% of thirty-eight matches and 25% of four, with the lowest combined xG on the board at 1.85 (f8); Lille’s 38.2% last season was the lowest in Ligue 1 (f11); Getafe’s 26.3% and 16.7% came under one unchanged coach while their division rose (f2).
  • Least supported — 1X2, rated Dangerous on all twenty cards without one exception, and the Asian handicap on eighteen. Twenty-three of the forty clubs have a coach appointed since June; the rest are meeting one. The purest case is f4 Bournemouth–Liverpool, where the coaching change is the fixture — Iraola returns to the club Liverpool took him from — and the two sides have drawn 21 of their last 42 league matches. The two partial exceptions on the handicap are f6, where Manchester City’s margins have narrowed to +1, +3, +1, +1 alongside falling shots and first-half goals, and f14, where Juventus and Atalanta are one point apart and about two and a half expected goals apart.
  • Where a family is genuinely settled — Corners on six cards and Discipline on three. On corners: Juventus win 9.75 a match and concede 2.25, a +7.50 differential and the largest on the board (f14); PSG have conceded 0.75 corners per league match — 0, 0, 0 and 3 — against a divisional 4.79 (f18); Lille win 1.75, the lowest figure anywhere here, against a Nice side winning 6.50 (f11); and Villarreal’s +3.17 has possession up seventeen points behind it (f16). On discipline: Getafe foul more than any club here under an unchanged coach, in front of an official booking 5.00 a game (f2); Deportivo foul at 13.50 with two reds in six, in front of César Soto Grado, who showed 8 reds in 19 matches (f15); and Hoffenheim’s 14.00 fouls held across two seasons, meeting the shortest leash in the Bundesliga (f17).
  • The most interesting disagreements, where the cards refuse to choose: f6 Manchester City–Sunderland, eleven goals in eight league matches against a combined 3.83 xG; f15 Deportivo–Betis, where Deportivo have 9 goals from 6.5 expected and Betis 8 from 12.2, in opposite directions inside one fixture; f14 Juventus–Atalanta, one point apart on the table and the widest modelled gap on the board; and f3 Auxerre–Brest, where Brest out-shoot everyone and have five points.
  • Where the evidence is thinnest, and which part of the board to trust least. In order: f17 Paderborn–Hoffenheim, where the home club’s head coach could not be identified at all, it has no xG history, it has not scored a goal, and three of its eighteen signings are on loan from today’s opponent with eligibility unstated; f13 Schalke–Elversberg, the only card where neither club has any xG history and no team news could be found for either; f5 Leeds–Crystal Palace, where the referee is making his Premier League debut and no officiating record exists; and f11 Nice–Lille and f3 Auxerre–Brest, where one club’s entire transfer window is unsourced and no team news exists for either side. The single thinnest reading anywhere is Fiorentina’s four-match league sample on f1, which is really two samples — three defeats under Grosso, one win under Vanoli, whose record at the club is one match long.
  • The one fixture that behaves normally: f19 Milan–Lecce is the only card on this board where the home side reports neither an injury nor a suspension and both team sheets are effectively settled. Everywhere else the decisive fact is a name on a lineup published an hour before kickoff — Julián Álvarez on f10, Carlos Baleba on f12, Buongiorno and McTominay on f1, Bernabé on f8, and on f20 an interim coach naming a back line with three centre-backs unavailable.

Scope. This page rates market families only. It contains no selections, no sides, no recommended lines and no confidence labels — by design. Where evidence is thin it says so rather than filling the gap.
